# HENRY
## CORNELIUS AGRIPPA,
->Of the Councils and Judicial Archives of His<-
->Sacred Imperial Majesty, concerning<-
->Occult Philosophy<-
->Book One,<-

->Previously issued at Antwerp with a six-year privilege from the Emperor: the remaining two books, however, whose<-
->Index is attached to this one, will be provided as<-
->soon as the Author’s<-
->occupations allow.<-

<note>The "Imperial Majesty" refers to Holy Roman Emperor Charles V, whom Agrippa served as historiographer and advisor. The "privilege" mentioned was an early form of copyright protection.</note>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page begins the preface 'To the Reader' of the first book. It establishes the author's defense of the term 'Magic' against anticipated religious and intellectual criticism.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial 'N' at the start of the text. It features a figure (possibly a putto or classical figure) seated within decorative foliage and strapwork, holding a branch or staff.</image-desc>

I DO NOT DOUBT that the title of our book, *On Occult Philosophy* <note>original: "de occulta Philosophia"</note>, or *On Magic*, will by its rarity entice a great many to read it. Among these, some of distorted opinion and weak mind—and many even malicious and ungrateful toward our talent—will approach. These men, in their rash ignorance, taking the name of Magic in the worst sense, will cry out almost as soon as they see the title that we are teaching forbidden arts, sowing the seeds of heresy, providing a stumbling block to pious ears and a scandal to excellent minds. They will claim I am a sorcerer <term>maleficum: a practitioner of harmful or "black" magic</term>, that I am superstitious, that I am possessed by demons, because I am a Magician. 

If I were to answer them that among learned men, the word "Magician" <note>original: "Magus"</note> does not signify a sorcerer, nor one who is superstitious or possessed, but rather a wise man, a priest, and a prophet; that the Sibyls <note>Prophetic women of Greek and Roman antiquity who were believed to possess divine knowledge</note> were Magicians, and therefore prophesied most clearly concerning Christ; and furthermore, that the Magi, through the wonderful secrets of the world, recognized that Christ, the author of the world, was born, and were the very first of all to come and worship him; and that the very name of Magic is accepted by philosophers, praised by theologians, and not even unwelcome to the Gospel itself—I believe those censors of such stubborn arrogance would sooner forbid themselves the Sibyls, the holy Magi, and even the Gospel itself, before they would receive the name of Magic into their favor. So much do they consult their own consciences...

<summary>In this preface, the author defends his work on 'Occult Philosophy' against charges of heresy and sorcery, arguing that true Magic is a pursuit of wisdom and divine prophecy aligned with Christian history.</summary>
<keywords>Occult Philosophy, Magic, Magi, Sibyls, Christ, Theology, defense of magic, sorcery</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>The previous page likely introduced the author's intent for this work on occult philosophy; the catchword "corum" suggests the continuation of a discussion regarding the Platonists or similar schools of thought.</meta>

->TO THE READER<-

...so that neither Apollo <note>The Greek and Roman god of music, healing, and prophecy.</note>, nor all the Muses, nor even an angel from heaven could vindicate me from their curses. To these people, I now give counsel that they should neither read our writings, nor understand them, nor remember them: for they are harmful, they are poisonous; the very mouth of Acheron <note>original: "Acherontis ostium"; Acheron was the river of woe in the Greek underworld.</note> is in this book; it speaks stones—let them beware lest it knock out their brains. 

But you, who come to read with an open mind, if you apply as much discretion of wisdom as bees do in gathering honey, then read securely: for I think you will receive no small amount of utility, and a great deal of pleasure. If you find anything that does not please you, pass it by and do not use it: for I do not approve of those things for you, but merely relate them. Yet do not reject the rest on that account; for it happens to those inspecting the volumes of physicians that they read of poisons at the same time as antidotes and medicines. 

I confess, furthermore, that Magic <note>original: "Magia"</note> itself teaches many superfluous things, and curious wonders intended for outward show; leave these aside as vain, yet do not remain ignorant of their causes. But those things which can be done for the utility of men—to avert evil events, to destroy sorceries <note>original: "maleficia"; specifically harmful magic or curses.</note>, to cure diseases, to drive out apparitions <note>original: "phantasmata"</note>, and to preserve the soundness of life, honor, and fortune, without offense to God or injury to religion—who would not judge those things to be as useful as they are necessary? 

But because I have warned you that I have written many things by way of relating them rather than affirming them (for it seemed necessary to do so so that we might omit as little as possible), many things moreover from the Platonists <note>original: "Platonicorum"</note>...

<summary>The author issues a stern warning to hostile readers while encouraging the fair-minded to discern between useful medicinal knowledge and vain or dangerous magic. He justifies the inclusion of controversial material by stating he is reporting these ideas as a scholar rather than personally endorsing every practice.</summary>
<keywords>preface, Apollo, Muses, Acheron, Magic, sorcery, medicine, Platonists, ethics of magic</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface to the reader, justifying the publication of a work written in his youth.</meta>

->TO THE READER<-

We have followed the opinions of the <term>Gentile philosophers</term><note>Non-Christian thinkers, primarily from Ancient Greece and Rome</note> and others where they suggested arguments suited to our writing style. Therefore, if I have erred anywhere, or if anything has been said too freely, forgive my youth; for I composed this work when I was less than a young man. Thus I may excuse myself and say: *When I was a child, I spoke as a child, I understood as a child; but when I became a man, I put away childish things* <note>original: "dum eram paruulus, loquebar ut paruulus... euacuaui quæ erant paruuli" — a reference to 1 Corinthians 13:11</note>. Indeed, in my book *On the Vanity and Uncertainty of the Sciences*, I have largely retracted this present book.

But here, perhaps, you will rebuke me again, saying: "Behold, you wrote this as a youth and retracted it as an elder; why then have you published it?" I confess, I began to write these books when I was very young, yet with the hope that I would one day release them more corrected and enriched. For that reason, I first offered them for correction to Johannes Trithemius, the Abbot of <term>Würzburg</term><note>original: "Peapolitano" refers to St. James's Abbey in Würzburg; Trithemius was a famous occult scholar and cryptographer</note>, formerly of Sponheim, a man most industrious in secret matters. 

It happened afterwards, however, that the work was intercepted before I could put the finishing touches to it. Because of corrupted copies, it began to circulate in a mutilated and unpolished state, flying through the hands of many in Italy, France, and Germany. Already, some—I know not whether through impatience or impudence—wished to expose this unformed work to the printing press. Struck by this single misfortune, I decided to publish it myself, thinking there would be less danger if the books went forth from my own hand somewhat more refined, rather than being made public by the hands of others as torn and disordered fragments. Furthermore, no...

<summary>The author asks for the reader's leniency regarding his youthful errors, explaining that he is publishing this work now only to prevent unauthorized and corrupted versions from being printed by others.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Trithemius, youth vs. maturity, publishing history, retraction, Vanity of the Sciences, Sponheim, Würzburg</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>The catchword "de" from the previous page likely refers to the beginning of a sentence regarding the preservation of the author's work.</meta>

->TO THE READER<-

...I considered it a great wrong if I did not allow this specimen of my youth to perish. However, we have added several chapters and inserted many things which it seemed negligent to omit, as the curious reader will easily perceive from the unevenness of the phrasing itself. For we did not wish to completely renew the work and, as they say, <note>original: "totam telam retexere" - a Latin idiom meaning to start over from scratch or unweave the web</note> reweave the entire web, but rather to correct it slightly and pour in some brilliance. Therefore, I ask you again, fair reader, not to judge these things based on the present time of publication, but to grant pardon to a curious youth if by chance you find anything in these pages that is otherwise than pleasing.

---

## TO THE REVEREND FATHER LORD JOHANNES TRITHEMIUS
### Abbot of Saint James in the suburbs of Würzburg <note>original: "Herbipolis" - the Latin name for the city of Würzburg</note>,
### Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa of
### Nettesheim sends greetings.

---

When I was recently staying with you (Reverend Father) for some time in your monastery at Würzburg, and we were conferring together on many things concerning chemistry <note>original: "chymicis" - referring here to alchemy</note>, many things concerning magic, many things concerning the Cabala <note>original: "cabalisticis" - Jewish mystical tradition often adapted by Renaissance scholars</note>, and other secret sciences and arts which still lie hidden in obscurity, a great question arose among the rest: why Magic itself—though it once held the very peak of sublimity by the unanimous judgment of all ancient philosophers, and was always held in the highest veneration by those ancient sages and priests—then...

<summary>In this preface and dedication, Agrippa explains his revisions to his youthful work and addresses his mentor, Johannes Trithemius, regarding the decline of the reputation of magic.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Trithemius, Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, Würzburg, alchemy, magic, Cabala, occult philosophy, Renaissance humanism</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle from Henry Cornelius Agrippa to his mentor, Johannes Trithemius, regarding the restoration of ancient wisdom.</meta>

# AGRIPPA TO JOHANNES TRITEMIUS

<margin>The Church Fathers and the Law</margin>
Regarding those things which, from the very beginning of the nascent Catholic Church, were always hateful and suspected by the holy fathers, finally exploded by the theologians, condemned by the sacred canons, and furthermore proscribed by the decrees of all laws: having contemplated the cause of this, it is in my judgment for no other reason than that, by a certain fatal depravity of times and men, many pseudo-philosophers and <term>Magi</term> <note>Agrippa uses "Magi" here to refer to practitioners of high natural and divine wisdom, distinguishing them from common sorcerers.</note> under a false name have crept in. These men, through various sects of errors and factions of false religions, have gathered together many truly execrable superstitions and deadly rites, as well as many wicked sacrileges taken from the orthodox religion, to the persecution of nature, the destruction of men, and the injury of God. They have published many books of rejected reading, such as we see circulated today, to which they have prefixed the most honorable name and title of Magic by theft and robbery.

Therefore, since these men hoped they would win credit for their execrable trifles with this most holy title of Magic, they have brought it about that the name of Magic, once most highly praised, is today rendered most hateful to all good and honest men. It is now considered a capital crime if anyone dares to profess himself a Magus in doctrine or works—unless perhaps some demented old woman living in the country wishes to be believed highly skilled and divinely powerful, so that (as <term>Apuleius</term> <note>A 2nd-century Roman writer and philosopher accused of magic; Agrippa quotes his famous work, *The Golden Ass*.</note> says) she might be able to pull down the heavens, suspend the earth, harden fountains, dissolve mountains, raise up the spirits of the dead, humble the gods, extinguish the stars, and illuminate Hell itself; or as <term>Virgil</term> <note>The Roman poet, often regarded in the Middle Ages as a prophet or magician.</note> sings:

<summary>Agrippa argues that true Magic has been unfairly condemned because fraudulent "pseudo-philosophers" have used the name to cover their own superstitions and sacrilegious practices. He laments that a once-noble title is now associated only with criminal acts or the delusions of country folk.</summary>

<keywords>Henry Cornelius Agrippa, Johannes Trithemius, Magic, pseudo-philosophy, superstition, Church Law, Apuleius, Virgil, history of magic</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory dedication to Johannes Trithemius, likely following a discussion on the origins of hidden wisdom. The catchword "eunto" suggests the next page begins with a verb of proceeding or going.</meta>

# TO JOHANNES TRITHEMIUS

<note>The author is addressing Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a famous Benedictine abbot and scholar of cryptography and the occult.</note>

Which promises by its songs to dissolve whatever mountains it wishes, but to cast hard cares upon others; to stop the water in the rivers, and to turn the stars backward; it summons the ghosts of the night, and you shall see the earth bellow beneath your feet, and the ash trees descend from the mountains. <note>The author is quoting classical poetry—likely Virgil or Ovid—to describe the stereotypical, "miraculous" powers attributed to sorcery.</note> Then there are the things which Lucan <note>Marcus Annaeus Lucanus, a Roman poet.</note> relates concerning that Thessalian Witch <note>Erictho, a gruesome sorceress from Lucan's Pharsalia.</note>, and Homer concerning the omnipotence of Circe; most of these things (as is admitted) exist as products of such deceptive opinion, such superstitious diligence, and such pernicious labor, that although they cannot fall under any non-nefarious art, they nevertheless presume to clothe themselves in the venerable title of **Magic**.

Since these matters stood thus, I wondered greatly, and was no less indignant, that no one until now has stood forth who might vindicate so sublime and sacred a discipline from the charge of impiety, or who might hand it down to us in a pure and sincere form. Indeed, among the more recent authors I have seen—Roger Bacon, Robert the Englishman, Peter of Abano, Albert the German <note>Albertus Magnus.</note>, Arnold of Villanova, Anselm of Parma, the Spaniard Picatrix, Cecco d'Ascoli the Florentine, and many others of obscure name—though they promised to pass on Magic, they provided nothing but certain ravings supported by no reason, or superstitions unworthy of all honest men.

Hence my spirit was stirred within me, and on account of both that admiration and indignation, I wished to philosophize myself, thinking that I would perform a not unpraiseworthy work, who from...

<summary>The author expresses his frustration with previous writers on magic, arguing that they have filled the discipline with superstitions and "ravings" rather than sacred philosophy. He lists several famous medieval occultists, dismissing their work as unworthy of the true title of Magic.</summary>

<keywords>Johannes Trithemius, Magic, superstition, classical mythology, Circe, Roger Bacon, Albertus Magnus, Picatrix, history of philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page serves as the introductory dedication of Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa's seminal work on occult philosophy, establishing his intent to rehabilitate the reputation of magic.</meta>

# AGRIPPA

From my youth, I have always been a curious and fearless explorer of the effects of wonders and operations full of mysteries. I have long deliberated within myself whether I might restore <term>Magic</term> <note>original: "Magiam"; here referring to the ancient "Magia" or wisdom of the Magi</note>—that ancient discipline of all wise men—by redeeming and purging it from the errors of impiety, adorning it with its own proper reasoning, and vindicating it from the injuries of slanderers. Until now, I never dared to descend into this arena. However, after the conversation we shared at <term>Würzburg</term> <note>original: "Herbipoli"; the Latin name for the city of Würzburg</note> regarding these matters, your excellent skill and learning, along with your ardent encouragement, gave me the audacity and spirit to proceed.

Therefore, having selected the opinions of philosophers of proven faith—and having purged the introductions of those malicious men who, lying with simulated knowledge, taught that the traditions of the <term>Magi</term> <note>The "Magi" were ancient Persian priest-scientists; Agrippa seeks to separate their true wisdom from later "dark" superstitions</note> and the rejected books of darkness were published by them—I have dispersed the shadows. In these recent days, I have composed three books on Magic, gathered in a brief compendium, and inscribed them with the less offensive title *Of Occult Philosophy*. 

I now submit these to your examination—you who possess the fullest expertise in these matters—to be corrected, judged, and evaluated. If anything has been written by me that might tend toward an insult to nature, an offense to the heavenly beings, or an injury to religion, I ask that you condemn the error. But if, with the scandal of impiety dissolved and purged, you find it maintains the tradition of truth, then I ask that you protect it. I desire you to act in such a way with these books of ours, and with Magic itself, so that nothing remains hidden...

<summary>In this dedicatory letter, Agrippa explains his lifelong fascination with the mysterious and his mission to "purge" the study of magic from superstitious and impious associations. He credits a conversation in Würzburg for giving him the courage to compile his findings into three books titled "Of Occult Philosophy."</summary>

<keywords>Agrippa, Occult Philosophy, Magic, Würzburg, Magi, restoration of ancient wisdom, censorship and correction</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory correspondence between the renowned abbot Johannes Trithemius and the young scholar Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa regarding the publication of Agrippa's work on magic and philosophy.</meta>

# JOHANNES TRITHEMIUS

...anything that might be of benefit, nor should anything be approved that can only cause harm: so that at last these books, having been tested by your examination, may be worthy to go forth into the public one day under happy auspices, and may not fear to undergo the judgment of posterity. Farewell, and be indulgent toward these bold undertakings.

### JOHANNES TRITHEMIUS, ABBOT
->of Saint James at Würzburg <note>original: "Herbipolis"</note>, formerly of Sponheim,<-
->to his own Heinrich Cornelius<-
->Agrippa of Nettesheim, greetings<-
->and affection.<-

With what great pleasure we received your work, most distinguished Agrippa, entitled *On Occult Philosophy* <note>original: "De occultiore Philosophia"</note>, which you offered to us for examination through the bearer of these presents, no mortal tongue can ever express, nor the writer's pen reveal. We are moved with the greatest admiration for your uncommon erudition, that you, as a young man, penetrate such secrets; and that you have been able to bring to light mysteries hidden even from many most learned men, not only beautifully and truly, but also with propriety and elegance. Wherefore, in the first place, we give thanks for your benevolence toward us, and if we are ever able, we shall undoubtedly return the favor to the best of our strength. We approve of your work, which no man—even among the most learned—will be able to praise sufficiently; furthermore, we urge that you continue with zeal into higher...

<meta>catchword: higher</meta>

<summary>Johannes Trithemius writes a glowing commendation to Agrippa, praising his profound knowledge of secret philosophy and approving the manuscript of "De Occulta Philosophia" for future publication.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Trithemius, Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, Occult Philosophy, Würzburg, Sponheim, scholarship, secret wisdom, book approval</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the letter from the Abbot Johannes Trithemius to the young scholar Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa. Trithemius is encouraging Agrippa to persevere in his difficult studies of occult philosophy and warns him to be selective about with whom he shares his secrets.</meta>

### TO CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

...matters <note>continuing from the previous page's "higher"</note>, where you have begun; nor should you permit such excellent powers of your **intellect** <term>ingenium: innate talent, genius, or intellectual capacity</term> to grow cold through idleness. We advise, petition, and request you with as much urgency as we can: that you always exercise yourself through your labor toward better things, and that you demonstrate the light of true **wisdom** <note>original: "sapientiae"</note>—by which you are most divinely enlightened—even to those who are ignorant. 

Do not let the consideration of any "good-for-nothings" <note>original: "nebulonum," literally "men of mist" or worthless scoundrels</note> pull you back from your purpose. Of them it is truly said: *The tired ox plants its foot most firmly* <note>original: "Bos lassus fortiter figit pedem." A proverb suggesting that those with limited abilities or those who are "exhausted" intellectually are often the most stubbornly set in their narrow ways.</note>. This is because, in the judgment of the wise, no one can be truly learned who has sworn an oath only to the basic principles of a single subject.

Divinity, however, has gifted you with an intellect both broad and sublime. Therefore, do not imitate the oxen, but rather the birds; do not think that you should linger over specific details, but instead confidently direct your mind toward universal truths. For a person is considered more learned in proportion to how few things they are ignorant of. 

Since your intellect is fully suited for all things, it should reasonably be occupied not with a few lowly things, but with many and more sublime matters. We warn you, however, to keep this one rule: communicate common things to common people, but share higher and secret things only with your more elevated and confidential friends. *Give hay to the ox, but sugar only to the parrot.* <note>Trithemius uses this metaphor to explain that one must match the "food" (information) to the "animal" (the listener's capacity). Commoners cannot digest "sugar" (higher secrets).</note> Understand my meaning, lest you be kicked by the heels of oxen, as has happened to many. 

Farewell, happy friend. If there is anything in my power that could be of use to you, you need only ask, and you will find it done without delay. And so that our **friendship** <note>original: "amicitia"</note> may grow stronger every day, write to us often and send some of your *nighttime studies* <term>lucubrationum: scholarly works produced by late-night study by candlelight</term>, in...

<summary>Johannes Trithemius encourages Agrippa to ignore narrow-minded critics and pursue universal knowledge, while cautioning him to keep his esoteric discoveries secret from the "ox-like" common people.</summary>
<keywords>intellect, wisdom, esotericism, secrecy, friendship, nighttime studies, Johannes Trithemius, Cornelius Agrippa</keywords>

...we urgently pray. Farewell again. From our monastery at Würzburg <note>original: "Peapolitano," a Latinization of the Abbey of St. James in Würzburg</note>. The 8th day of April, in the year 1510.

### TO THE MOST REVEREND IN CHRIST
->To the Father and most illustrious Prince, Hermann of the Counts of Wied, by the grace of God Archbishop of the holy church of Cologne, Prince Elector of the Holy Roman Empire, Arch-chancellor throughout Italy, Duke of Westphalia and Angria, etc., born Legate <term>Legatus natus: a permanent representative of the Pope by virtue of his office</term> of the most holy Roman church, and Vicar General in matters of the pontificate, Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa of Nettesheim sends his greetings.<-

Such is the celebrity of your illustrious fame, most reverend and most illustrious Prince, such the greatness of your **virtues** and the splendor of your **learning**, and your frequent practice of the finest literature, combined with solid prudence, solemn speech, and elegant readiness of expression, knowledge of many subjects, constant religion, and most praiseworthy character, with which you are endowed above the common custom of others. I say nothing of the ancient ancestral portraits of your eminent nobility, or the treasures of both old and new wealth, the breadth of your domain, and the mitres <note>The ceremonial headdress of a bishop, representing high ecclesiastical office</note> of your sacred dignities, in which your excellence stands out even as your physical...

<meta>catchword: body <note>original: "ris," the end of "corporis" (body) from the next page</note></meta>

<summary>This page concludes a letter of encouragement from Trithemius and begins Agrippa's formal dedication of his work to Hermann von Wied, the powerful Archbishop-Elector of Cologne. Agrippa praises the Archbishop's learning, character, and noble standing to secure his patronage.</summary>
<keywords>Hermann von Wied, Archbishop of Cologne, Prince Elector, Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, Würzburg, dedication, patronage, ecclesiastical titles, 1510</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication by Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa to Hermann von Wied, Archbishop of Cologne. The previous page ended with a description of the Archbishop's physical excellence, which this page completes.</meta>

### TO THE ARCHBISHOP OF COLOGNE

...body with a fitting form and strength. Although all these attributes are of the highest order, I nonetheless consider you to be far greater than all of them because of your heroic and most illustrious virtues. By these, you have surely ensured that the more learned and the more devoted to virtue a man is, the more he desires to find favor with your kindness. Therefore, I also decided to seek that honor of yours, but in the manner of the Parthians <note>original: "Parthorum more." This refers to an ancient custom, often mentioned by Roman writers, where it was considered a grave breach of etiquette to approach a ruler or person of high status without bringing a gift.</note>—that is, I say, not without a gift. We see this custom of greeting a Prince derived from the ages of the ancients even into these our times, and observed up to this point. 

When I saw so many other men of exceptional learning adorning you with the beautiful and great gifts of their scholarship, I did not want to be the only one to desert your honor and veneration, nor did I dare to approach your Highness with empty hands. While I was thinking and looking around in my library for what gift I might bestow upon so distinguished a Prince, behold, from among my hidden stores, there suddenly appeared the books on **Occult Philosophy**, or **Magic**, which I had begun to write while still a very young man and had set aside for many years, as if forgotten. 

Soon, out of reverence for your Highness, I hastened to complete them, as if to fulfill a vow <note>The author uses the language of religious obligation ("vota reddenda") to describe his drive to finish the manuscript for his patron.</note>. I was truly persuaded that I could give you nothing more pleasing than a new work on the most ancient and abstruse doctrine—a work, I say, of our curious youth, but a doctrine of antiquity which I dare say no one until now has attempted to restore. Nevertheless, these works of ours are not ascribed to you because they are worthy of you, but so that they might reveal...

<meta>catchword: to me <note>original: "mihi"</note></meta>

<summary>In this dedication, Agrippa explains that he chose to present his rediscovered and completed manuscript on "Occult Philosophy" as a gift to Archbishop Hermann von Wied, following the ancient tradition of offering a token of scholarship to a patron.</summary>
<keywords>Hermann von Wied, dedication, Occult Philosophy, Magic, Parthian custom, patronage, Agrippa von Nettesheim</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page concludes the formal dedication of "Three Books of Occult Philosophy" to Hermann von Wied. Agrippa explains that although the work began in his youth, he has revised it extensively as a mature scholar, bridging the gap between his early interests and his current expertise.</meta>

# Agrippa to the Archbishop of Cologne

*...access for me to your benevolence. I ask, therefore, if it is not improper, that these studies might find some excuse with you. For I intend to offer my vows* <note>original: "vota." Here, Agrippa uses the language of a sacred promise or a formal expression of loyalty to a patron.</note> *to you, should these studies of my youth come to public notice under the sponsorship of your Eminence. Then, with envy driven away, their memory may remain by right of permanence, providing us the fruit of a good conscience; for many things in them seem to me, even now in my older years, to be both highly useful and necessary to know. You hold, therefore, a work not only of my youth, but also of my present age. Indeed, I have corrected many errors of that youthful effort; I have polished many passages, and expanded the work with many chapters—which can be easily detected by the unevenness of the prose itself* <note>Agrippa suggests that a careful reader can tell the difference between his youthful writing style and his more mature, revised additions.</note>*. Thus, you shall know that*
->*my whole life is devoted to your*<-
->*service. Farewell, most happy Prince of*<-
->*happy Cologne. From Mechelen,*<-
->*In the year 1531,*<-
->*in the month of January.*<-
-><-

<summary>Agrippa concludes his dedication by explaining that this version of his work on magic has been significantly revised and expanded in his maturity, officially dedicating his life's work to the Archbishop's service.</summary>

## ¶ Index of the Second Book.

I. 1. On the necessity of Mathematical disciplines, and concerning many wonderful works which are performed by mathematical arts alone.
II. On numbers and their power.
III. How many virtues numbers possess in things both natural and transnatural <term>transnatural: things that exist beyond the physical world; metaphysical or supernatural</term>.
IIII. On unity <note>the number one</note> and its scale <term>scale: Latin *scala*, literally a ladder; in this context, a table of correspondences showing how a number manifests across different levels of reality (divine, celestial, elemental, etc.)</term>.
V. On duality <note>the number two</note> and its scale.
VI. On the triad <note>the number three</note> and its scale.
VII. On the quaternary <note>the number four</note> and its scale.
VIII. On the quinary <note>the number five</note> and its scale.
IX. On the senary <note>the number six</note> and its scale.
X. On the septenary <note>the number seven</note> and its scale.
XI. On the octonary <note>the number eight</note> and its scale.
XII. On the nonary <note>the number nine</note> and its scale.
XIII. On the decade <note>the number ten</note> and its scale.
XIIII. On the number eleven and twelve, with a double scale of the number twelve, namely the cabalistic <note>referring to the Jewish mystical tradition of Kabbalah</note> and the orphic <note>referring to the ancient Greek mystical teachings attributed to the legendary poet Orpheus</note>.
XV. On numbers which are above twelve, and their power and virtues.

# INDEX OF THE THIRD [BOOK].

**IIII.** On the two supports of ceremonial Magic: religion and superstition. <note>original: "ceremonialis Magiæ"</note>
**V.** On the three leaders of religion who lead us to the path of truth.
**VI.** How, by these leaders, the human soul ascends into the divine nature and is made a worker of miracles.
**VII.** That the knowledge of the true God is necessary for the Magician, and what the ancient Magi and Philosophers thought concerning God.
**VIII.** What the ancient Philosophers thought concerning the divine trinity.
**IX.** What is the true and most holy trinity, according to the orthodox faith.
**X.** On divine emanations, which the Hebrews call "enumerations," others "attributes," and the Gentiles call "gods" and "powers"; also concerning the ten Sephiroth and the ten most sacred names of God presiding over them, and their interpretation. <term>Sephiroth: in Jewish Kabbalah, these are the ten attributes or emanations through which the Infinite (Ein Sof) reveals itself and creates the universe.</term>
**XI.** On the divine names, and their power and virtue.
**XII.** On the influence of the divine names through individual mediums into these lower realms.
**XIII.** On the members of God and their influence upon our own limbs. <note>The OCR reads "diei" (of the day), but the standard text of Agrippa's work is "Dei" (of God), referring to the Kabbalistic concept of the "Divine Man" or Adam Kadmon, where divine attributes correspond to parts of the human body.</note>
**XIIII.** On the gods of the nations, and the souls of celestial bodies, and to which powers certain places were once sa- <note>The word is cut off; the catchword indicates it continues as "sacrata" or "consecrated."</note>

<meta>This page follows the table of contents for the Third Book of Agrippa’s work. Having concluded the index of chapters on ritual preparation and divine frenzy, the text now transitions to commemorative poetry. These epigrams serve as a funerary tribute to Agrippa’s second wife, Jeanne Louise de la Marck, who died in 1529. Such poems were common in Renaissance scholarly works to demonstrate the author’s social standing and the virtues of his household.</meta>

## BY HILARIUS BERTULPHUS LEDIUS
<note>Hilarius Bertulphus was a humanist scholar and secretary to Erasmus; his inclusion here highlights Agrippa’s connections to the high intellectual circles of the 16th century.</note>
->*An Epigram on the deceased wife of Agrippa.*<-

When Juno asked Venus for her girdle, <note>original: "cinglū" (cingulum); refers to the *Cestus*, the magical girdle of Venus which granted the power of seduction and grace.</note> in which 
All loveliness is contained, and all love:
That which she might wear in place of a lover for her husband,
By which she seeks to embrace her own [husband] more tightly,
But Venus said: "Seek this under another oak, <note>original: "alia sub quercu collige"; a Latin idiom meaning "look elsewhere" or "you're barking up the wrong tree."</note> if you please.
I have no supply of that from this moment on.
Rather, seek out Geneva, which excels in this one gift, <term>Gebennas: The Latin name for Geneva, the home region of Agrippa's wife.</term>
Which the Rhone washes among its broad waters.
There is the distinguished Jane Louise, <note>original: "Iana Loysa"; referring to Jeanne Louise de la Marck, Agrippa’s second wife.</note> of HEINRICH 
AGRIPPA; she is the wife who now possesses the girdle."

## BY AURELIUS AQUAPENDENTANUS
->*An Epigram on the same woman.*<-

Others have praised the beauty which, Jane Louise,
The gods above bestowed upon you as something extraordinary.
This man celebrates your modesty in a full song; Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom, representing Jane Louise’s intellectual qualities.</note>
Says, "Live long for me, learned Louise."
And who would not wish to celebrate your character, and grand words, and moderation,
And lineage, and the Graces?
Too wicked Fate, <term>Parca: One of the three Parcae or Fates who spin, measure, and cut the thread of human life.</term> alas, too violent, why did you dare
To snatch away this divine Radiance from men?
You gods above, keep her soul, keep Louise.
Jane, indeed you are worthy of nectar and ambrosia. <note>The food and drink of the gods, signifying her immortality in the afterlife.</note>

The poems use classical imagery—such as the girdle of Venus and the goddess Pallas—to elevate her memory to a divine status.</summary>

### *How Magicians gather powers from the threefold world,*
### *will be demonstrated in these three books. Chapter 1.*

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'C' featuring two putti or winged cherubs amidst floral scrolls; one putto is playing a curved horn.</image-desc> SINCE THE WORLD IS THREEFOLD—elemental <note>the physical world of the four elements: earth, air, fire, and water</note>, celestial <note>the realm of the planets and stars</note>, and intellectual <note>the spiritual realm of angels and the Divine Mind</note>—and since each lower realm is governed by the one above it, and receives the influence of its powers, it follows that the Archetype <term>Archetype|The original blueprint or the Mind of God from which all creation flows</term> and supreme Creator himself—through angels, the heavens, stars, elements, animals, plants, mi- <note>The original Latin "me-" continues as "metalla" (metals) on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page serves as the title and introduction to Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa's famous treatise on magic, defining the universe as a hierarchy of three interconnected worlds.</summary>
<keywords>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, Occult Philosophy, Threefold World, Neoplatonism, Elemental World, Celestial World, Intellectual World, Archetype</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 1, completing the word "metalla" (metals) and concluding the introductory chapter where Agrippa outlines the structure of his work and the Neoplatonic theory of ascent through the "Threefold World."</meta>

->DE OCCULTA PHILOSOPH.<-

-tals, stones, and the virtues of his omnipotence from there <note>from the divine realm</note> into us, for whose service he established and created all these things, the Magicians do not think it unreasonable that we can ascend through these same steps, through each individual world, to that same <term>Archetypal world|The realm of divine ideas or the "blueprint" of the universe in the mind of the Creator</term>, the Maker of all things and the First Cause, from whom all things are and from whom all things proceed: and not only to be able to enjoy these powers which pre-exist in the nobler things, but furthermore to be able to attract other new ones from above. Hence, they hunt for the powers of the elemental world through various mixtures of natural things by means of medicine and natural philosophy; next, they connect the celestial virtues to those mixtures by the rays and influences of the celestial world, according to the rules of the <term>Astrologers|In the Renaissance, those who studied the movements of stars to understand their influence on human affairs</term> and the disciplines of the <term>Mathematicians|This term often referred to those who studied "mathesis," including geometry, numerology, and the calculation of planetary positions</term>; furthermore, they strengthen and confirm all these things with the powers of different <note>referring to "Intelligences" or angelic beings</note> spirits through the sacred ceremonies of religions. I shall now attempt to hand down the order and process of all these things in these three books: of which the first shall contain <term>Natural Magic|The study of the hidden properties of physical things—plants, minerals, and animals</term>, the second <term>Celestial Magic|Magic that works with the influences of the planets and stars</term>, and the third <term>Ceremonial Magic|Magic involving the invocation of spiritual beings through religious ritual</term>. But I know not whether it is clearly worthy of pardon, that I, a man of lesser genius and literature, have so freely approached such a difficult, so arduous, so intricate a business in my very youth: wherefore, whatever has been said by me here, and will be said below, I do not wish anyone to assent to it more... <note>The sentence continues on the next page: "...more than their own conscience and the judgment of the Church shall permit."</note>

<summary>Agrippa explains how a practitioner can ascend the hierarchy of the universe—from physical elements to stars and finally to the divine—by combining natural philosophy, astrology, and religious ceremony. He introduces the three-part structure of his treatise and offers a humble disclaimer regarding his age and ability.</summary>
<keywords>Archetypal world, Natural Magic, Celestial Magic, Ceremonial Magic, Astrologers, Mathematicians, Neoplatonism, chain of being</keywords>

# BOOK I.

A 2
<meta>catchword: What</meta>

...I assent the more, except insofar as they have been rejected by the Catholic Church <note>Agrippa frequently included these disclaimers to protect himself from charges of heresy, though his work was often at odds with Church doctrine.</note> and the assembly of the faithful.

---

->What Magic is, what its parts are, and<-
->what kind of person a professor of Magic<-
->ought to be. Chapter 2.<-

The magical faculty, possessing vast power, filled with the highest mysteries, encompasses the most profound contemplation of the most secret things: its nature, power, quality, substance, and virtue, and the knowledge of all nature. It instructs us how things differ from one another and how they harmonize; from here producing its wonderful effects by uniting the virtues of things through their mutual application, and by joining and marrying <note>In the hermetic tradition, "marrying" (maritans) refers to the act of linking terrestrial objects to celestial influences to draw down their power.</note> the lower things to the gifts and virtues of the higher things wherever they are fitting. This is the most perfect and supreme science, this higher and holier philosophy; it is, in short, the absolute fulfillment of all the most noble philosophy. For since all systematic philosophy is divided into Physics, Mathematics, and Theology, Physics <term>Physics|In the 16th century, "Physica" or Physics referred to Natural Philosophy—the study of the physical world, its laws, and its materials.</term> teaches the nature of those things which are in the world, and investigates and explores their causes, effects, times, places, manners, outcomes, wholes, and parts.

How many there be, and what kinds of things are called elements;
What heat may bring forth, what the earth, and what the moist air.
<note>original: "Quot sint, quæ rerum species elementa uocentur, / Quid calor efficiat, quid terra, quid humidus aër."</note>

<gloss>Magic, Physics (Natural Philosophy), Mathematics, Theology, Philosophy, Elements</gloss>

<summary>Agrippa defines Magic as the highest form of philosophy that unites the physical, mathematical, and divine sciences to achieve wonderful effects. He begins by explaining the role of "Physics" (Natural Philosophy) in understanding the constituent elements of the world.</summary>

<keywords>definition of magic, natural philosophy, physics, elements, celestial influence, harmony of nature, systematic philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 2 of Book I, where Agrippa defines the three parts of Magic. Having introduced the concept on the previous page, he now details the first two pillars: Physics (Natural Philosophy) and Mathematics (Astronomy and Geometry), illustrating their scope through the poetry of Virgil.</meta>

<header>ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY.</header>

What they generate: from where also come the beginnings of the great heaven,
From where the flow of the sea, or the rainbow with its various colors:
What makes the clouds return noisy thunders:
From where the lightning is hurled from the ethereal court:
What secret torches by night, what cause brings forth
Comets, and what blind power shakes the swollen 
Lands, what are the seeds of gold, what the seeds of iron,
And the whole ingenious force of hidden nature.
Physics, the observer of natures, embraces all these things, and those of which Virgil <note>Agrippa frequently quotes the Roman poet Virgil, particularly his *Georgics* and *Aeneid*, viewing him not just as a poet but as a "philosopher-magus" who encoded natural secrets in his verse.</note> sings:
"From where the race of men and cattle come, as well as rain and fires,
From where the tremor in the earth, and by what force the deep seas swell,
Their barriers broken, and then sink back into themselves;
The virtues of herbs, the spirits and rages of wild beasts,
Every kind of shrub, and of stones also, and of reptiles."
Mathematics <term>Mathematics|In Agrippa's time, "Mathematics" referred to the Quadrivium: Arithmetic, Geometry, Music, and Astronomy. Here, he focuses on its application to the physical dimensions of the world and the movement of the stars.</term>, however, teaches us to know nature in its flat and three-fold extended dimensions, and to observe the motion and the progressions of the celestial bodies:
"By what swift motion the golden stars are snatched away,
What now forces the darkening moon to grow dim,
And the sun to suffer eclipses when its light is withdrawn,"
and those things of which Virgil sings:
"In what circle the golden sun rules the world, measured in certain parts,
Through the twelve constellations of the cosmos,"
It shows the ways of heaven and points out the stars:
"The various eclipses of the sun, and the labors <note>original: "labores" — often used in Latin to refer to the "sufferings" or eclipses of the celestial bodies.</note> of the moon,
Arcturus, the Pleiades, the Hyades, and the twin Bears." <note>original: "Triones" — refers to the "threshing oxen," a Roman name for the seven stars of the Great and Little Bears (Ursa Major and Minor).</note>

<summary>This page defines the first two branches of Magic—Physics and Mathematics—explaining how they allow the practitioner to understand natural phenomena and celestial movements. Agrippa uses extensive quotes from Virgil to show that these scientific inquiries are essential for uncovering the hidden forces of the universe.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the three pillars of Magic. Having previously touched upon Physics, Agrippa now concludes the section on Mathematics (specifically Astronomy and Astrology) and transitions into the third pillar: Theology. He then explains how Magic serves as the synthesis of these three sciences and lists the ancient authorities who mastered this art.</meta>

# BOOK I.

<sig>A 3</sig>

"Why the winter suns hasten so much to bathe themselves in the ocean, or what delay hinders the slow nights:" <note>original: "Quid tantum oceano properent se tingere soles Hyberni, uel quæ tardis mora noctibus obstet." Agrippa continues quoting Virgil’s *Georgics* (Book II) to illustrate that the study of seasons and the length of days is a mathematical and astronomical pursuit.</note>

All of these things are discerned through Mathematics itself.

"From here we can foretell the storms in the uncertain sky, from here the day of harvest and the time of sowing; and when it is fitting to strike the faithless marble <note>Poetic language for the surface of the sea.</note> with oars, when to launch the armed fleets, or when to fell the pine tree in the woods at the right season." <note>original: "Hinc tempestates dubio prædiscere coelo Possumus..." from Virgil’s *Georgics* (Book I). Agrippa argues that knowing the right timing for human actions depends on observing the stars.</note>

Theology, moreover, teaches what God is, what the mind is, what an intelligence is, what an angel is, and finally what a demon <term>Demon|In the Renaissance context, a 'daemon' was not necessarily an evil entity, but a mediating spirit or "genius" that could be good or bad.</term> is; it teaches what the soul is, what religion is, and what are the sacred institutions, rites, temples, observations, and sacred mysteries. It also instructs us concerning faith, miracles, the power of words and figures, secret operations, and the mysteries of seals <note>Signacula; these are often geometric symbols or characters used in talismans to represent spiritual forces.</note>. And, as Apuleius <note>Lucius Apuleius (c. 124–170 AD), a Platonic philosopher and author of *The Golden Ass*, who was himself once accused of using magic.</note> says, it teaches us to rightly know and be skilled in the laws of ceremonies, the divine law of sacred things, and the rights of religions. 

But now I shall collect myself: Magic itself embraces, unites, and actuates these three most powerful faculties <note>The three "faculties" or branches of knowledge: Physics, Mathematics, and Theology.</note>; therefore, it was deservedly held by the ancients to be the highest and most sacred science. This science is found illustrated by the most weighty authors and most famous writers, among whom especially Zamolxis <note>A legendary social and religious reformer of the Thracian Getae, often identified as a student of Pythagoras.</note> and Zoroaster <note>The ancient Persian prophet, traditionally regarded by Renaissance mages as the "inventor" of magic and the first of the 'Magi'.</note> shone forth, so much so that they were believed by many to be the inventors of this science. Following in their footsteps, Abaris the Hyperborean, Caramandas, Damigeron, Eudoxus, Hermippus, and other more famous high priests existed, such as Hermes Trismegistus <note>The "Thrice-Great Hermes," a legendary Egyptian figure to whom many foundational texts on alchemy, magic, and philosophy (the Hermetica) were attributed.</note>, Porphyry, Iamblichus, Plotinus, Proclus, Dar- <meta>catchword: -danus</meta>

<summary>Agrippa concludes that Magic is the ultimate "sacred science" because it synthesizes Physics, Mathematics, and Theology. He lists legendary and historical figures—from Zoroaster to the Neoplatonists—who practiced and preserved this hidden knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Mathematics, Theology, Magic, Virgil, Zoroaster, Hermes Trismegistus, Neoplatonism, Sacred Mysteries, Demonology</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page concludes the list of ancient masters of magic and establishes the essential curriculum for the aspiring magician. It continues the argument that Magic is not a single subject, but the ultimate synthesis of Natural Philosophy (Physics), Mathematics (Astronomy), and Theology.</meta>

# ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY
<sig>A 3</sig>

...[Bactrian] <note>original: "danus"; the word completes "Bactrianus" (the Bactrian), referring to Zoroaster from the previous page.</note>, Orpheus the Thracian, Gog the Greek, Germa the Babylonian, and Apollonius of Tyana. Hostanes also wrote excellently on this art; his books were unearthed from a tomb and Democritus of Abdera illustrated them with his own commentaries. Furthermore, Pythagoras, Empedocles, Democritus, Plato, and many of the most noble philosophers traveled abroad to learn this art; upon their return, they preached it with the highest sanctity and held it among their secrets. Indeed, we have found that Pythagoras and Plato, in order to learn it, approached the prophets of Memphis <note>The priests and seers of Memphis, Egypt, were considered by Renaissance scholars to be the keepers of the "Hermetic" tradition.</note> and traveled through almost all of Syria, Egypt, Judea, and the schools of the Chaldeans <term>Chaldeans|A term historically used to refer to the ancient Babylonians, particularly their caste of priests who specialized in astrology and magic.</term>, so that the most holy monuments of Magic would not be hidden from them, and so they might be imbued with divine matters.

Whoever, therefore, now desires to study in this faculty, if he is not learned in **Physics**—in which the qualities of things are declared, and in which the occult <note>In this context, "occult" simply means "hidden" or "not immediately visible," such as the way a magnet pulls iron.</note> properties of every being are revealed; and if he is not a practitioner of **Mathematics**, specifically in the aspects and figures of the stars, upon which the virtue and property of every sublime thing depends; and if he is not learned in **Theology**, where the immaterial substances that dispense and administer all things are manifested—he will not be able to understand the underlying logic of Magic. For no work perfected by Magic itself exists, nor is there any truly magical work, that does not embrace these three faculties.

<meta>This page transitions from the general definition of the magician’s education (Physics, Mathematics, and Theology) to the specific study of Natural Philosophy (Physics). It begins the technical exploration of the physical world by defining the four elements, which Agrippa considers the "alphabet" of magic.</meta>

# BOOK I.

### On the four elements, their qualities, and 
### their mutual mixing. Chapter 3.

There are four elements and primary foundations of all physical things: fire, earth, water, and air. From these, all "elemented" things <term>elemented things|In Renaissance philosophy, this refers to any physical object or organism composed of the four elements, as opposed to the "pure" elements themselves which exist in a more abstract state.</term> in these lower realms are composed. This composition occurs not by a simple piling together <note>original: "coaceruationis"; referring to a mere mechanical heap rather than a chemical or spiritual union.</note>, but according to transmutation and union; likewise, when these things perish, they are resolved back into the elements.

None of the elements we perceive with our senses is entirely pure; rather, they are mixed to a greater or lesser degree and are capable of being transformed into one another. For instance, earth that becomes muddy and dissolved becomes water; when that water is thickened and condensed, it becomes earth again. Through heat, water evaporates and passes into air; when that air is superheated, it turns into fire. When this fire is extinguished, it returns to air; yet when it is cooled after intense burning <note>original: "superadustione"</note>, it becomes earth, stone, or sulfur—as is made manifest by the residue left by a lightning strike. 

Plato, however, is of the opinion that earth is entirely untransmutable, while the other elements can transform both into earth and into each other. According to this view, earth is not changed by the subtler elements but is instead divided—either dissolved or mixed—into those things that dissolve it, eventually returning to its own nature. 

Each of the elements has two specific qualities. The first of these it retains as its own proper nature, while in the second—acting as a middle ground—it agrees with the element that follows it. For fire is **hot and dry**; earth is **dry and cold**; water is **cold and moist**; and air is **moist and hot**. And in this manner, according to...

<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: two</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the four elements (fire, earth, water, and air) as the fundamental building blocks of the physical world, explaining how they transform into one another and listing their primary qualities.</summary>
<keywords>four elements, transmutation, Plato, qualities of elements, fire, earth, water, air, physics, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical classification of the four elements begun on the previous page. Having established that elements can transform into one another, Agrippa now details the specific mathematical and philosophical qualities that define their relationships according to the Stoics and Plato.</meta>

->ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY<-

*...through two contrary qualities, the elements are contrary to each other: as fire is to water, and earth to air. Furthermore, according to another method, the elements are opposed to each other: for some are heavy, such as earth and water, and others are light, such as air and fire. For this reason, the Stoics* <term>Stoics|A school of ancient Greek philosophy that viewed the universe as a rational, material entity governed by divine reason; they categorized physical matter as either "acting" or "being acted upon."</term> *designated the former as passive and the latter as active. Accordingly, Plato, distinguishing them by yet another method, assigns three qualities to each: namely, to fire he assigns sharpness, rarity* <note>original: "raritatem"; in this context, it refers to being thin or subtle, the opposite of dense.</note>, *and motion; but to earth he assigns darkness, density, and rest. According to these qualities, the elements of fire and earth are contraries. The remaining elements, however, borrow their qualities from these: so that air receives two qualities of fire—rarity and motion—and one of earth, namely darkness. In contrast, water receives two qualities of earth—darkness and density—and one of fire, namely motion. Furthermore, fire is twice as rare as air, three times more mobile, and four times sharper. Air, moreover, is twice as sharp as water, three times more rare, and four times more mobile. Likewise, water is twice as sharp as earth, three times more rare, and four times more mobile. Therefore, just as fire relates to air, so air relates to water, and water to earth; and conversely, as earth relates to water, so water relates to air, and air to fire. This is the root and foundation of all bodies, natures, virtues, and wondrous works. Whoever knows these qualities of the elements and their mixtures will easily perform wonderful and stupendous works, and will be perfected in natural magic* <term>Natural Magic|For Agrippa, this is the highest branch of natural philosophy, using the inherent, "occult" properties of physical things to produce marvelous effects without the aid of spirits.</term>.*

<meta>catchword: Concerning the triple [De triplici]</meta>

<summary>This page explains the elemental theories of the Stoics and Plato, defining the elements through mathematical proportions of sharpness, density, and motion. Agrippa argues that understanding these ratios is the essential foundation for practicing natural magic.</summary>

BOOK I.

## On the Threefold Nature of the Elements to be Considered
### Chapter 4.

Therefore, there are the four elements we have mentioned, and without a perfect knowledge of them, we can produce no effect in **Magic**. <term>Magic|In this context, "Magia" refers to the study of the hidden laws of nature and the divine, rather than modern stage magic.</term> Each of them, however, is threefold, so that the number four <note>original: "quaternarius"</note> completes the number twelve; and progressing through the seven <note>original: "septenarium"</note> toward the ten <note>original: "denarium"</note>, it advances to the **supreme unity**—from which every power and wondrous operation depends. <note>Agrippa is using Pythagorean numerology here: the 4 elements across 3 levels of reality create 12 (the signs of the zodiac); the progression through 7 (the planets) leads to 10 (the Tetractys), which symbolizes the return to the One or God.</note> In the first order, therefore, the elements are pure; they are neither composed nor changed, nor do they undergo mixing, but are incorruptible. It is not *from* them, but *through* them that the powers of all natural things are brought into effect. Their powers can be explained by no one, because they can do all things in all things. He who is ignorant of these can reach no operation of wondrous effects.

Elements of the second order are composite, manifold, various, and impure; yet they are reducible through **art** <note>original: "artem"; referring here to Alchemy or the Hermetic arts used to purify substances.</note> to a state of pure simplicity. Once they have been returned to their simplicity, their power is above all things, giving completion to all occult operations and the workings of nature; and these are the foundation of all **natural Magic**.

Elements of the third order are not elements primarily or in themselves, but are twice-composed <note>original: "decomposita"; refers to substances that have been mixed and transformed further away from their original pure state.</note>, varied, manifold, and interchangeable among one another. They are the infallible medium, and therefore they are called middle na-

<meta>catchword: tures,</meta>

<summary>Agrippa explains that the four elements exist in three "orders" of purity, ranging from incorruptible spiritual forces to the impure, changeable matter of the physical world. He argues that mastering these elements, particularly through alchemical purification, is the essential foundation for all magical work.</summary>

<keywords>four elements, Pythagorean numerology, natural magic, alchemy, purification, three orders of elements, occult philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page completes Agrippa's classification of the elements into three "orders." On the previous page, he described elements of the first order as pure and incorruptible, and the second order as composite but reducible to simplicity through art. Here, he describes the third and final order—the "middle nature"—which acts as a bridge for magical operations.</meta>

->DE OCCVLTA PHILOSOPH.<-

...[na]ture, or the soul of the middle nature, there are very few who understand their deep mysteries. In them, through certain numbers, degrees, and orders, is the completion of every effect in any natural, celestial, and super-celestial thing. They are wonderful and full of mysteries, those things which can be worked in **Magic**, both natural and divine. For through them comes the binding <note>original: "ligatio"; referring to the magical practice of binding spirits or natural forces.</note> of all things, as well as dissolutions, **transmutations**, the knowledge of the future and prediction; even the driving out of evil demons and the conciliation of good spirits descends from them. Therefore, without these threefold elements and a knowledge of the same, let no one trust that he can work anything in the occult sciences of Magic and nature. But whoever knows how to reduce these—the impure into the pure, the manifold into the simple—and knows how to discern their nature, virtue, and power in number, degrees, and order, without division of substance, he will easily obtain the knowledge and perfect operation of all natural things and celestial secrets.

Just as the demons of darkness are stronger in the darkness itself, so the good demons—who are angels of light—receive an increase from light, not only from the divine, solar, and celestial light, but even from the light of the fire that is among us. # BOOK I.

...eed; and the Almighty himself, in the Old Law <note>original: "ueteri lege"; referring to the Mosaic Law or Old Testament.</note>, required that all his sacrifices be offered with fire, and that a fire should burn forever upon the altar. This same practice was observed by the priests of Vesta among the Romans, who preserved and guarded a perpetual flame.

However, the basis and foundation of all the <term>elements</term> <note>In the 16th century, "elements" referred to the four building blocks of the universe: Fire, Earth, Air, and Water.</note> is the **Earth**. For she is the object, the subject, and the receptacle of all celestial rays and influences. Within herself, she contains the seeds and <term>seminal virtues</term> <note>original: "seminales uirtutes"; these are the hidden blueprints or spiritual "codes" that allow life to take form.</note> of all things. For this reason, she is said to be animal, vegetable, and mineral. Being fertilized by all the other elements and the heavens, she is capable of bringing forth all things from within herself. If you take from her any part—no matter how hidden, washed, purified, or refined—and leave it for a time **under the open sky**, it will soon be fertilized by celestial powers. Becoming "pregnant," it will produce plants from itself; it will produce worms and living creatures; it will produce small stones and the bright sparks of metals. Within her lie the greatest secrets, especially when she has been purified by the craft of fire and reduced to her simplest state through proper washing. She is the first matter of our creation, and the truest medicine for our restoration and preservation.

# ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY

...and [be able to act]: no herb, nor any plant of any kind, could sprout without the moistening of water. Within water itself is the <term>seed-bearing virtue</term> <note>original: "seminaria... uirtus"; the inherent power within a substance to generate life.</note> of all things: first of animals, whose seed is clearly watery; but also the seeds of shrubs and herbs, though they be earthy, must necessarily be softened by water if they are to be fertile—whether this happens by drinking in the moisture of the earth, or by dew, or rain, or by water applied purposefully.

Indeed, only Earth and Water are described by **Moses** as producing a **living soul** <note>original: "animam uiuentem"; a reference to the Book of Genesis regarding the creation of life.</note>. But to water he attributes a double production: namely, of those creatures swimming in the waters, and those flying in the air above the earth. The same scripture testifies that even earthly productions are partly due to water itself, saying that after creation, the shrubs and plants had not germinated because **God** had not yet rained upon the earth.

So great is the power of this element that not even spiritual **regeneration** <note>A reference to the Christian sacrament of Baptism.</note> can happen without water, just as **Christ** himself testified to **Nicodemus**. Its power in religion is also greatest, in atonements and purifications, and its necessity is no less than that of fire. Its benefits are infinite and its uses manifold, and all things consist by its power; as that which possesses the power of generating, nourishing, and increasing.

Hence **Thales of Miletus** <note>A Greek philosopher (c. 624 – 548 BC) who famously proposed that water is the "first principle" or primary substance of the universe.</note> and **Hesiod** <note>An ancient Greek poet whose *Theogony* describes the origins of the world.</note> established water as the principle of all things, and said it was the oldest and most powerful of all elements, because indeed it commands all the others. For, as **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman author of the *Naturalis Historia*.</note> says, the waters devour the lands, they [quench] flames,

### ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY.

*...offering, binding, moving, and filling all things. No less does it receive and retain within itself the <term>species<gloss>perceptible forms or mental images</gloss></term> of all things—whether natural or artificial—and of any speech whatsoever, like a kind of divine mirror. Carrying these with it and entering the bodies of humans and animals through the pores, it impresses them both in sleep and while awake; thus, it provides the material for various wonders, dreams, omens, and auguries. From this they also say it happens that one passing a place where a man has been killed, or where a fresh corpse is hidden, is stirred by fear and dread: because the air in that place is full of the horrible images of the homicide, and when it is breathed in, it affects and disturbs the spirit of the person with similar images; from which it happens that dread follows. For everything impressing itself suddenly strikes nature with amazement. From this, many philosophers have judged that the air is the cause of dreams and of many other impressions on the soul, through the carrying of <term>idols</term> <note>original: "idolorum"; referring to the "eidola" of Epicurean philosophy—thin films or husks shed by objects that travel through the air to the senses.</note>, or likenesses, or species, which fall away from things and speeches, multiplied in the air itself, until they reach the senses, and finally the **imagination** and soul of the receiver: namely, those which wi*

### BOOK I.

...unburdened by cares and unimpeded, expecting such images coming toward it, it is informed by them. For the very <term>species<gloss>perceptible forms or mental images</gloss></term> of things themselves, although they are carried by their own nature to the senses of humans and animals, can nevertheless acquire some impression from heaven while they are in the air. Because of this—and combined with the suitability arising from the physical disposition of the receiver—they may be carried more strongly to the senses of one person than another.

And from this it is naturally possible—and far removed from all superstition, with no other mediating spirit <note>Agrippa emphasizes that this is "natural magic," not involving demons or angels, but rather the natural properties of the element of Air.</note> involved—for one person to be able to communicate the concept of his mind to another at any distance whatsoever, even the longest or an unknown distance or dwelling, in a very short time. And even if the time in which this happens cannot be measured precisely, it is nonetheless necessary that it occur within twenty-four hours; and I know how to do this, and have done it often.

The **Abbot Trithemius** <note>original: "Abbas Tritemius"; Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516) was Agrippa’s mentor and a famed cryptographer and occultist who wrote about secret communication.</note> once knew the same and did it. How certain <term>idols</term> <note>original: "idola"; referring to the "eidola" of Epicurean philosophy—thin films or husks shed by objects that travel through the air to be perceived by the senses.</note>—not only spiritual but also natural—flow out from things (for example, through a certain influx from one body to another) and grow strong in the air itself, and offer and show themselves to us through light, motion, sight, and even other senses, and sometimes perform wonders upon us, **Plotinus** <note>original: "Plotinus"; a 3rd-century Neoplatonist who taught that the universe is an interconnected living being where all parts influence one another.</note> proves and teaches.

And we see how, when the **south wind** <note>original: "austro flante"; the south wind was traditionally viewed as warm and moist, capable of "thickening" the air.</note> blows, the air is thickened into thin clouds, in which images of very distant things are reflected as if in a mirror—images of camps, mountains, horses, and people, and other things—which vanish as soon as the clouds depart. And in...

<summary>Agrippa explains how the Air acts as a medium for "species" and "idols," enabling natural telepathy and the appearance of mirages. He claims to have personally practiced long-distance mental communication within a 24-hour window, citing the Abbot Trithemius and Plotinus as authorities.</summary>

# ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY

<center>### BOOK I.</center>

In his work on **meteors** <note>original: "meteoris"; referring to Aristotle's *Meteorologica*.</note>, **Aristotle** explains that the **Iris** <gloss>the rainbow</gloss> is formed in a cloud of air through a certain likeness to a mirror. And **Albertus** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval scholar who wrote extensively on natural science.</note> says that the **images** <term>effigies<gloss>outward forms or likenesses</gloss></term> of bodies can be easily formed in moist air by a natural power, in the same way that the images of things exist within the things themselves. 

**Aristotle** also recounts that it happened to someone with weak vision that the air nearby became like a mirror for him, and the **visual ray** <note>Ancient and medieval optics often posited "extramission," the idea that the eye sends out rays to "touch" objects.</note> was reflected back to him because it could not penetrate the air. Consequently, wherever he went, he believed his own **image** <term>idolum<gloss>a phantom or optical double</gloss></term> was preceding him, facing toward him. 

Similarly, through the craftsmanship of certain mirrors, the images we desire can be projected into the air, even at a distance, outside of the mirrors themselves. When unlearned men see these, they think they are seeing the shadows of **demons** or of **souls**, when in fact they are nothing but certain **likenesses** <term>simulachra<gloss>representations or optical illusions</gloss></term> related to the objects themselves and entirely devoid of life.

Furthermore, it is well known that if someone is in a dark place, devoid of all light except for a tiny hole where a ray of the sun enters, and a piece of white paper or a flat mirror is placed there, they can see upon it whatever is happening outside in the sunlight <note>This is a classic description of the *camera obscura* (dark chamber), a foundational principle of photography.</note>. 

There is also another even more wonderful **marvel** <term>præstigium<gloss>an amazing feat or illusion</gloss></term>: if someone, on a clear night of the full moon, takes images painted with a certain skill or written letters and places them against the rays of the moon, their likenesses are multiplied in the air and carried upward. Being reflected along with the rays of the moon, another person who is aware of the matter can see, read, and recognize them at a great distance upon the very disk or circle of the moon. This is extremely <note>The text cuts off here at "uti-", completed by the catchword "lissimum" on the next page to form *utilissimum* (useful).</note> [useful] for sending secret messages to besieged villages and cities.

<meta>catchword: useful</meta>

<summary>Agrippa describes natural optical phenomena such as rainbows and reflections, explaining how images can be projected into the air and even used to send secret messages via the moon's reflection.</summary>

### BOOK I.

[...is a most] exquisite artifice, once practiced by Pythagoras, <note>Pythagoras (c. 570–495 BC) was an ancient Greek philosopher. While best known for the Pythagorean theorem, in the Renaissance he was often associated with "natural magic" and secret mathematical arts.</note> and even today still known to some, just as it is not unknown to me. And all these things, and many more and greater besides, are founded in the very nature of the air, and find their explanations in **Mathematics** and **Optics**. And just as these likenesses <note>original: "simulachra," referring to images or optical reflections</note> are reflected to the sight, so sometimes they are even reflected to the hearing, which is manifest in the **echo**. But there are more hidden artifices than those, by which one may be able, even across a remote distance, to hear and understand what another speaks or whispers in secret.

From the element of air also come the **winds**; for they are nothing other than air in motion and stirred up. Of these there are four principal ones, blowing from the four quarters of the heaven: namely, **Notus** from the south, **Boreas** from the north, **Zephyrus** from the west, and from the east **Apeliotes**, otherwise known as **Eurus**. Pontanus <note>Giovanni Gioviano Pontano (1429–1503), an Italian humanist and poet known for his didactic poems on astronomy and meteorology.</note> encompassed these in these two short verses, saying:

> Boreas breathes from high Olympus, Notus from the depths;
> Zephyrus sits in the northwest, and Eurus comes from the sunrise. <note>original: "A summo Boreas, Notus imo spirat Olympo; Caurum insedit Zephyrus, uenit Eurus ab ortu"</note>

**Notus** is the southern wind: cloudy, moist, hot, and sickly—the one whom Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), a Church Father best known for translating the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate).</note> calls the "cupbearer of the rains." Ovid <note>The Roman poet Ovid (43 BC – 17 AD). This famous description of the South Wind comes from his "Metamorphoses," Book I.</note> describes him thus:

> Notus flies out on soaking wings,
> His terrible face covered in a pitch-black mist;
> His beard is heavy with rain-clouds, water flows from his white hair;
> Mists sit upon his brow, and his wings and the folds of his robes drip with dew.

BOOK I.

## On the types of compounds, how they relate to the elements, and what the elements themselves have in common with the soul, the senses, and character

### Chapter 7.

Following the four simple elements, there follow next the four classes of perfect things composed from them: stones, metals, plants, and animals. Although all the elements come together in the composition of each of these, each class nevertheless follows and imitates one primary element. For all stones are earthy, as they are heavy by nature and sink; they are so solidified by dryness that they cannot be melted. Metals, however, are watery and capable of being liquefied; as natural philosophers <note>original: "Physici"</note> admit and as Alchemy proves, they are generated from a viscous water or from watery quick-silver <note>term: "argentum uiuum" (mercury), which was believed in alchemy to be the "mother" of all metals</note>. Plants have such an affinity with the air that they will not sprout or grow together except under the open sky. So it is with all animals:

> *A fiery vigor and celestial origin belongs to them,* <note>This famous line is from Virgil’s *Aeneid*, Book 6, line 730, describing the divine spark within living things.</note>
> *And fire is so native to them that if it is extinguished,*

all life soon fails. Furthermore, each of these classes is itself divided according to the degrees of the elements. For among stones, those are called primarily "earthy" which are opaque and heavy; those are "watery" which are transparent and condensed from water, such as crystal, beryl, and pearls in shells; and there are "airy" stones which float upon the water and are spongy, such as
