In plants as well, the roots imitate the earth because of their density; the leaves imitate water because of their sap; the flowers imitate air because of their delicacy; and the seeds imitate fire because of their generative spirit. Furthermore, some plants are called hot, others cold, others moist, and others dry, borrowing the names of the elements from their respective qualities. Among animals, some are more earthy than the rest and inhabit the bowels of the earth, such as worms, earthworms, moles, and many reptiles; others are watery, such as fish; others are airy, which cannot live outside the air; and there are also fiery animals inhabiting fire, such as salamanders and certain cicadas called pyraustas <note>original: "pyraustas", a mythical insect or moth said to live in the flames of a furnace.</note>, as well as those that boil with a certain fiery heat, such as pigeons, ostriches, lions, and those which the Wise One <note>original: "Sapiens", likely referring to the author of the biblical Book of Wisdom (specifically Wisdom 11:18) or Job.</note> calls beasts breathing out fiery vapor. Furthermore, in animals, the bones represent earth, the flesh air, the vital spirit fire, and the humors represent water. These humors are also divided among the elements: for red choler <note>term: "rubra cholera" (yellow bile), the humor associated with a hot and dry temperament.</note> yields to fire, blood to air, phlegm <note>original: "pituita"</note> to water, and black bile to earth. Finally, in the soul itself, according to the testimony of Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo, the influential early Christian theologian.</note>, the intellect represents fire, reason represents air, imagination represents water, and the senses represent

senses. And these are also divided among the elements: for sight is fiery, since it does not perceive without fire and light; hearing is airy, for sound is produced by the striking of the air; both smell and taste are referred to water, for without its moisture there is neither flavor nor odor; finally, touch is entirely earthy, associating itself with coarser bodies. The actions and operations of man are also governed by the elements: for a slow and solid movement is marked by earth; fear, sluggishness, and relaxed effort are marked by water; alacrity <note>original: "alacritatem"; refers here to a cheerful readiness or briskness of spirit.</note> and friendly manners by air; and a sharp, angry impulse by fire. Therefore, the elements are the first of all things; all things are from them and according to them, and the elements themselves are in all things, diffusing their powers through everything.

->Chapter 8.<-

It is the unanimous opinion of all the Platonists <note>Followers of Plato's philosophy, particularly the Neoplatonists who believed in a hierarchical universe where physical things reflect spiritual truths.</note> that just as in the Archetypal world <term>Archetypal world: the "divine blueprint" or world of ideas that exists in the mind of God, serving as the model for the physical world.</term> all things are in all things, so also in this corporeal <note>physical or bodily</note> world, all things are in all things, though in different ways according to the nature of the things receiving them. Thus, the elements are not only in these lower things, but also in the heavens, in the stars, in demons, in angels, and finally in the very Creator and Archetype of all. But in these lower things, the elements are certain coarse forms, immersed in

For the solidity of the celestial earth is there without thickness; the agility of the water and air is far from flowing away; the heat of fire there is not burning, but shining, and quickening <note>original: "uiuificās"; giving life or vitalizing.</note> all things with its heat.

Furthermore, among the stars <note>In the geocentric model used here, "stars" (stellae) refers to both the fixed stars and the wandering stars, which we call planets.</note>, Mars and the Sun are fiery; Jupiter and Venus are airy; Saturn and Mercury are watery; and those are earthy which inhabit the eighth sphere <term>eighth sphere: the Firmament, or the layer of the fixed stars, located beyond the seven planetary spheres.</term> and the Moon (which is, however, thought by many to be watery), specifically when, like the earth, she attracts the celestial waters; being soaked in these, she flows into us and communicates them by her proximity.

There are also among the [zodiac] signs certain ones that are fiery, some earthy, some airy, and some watery. They rule the elements in the heavens, distributing those four triplicities <term>triplicities: a grouping of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element, representing the beginning, middle, and end of that element's influence.</term> to them, providing each element with a beginning, middle, and end. Thus, the beginning of fire is Aries, its progress and increase is Leo, and its end is Sagittarius. The beginning of earth is Taurus, its progress is Virgo, and its furthest point is Capricorn. The beginning of air is Gemini, its movement is Libra, and its boundary is Aquarius. The beginning of water is Cancer, its middle is Scorpio, and its end is Pisces.

From the mixtures of these planets and signs, together with the elements, all bodies are composed. Now, indeed, even the demons <note>original: "dæmones"; in Renaissance magic, these are not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense but spiritual beings or intelligences that inhabit various realms of the universe.</note> are distinguished from one another by this reasoning, so that some are called fiery, others terrestrial, others airy, and others watery. Styx <note>In Greek mythology, the river that forms the boundary between Earth and the Underworld.</note> is watery, and Acheron <note>Another river of the Underworld, associated here with earthiness or sorrow.</note> is earthy. Indeed, we also read in the Gospels of the fire of Gehenna <note>original: "gehennæ"; a valley near Jerusalem used as a metaphor for the fires of hell.</note> and the eternal fire into which the cursed will be commanded to go. In the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation.</note>, we read of the lake of fire; and Isaiah says of the damned that the Lord will strike them with corrupt air. In the book of Job, we read that they shall pass from the waters of snow to excessive heat; and in the same book, we read of a land dark and covered with the gloom of death, a land of misery and darkness.

Furthermore, these elements are placed even within the super-mundane angels and blessed intelligences. For in them, there is a stability of essence, which is an earthy power by which they are the firm seats of God. There is also mercy and piety, a watery virtue that cleanses; hence they are called "waters" by the Psalmist where he says of heaven, "You who rule the waters above it." <note>A reference to Psalm 104:3.</note> There is also in them a subtle spirit of air and a shining fire of love. For this reason, they are called in the Holy Scriptures the "wings of the wind," and elsewhere the Psalmist says of them: "You who make your angels spirits, and your ministers a burning fire." <note>Psalm 104:4.</note>

Among the angelic orders <note>Refers to the traditional nine-fold hierarchy of angels established by Dionysius the Areopagite.</note>, the Seraphim, Virtues, and Powers are fiery. The Cherubim are earthy. The Thrones and Archangels are watery. The Dominations and Principalities are airy.

Is it not also read of the Archetype <term>Archetype: the original model or "blueprint" of all things, referring to God.</term> Himself, the maker of all things, "Let the earth be opened, and let it bring forth a Savior"? <note>Isaiah 45:8. Here, the author identifies the "earth" quality in God as the source of manifestation.</note> Is it not said of the same that He is a "fountain of living water, cleansing and reigning"? Does not the same Spirit breathe the "breath of life"? And is He not also—as Moses and Paul testify—a "consuming fire"? <note>Deuteronomy 4:24 and Hebrews 12:29.</note> Therefore, no one can deny that the elements are found everywhere and in all things, each in its own proper way.

### On the virtues of natural things depending directly on the elements
#### Chapter 9.

**T**he natural virtues of things are partly elemental, such as heating, cooling, moistening, and drying. These are called "primary operations" or "primary qualities," and they are active by nature. For these qualities alone completely transform the entire substance of a thing, which no other qualities can do. 

However, certain other qualities are inherent in things because of the elements that compose them, which go beyond these primary qualities. These include: ripening, digesting, dissolving, softening, hardening, <term>styptic: substances that contract tissues or stop bleeding.</term> cleansing, corroding, <term>caustic: burning or stinging.</term> opening, evaporating, strengthening, soothing, gluing together, <term>obstructing: original "opilatiuae," referring to qualities that block or close pores.</term> expelling, retaining, attracting, repelling, numbing, expanding, lubricating, and many others. For an elemental quality has many ways to act within a mixture <note>A "mixture" refers to any physical object or organism composed of the four elements.</note> which it cannot perform on its own. And these operations are called "secondary qualities," because they follow the nature and mea-

BOOK I.

*...nature of the mixing of primary virtues:* <note>The "primary virtues" refer to the four basic elemental qualities: heat, cold, moisture, and dryness.</note> *as is treated extensively in the books of physicians. Just as maturation is the operation of natural heat within the substance of matter according to a specific measure, so is hardening the operation of cold; likewise freezing, and so on. And these operations sometimes act upon a specific limb,* <note>original: "membrū determinatū"; meaning a specific organ or part of the body.</note> *such as those that provoke urine, or milk, or menstruation. These are called "third qualities," which follow the secondary, just as the secondary follow the primary. Through these primary, secondary, and third qualities, many diseases are both cured and caused.*

*Many things are also made by artifice that men greatly wonder at, such as the fire that burns in water, which they call Greek Fire;* <note>original: "ignem graecum"; a legendary incendiary weapon used by the Byzantine Empire that was famously difficult to extinguish and could burn on water.</note> *Aristotle teaches many of its compositions in a specific treatise on this subject. Furthermore, inextinguishable fires, incombustible oils, and perpetual lamps are made, which can be extinguished neither by wind, nor water, nor by any other means. This would seem utterly incredible, were it not for that most famous lamp that once shone in the temple of Venus, in which burned the stone asbestos, which once lit, in truth is never extinguished. Conversely, wood or some other combustible material is prepared so that from [fire...]*

### *Chapter 10. On the Occult Virtues of Things.*

Moreover, there are other virtues in things which do not belong to any element <note>In this context, "element" refers to the four classical elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note>, such as driving away poison, dispelling carbuncles <note>original: "anthraces"; referring to inflammatory skin lesions or sores.</note>, attracting iron, or some other thing: and this virtue is a consequence of the species and form <term>Form: in Renaissance philosophy, the "form" is the spiritual or essential blueprint of an object that gives it its specific identity and powers, distinct from its physical "matter."</term> of this or that thing. Hence, even in a tiny quantity, it has a significant effect in action, which is not granted to an elemental quality. For these virtues, because they are highly "formal," can achieve a great deal with very little matter; however, an elemental virtue, because it is material, requires much matter to act significantly. Moreover, they are called "occult properties" because their causes are hidden, so much so that the human intellect cannot investigate them in every respect; therefore, philosophers have acquired the greatest part of them through long experience rather than through the investigation of reason. For just as food is digested in the stomach through heat, which we re-

Likewise, there are other innate powers within things that go beyond the elemental qualities <note>The "four elements": Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note> we recognize. We admire and are often struck with wonder by these unknown powers, which are rarely or never seen—such as the phoenix, that unique bird which renews itself, as we read in the works of Ovid:

> There is one bird which restores and reseeds itself,
> The Assyrians call it the phoenix. <note>original: "Vna est quæ reparat, séq; ipsa reseminat ales, / Assyrij phoenica uocant." From Ovid's Metamorphoses, XV, 392-393.</note>

And in another place:

> Egypt gathers to witness the miracle of such a sight,
> And the cheering crowd hails the rare bird.

So too the ostrich "cooks" and digests cold, hard iron as nourishment for its body; it is even said that its stomach remains unharmed even by red-hot iron. Similarly, the little fish called the *echines* <note>original: "echines"; commonly known as the remora or "suckerfish." Ancient naturalists like Pliny claimed this small fish could halt a massive ship in mid-voyage.</note> so restrains the force of the winds and tames the fury of the sea that, no matter how much the storms rage or how many sails are caught in the wind, it can halt a ship by touch alone, holding it so still that it cannot be moved. In the same way, the salamander and the *pyrausta* <note>original: "pyrausta"; a mythical fire-dwelling insect or "fire-fly" described by Pliny and Aristotle.</note> are small creatures that live in fire; though they may appear to burn, they are never harmed. There is a similar account of a certain bitumen <note>A naturally occurring petroleum or asphalt-like substance.</note> used to coat the weapons of the Amazons, which could be destroyed by neither iron nor fire. Legend says Alexander the Great used this same substance to coat the bronze "Caspian Gates." <note>The Gates of Alexander, a legendary barrier supposedly built in the Caucasus to keep out the forces of Gog and Magog.</note> We also read that with similar bitumen...

The holy Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), a Father of the Church best known for translating the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate).</note> himself reports that one of these once spoke to the blessed Anthony the Hermit, condemning the pagan error of worshipping animals and asking him to pray to the common God on its behalf. Jerome also affirms that one of these was once produced alive for public view and was soon after sent to the Emperor Constantine.

## How occult virtues are infused into the species of things by the Ideas, through the principles of the World Soul and the rays of the stars; and which things abound most in this virtue

### Chapter 11.

The Platonists <note>Followers of Plato’s philosophy, who believed that the physical world is a reflection of a higher, perfect reality.</note> say that all lower things are formed according to higher Ideas. They define an "Idea" as a form that exists above bodies, souls, and minds; it is single, simple, pure, immutable, indivisible, incorporeal, and eternal. They hold that the nature of all Ideas is the same. Furthermore, they place Ideas first in the Good itself—that is, in God—by way of causality.

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the Platonic theory of Ideas from Chapter 11. It explores the hierarchy of how these perfect forms descend from the Divine Mind into the World Soul, and finally into material nature as "shadows" and "seeds."</meta>

->BOOK I.<-

...[minds] to be distinct among themselves only by certain relative reasons: lest whatever exists in the world be one single thing without any variety; yet they agree among themselves in essence, lest God be a multiplex substance. Secondly, they place these Ideas in the intelligible itself, that is, in the World Soul <term>anima mundi; in Platonic philosophy, the animating spirit that permeates all nature</term>, through forms properly called, and moreover differing from each other as absolute forms, so that in God all Ideas are indeed one form: but in the World Soul they are many. They are placed in the subsequent minds—whether joined to a body or separated from a body—already by a certain participation, becoming more and more distinct by degrees. They place in Nature <note>original: "natura"; here referring to the creative, biological force of the world</note> certain lowest seeds of forms infused by the Ideas: finally, they place them in matter as shadows. Added to this is the fact that there are as many seminal reasons <term>rationes seminales; "seed-like reasons" or formative principles that guide the development of living things</term> of things in the World Soul as there are Ideas in the Divine Mind <term>mens divina; the supreme intellect of God</term>; by which reasons the Soul itself built for itself in the heavens, beyond the stars, even figures: and it impressed properties upon all these. From these stars, figures, and properties, therefore, all the virtues and properties of the lower species depend, so that any species has a celestial figure suitable to it, from which also proceeds its own marvelous power in operation, just as it receives its own proper endowment from its Idea through the seminal reasons of the World Soul. For Ideas are not only the causes of the existence of any species, but they are also the causes of every virtue that is within such a species; and this is what many of the philosophers say, that to certain virtues, specifically a certain and sta=

[intelligi]ble <note>original: "bilem", the suffix of "intelligibilem"</note> reason, being neither fortuitous nor accidental, but effective, powerful, and unfailing, doing nothing in vain or without purpose. Through these, the virtues <term>virtutes; in this context, "virtues" refers to the inherent powers or medicinal/magical properties of a thing</term> existing in the nature of things are set in motion. These virtues are truly the operations of the Ideas. However, they appear differently by accident <term>per accidens; a philosophical term meaning a non-essential or external change</term>, namely due to the impurity and inequality of matter. In this way, we find that things even of the same species are more or less powerful, according to the purity or the confusion of the matter; for all celestial influences can be hindered by the confusion and unsuitability of matter. Thus, it was a proverb among the Platonists themselves that "celestial virtues are poured in according to the merit of the matter." <note>This refers to the idea that a physical object must be "worthy" or properly prepared to receive divine or astrological power.</note> Virgil mentions this when he sings:

> They have a fiery vigor and a celestial origin
> in their seeds, so long as harmful bodies do not slow them down. <note>Virgil, *Aeneid* 6.730-731. The original Latin: "Igneus est ollis uigor, & cœlestis origo / Seminibus, quantum non noxia corpora tardant."</note>

Therefore, those things in which the Idea is less submerged in matter—that is, those which receive a greater likeness to the "separated" forms <note>pure spiritual forms existing apart from physical bodies</note>—possess more powerful virtues in their operation, similar to the operation of the Idea itself. We know, therefore, that the position and the figure of the celestial bodies are the cause of every noble virtue found in the lower species.

### BOOK I. 33

[An individual] <note>original: "diuiduum", the suffix of "individuum"</note> when it begins to exist under a specific **horoscope** <term>horoscopo; the configuration of the heavens at the moment of birth or beginning</term> and celestial **constellation**, acquires along with its existence a certain wonderful power of acting and of being acted upon <note>original: "patiendi"; in classical philosophy, this refers to the capacity of a thing to be influenced or changed by an external force</note>. This is a marvelous thing, even beyond the power it possesses from its own species. This occurs both through the influence of the heavens and through the **obedience of matter** toward the **Soul of the World** <term>Anima Mundi; a philosophical concept suggesting the universe itself is a living being with a soul that connects all physical matter</term>. This obedience is exactly like the way our own bodies obey our souls. For we feel within ourselves that whatever form we imagine, our body is moved toward it—whether with pleasure, or by shuddering in horror, or by fleeing. In the same way, the celestial souls [the stars and planets] frequently conceive of various things, and then physical matter is moved through obedience toward those conceptions.

Thus, in nature, many wonders appear resulting from the "imagination" of these higher motions. In this way, not only natural objects but even artificial things sometimes receive diverse **virtues** <term>virtutes; the inherent magical, medicinal, or spiritual powers of an object</term>, especially if the soul of the craftsman is striving toward the same goal. This is why **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a highly influential Persian philosopher and physician</note> says that whatever happens here on Earth must first exist in the motions and conceptions of the stars and the celestial spheres. In this way, various effects, inclinations, and behaviors are produced in things. This happens not only because matter is arranged in different ways (as many people believe), but because of the varied celestial influence and a unique form—not a difference of species, but one that is particular and individual to that thing.

The degrees of these powers are variously distributed by the **First Cause** of all things, God. While He remains unchanging, He distributes to each individual as He wills. In this work, He is assisted by **secondary causes**—both angelic and celestial—which act upon corporeal matter and other...

### From where the hidden virtues of things proceed
#### Chapter 13.

It is known to all that a certain power exists in the **Magnet** by which it attracts iron, and that the **Diamond** <note>original: "Adamas"</note> by its presence removes the power of the magnet. In the same way, **amber** <note>original: "electrum"</note> and the **balas ruby** <note>original: "balagius"; a type of pink or red spinel</note>, when rubbed and warmed, draw straws toward them. The stone **Asbestos**, once set on fire, is never, or only with great difficulty, extinguished. The **carbuncle** <note>a deep red gemstone, such as a garnet or ruby</note> shines in the darkness. The **eaglestone** <note>original: "ætites"; a stone supposedly found in eagles' nests</note>, when placed above, strengthens the offspring of women and plants, but when placed below, it draws them forth. **Jasper** stops blood; the **remora** <note>original: "echeneis"; a small fish anciently believed to be able to stop ships by clinging to them</note> halts a ship; **rhubarb** expels bile <note>original: "choleram"; referring to yellow bile, one of the four bodily humors</note>.

The **chameleon's liver**, if burnt upon the highest roof tiles, calls forth rain and thunder. The **Heliotrope** stone <note>also known as bloodstone</note> dazzles the sight and renders its wearer invisible. The **ligurius** stone <note>a legendary stone supposedly formed from lynx urine</note> removes illusions from the eyes. The smoke of the **liparis** stone calls forth all manner of beasts. The **synochitis** stone brings forth the shades of the underworld <note>original: "umbras inferorum"</note>. The **anachitis** makes the images of the gods <note>original: "diuorum"</note> appear. The **ennectis**, when placed under those who are dreaming, provides oracles. There is an **Ethiopian herb** by which they say ponds are dried up and all closed things are opened. We also read that the **latace** herb is given by the Kings of the Persians to their Ambassadors so that, wherever they might go, they would abound in a plenty of all things. There is...

...and the Spartan or Scythian herb <note>likely licorice or *Glycyrrhiza*, traditionally used by Scythian warriors to stave off hunger</note>, which, when tasted or even just held in the mouth, they say allows the Scythians to endure hunger and thirst for twelve days. **Apuleius** <note>a 2nd-century Roman philosopher and author of *The Golden Ass*</note> also claims that he was taught by a divinity that there are many kinds of herbs and stones by which men can even grant themselves perpetual life. However, he says it is not divinely permitted for humans to possess this knowledge; for even now, living such short lives, they greedily pursue evil and dare every crime. If they were to receive a long life, they would not even spare the gods themselves.

Truly, from where these **virtues** <term>virtutes; the inherent, miraculous powers of objects</term> originate, no one among those who have published massive volumes on the properties of things has fully revealed—not **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of Hermeticism</note>, nor **Bocchus**, nor **Aaron**, nor **Orpheus**, nor **Theophrastus**, nor **Thabit** <note>Thabit ibn Qurra, a 9th-century Sabian astronomer and mathematician</note>, nor **Zenothemis**, nor **Zoroaster**, nor **Evax** <note>a legendary King of the Arabs said to have written a book on gems</note>, nor **Dioscorides**, nor **Isaac the Jew**, nor **Zacharias of Babylon**, nor **Albertus** <note>Albertus Magnus, the medieval scholastic known for his work on minerals and plants</note>, nor **Arnoldus** <note>Arnold of Villanova, a famous physician and alchemist</note>. And yet all these have confessed, as **Zacharias** writes to **Mithridates**, that a great power and human fates themselves reside within the virtues of stones and herbs.

Therefore, a deeper investigation is required to find from where these come. **Alexander the Peripatetic** <note>Alexander of Aphrodisias, an influential commentator on Aristotle</note>, not departing from his reliance on the senses and physical qualities, is of the opinion that these arise from the elements and their specific qualities. This might perhaps be considered true, except that those elemental qualities (hot, cold, wet, dry) are shared across many things, whereas the operations of stones are many and varied, agreeing neither in species nor in genus. Therefore, the **Academics** <note>the followers of the Platonic school</note> with their master **Plato**, attribute these virtues to the **Ideas** <term>ideis; the perfect, eternal archetypes of all things</term> which give things their stability. **Avicenna**, however, refers them to the **Intelligences** <note>the angelic or spiritual beings that govern the celestial spheres</note>, and **Hermes**...

->## ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY.<-

...to the stars, while **Albertus** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval scholar who argued that the "specific form" of a thing gave it its unique powers</note> attributes them to the "specific forms" of things, tracing these operations back to their essence. And although these authors seem to oppose one another, none of them, if understood correctly, departs from the truth, since all their sayings agree on the same effect in many respects.

For **God**, first of all—the end and origin of all virtues—provides the seal of **Ideas** <term>ideae; the perfect, eternal patterns of all things held in the Divine Mind</term> to His ministering **Intelligences** <note>spiritual beings or angels that govern the celestial spheres</note>. These, like faithful executors, entrust each thing committed to them with an "ideal power," sealing it into the heavens and the stars. These stars act as instruments, meanwhile disposing matter to receive those forms which reside in the divine majesty (as **Plato** says in the *Timaeus* <note>Plato’s influential dialogue regarding the creation of the universe and the soul</note>) to be drawn down through the stars.

The "Giver of Forms" <note>original: *dator formarum*, a philosophical term for the source of life and essence</note> distributes these forms through the ministry of the **Intelligences**, whom He has established as rulers and guardians over His works. To them, the faculty over the things committed to their charge is entrusted, so that every virtue of stones, herbs, metals, and all other things comes from those presiding Intelligences. Therefore, form and virtue proceed first from the **Ideas**, then from the presiding and ruling **Intelligences**, afterward from the disposing aspects of the heavens, and further from the disposed temperaments <note>original: *complexionibus*; the specific balance of the four elements in an object</note> of the elements, corresponding to the influences of the heavens by which the elements themselves are arranged.

Therefore, operations of this kind exist in these inferior things <note>the earthly realm</note> through "expressed forms," but in the heavens through "disposing virtues," in the Intelligences through "mediating reasons," and in the **Archetype** <term>Archetypus; the original divine model of the universe</term> through **Ideas** and exemplary forms, all of which...

### BOOK I.

*...and it is necessary that the effects and virtues of each thing agree in their execution.* Therefore, there is a virtue and a wonderful operation in every *herb* and *stone*, but it is even greater in a *star*. Beyond this, each thing acquires many qualities from the presiding **Intelligences** <note>spiritual beings or angels traditionally believed to govern the movements of the planets and stars</note>; and most of all from the **Supreme Cause** <note>God, the ultimate source of all existence</note>, to whom all things correspond in a mutual and perfected way. They harmonize in their own musical concord, as if in certain hymns, always praising that highest **Craftsman** <term>opifex; a term for God as the architect of the universe</term>, just as the holy youths in the **Chaldean furnace** <note>A reference to the Biblical story of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego (Daniel 3), who were cast into a furnace by King Nebuchadnezzar but were miraculously unharmed and sang a song of praise</note> invite all things to do, singing:

> *Bless the Lord, all things that grow in the earth, and all things that move in the waters, all birds of the air, beasts and cattle, together with the children of men.* <note>original: "Benedicite dominum uniuersa germinātia in terra, & omnia quæ mouentur in aquis, omnes uolucres cœli, bestiæ, & pecora simul cum filijs hominum."</note>

Therefore, there is no other cause for the necessity of these effects than the connection of all things with the **First Cause**, and their correspondence to those **Divine Exemplars** and **Eternal Ideas**. From these, every single thing holds a specific and determined place in the **Archetype** <term>the original model or "world-blueprint" existing in the Divine Mind</term>, from which it draws its life and origin. Every virtue of herbs, stones, metals, animals, words, and prayers—and of all things that exist—is implanted by **God**.

Although He usually works through the **Intelligences** and the heavens upon these lower things, sometimes **God** performs these works immediately by Himself, bypassing these intermediaries or suspending their ministry. Such works are then called **miracles**. For while the **Secondary Causes** <note>natural agents like stars or elements that act as intermediaries</note>—which **Plato** and others call "ministers"—act by necessity according to the command and order of the **First Cause**, and necessarily produce their effects...

### ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY

...lead, nonetheless God sometimes, according to His pleasure, so releases or suspends those things, that they completely depart from the necessity of that rule and order. And these are the greatest **miracles** of God: thus the fire in the **Chaldean furnace** <note>A reference to the Biblical story of Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego, who were miraculously protected from fire</note> did not burn the youths; thus the sun, at the command of **Joshua**, retreated from its course for the space of one day; thus at the petition of **Hezekiah**, it moved backward ten lines—or hours <note>Referring to the shadow moving backward on a sundial as a sign from God (2 Kings 20)</note>; thus while **Christ** suffered, the sun endured an eclipse during a full moon <note>In nature, a solar eclipse can only occur during a new moon; its occurrence during a full moon at the Crucifixion was considered a supernatural event</note>. And the reasons for these operations can be investigated or reached by no discourse of reason, no **Magic**, nor any science—however occult or profound—but must be learned and sought out from divine oracles alone.

->On the **Spirit of the World**, what it is, and that it is the<-
->bond of occult virtues<-
->Chapter 14.<-

**Democritus**, however, and **Orpheus**, and many of the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who taught that the universe was governed by mathematical and spiritual harmony</note>, having most diligently examined the powers of the heavens and the natures of things below, said that all things are full of gods. Nor was this without reason, since there is no thing with such outstanding powers that, if widowed of divine aid, it would be content with its own nature. But they called the divine virtues diffused through things "gods," which **Zoroaster** <note>The ancient Persian prophet often cited in Renaissance magic as a source of primordial wisdom</note> called "divine attractions" <note>original: *illices*</note>; **Synesius** <note>A 4th-century philosopher and bishop known for his works on dreams and providence</note> called them "symbolic enticements," others called them "lives," and still others called them "souls," and they said that the virtues of things depend on these.

<summary>Agrippa argues that while nature follows a set order, God can perform miracles that defy all magical or scientific explanation. He then introduces the concept of the Spirit of the World, explaining that ancient philosophers viewed all of nature as being animated by divine "souls" or "lives."</summary>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...they said this was possible, because it is characteristic of the soul alone to be extended from one material thing into other things upon which it acts, just as a human being extends the intellect toward things that can be understood, and the imagination toward things that can be imagined. And this is what they meant when they said the soul of one being goes out and enters into another, enchanting it and hindering its operations, just as a **Diamond** <term>Adamas: In ancient and medieval lore, the diamond was believed to have the power to neutralize magnets</term> prevents a **Magnet** from attracting iron. Since, however, the soul is the primary mover and—as they say—is spontaneous and self-moving, while the body, or matter, is of itself ineffective for movement and far degenerate from the soul itself, they therefore say there is a need for a more excellent medium; namely, something that is almost not a body, but almost already a soul; or almost not a soul, and almost already a body, by which the soul is connected to the body.

They imagine such a medium to be the **spirit of the world**: namely, that which we call the **fifth essence** <term>quintessence: a celestial substance distinct from the four earthly elements (earth, water, air, fire), acting as a bridge between the spiritual and physical realms</term>. It is so named because it does not consist of the four elements, but exists as a certain fifth thing subsisting above or beyond them. Therefore, such a spirit is necessarily required as a medium by which celestial souls may dwell within a denser body and bestow wonderful gifts. This spirit is of such a form in the body of the world as our own spirit is in the human body: for just as the powers of our souls are applied to our limbs through the spirit, so the power of the soul of the world is spread through all things by the fifth essence. Indeed, nothing is found in the whole world that lacks a spark of its power. However, it is infused more, and most greatly, into those things that have drawn in the most of this spirit...

...they say: moreover, it is drawn in through the rays of the stars, insofar as things render themselves conformable to them. Therefore, through this spirit, every **hidden property** <term>occulta proprietas: the secret qualities of things—like magnetism—that cannot be explained by the physical mixture of the four elements</term> is propagated into herbs, stones, metals, and living creatures, by way of the sun, the moon, the planets, and through the stars that are higher than the planets. 

Moreover, this spirit can be more beneficial to us if someone knows how to separate it most from the other elements, or at least to make frequent use of those things which abound most in this spirit. For indeed, those things in which that spirit is less immersed in the body and less restrained by matter act more powerfully and more perfectly, and also more readily generate their own likeness. For every generative and seminal power is within it; for this reason, the **Alchemists** strive to separate that spirit from gold and silver. Once it has been rightly segregated and extracted, if they then apply it to any matter of the same kind—that is, to any of the metals—they immediately produce gold or silver. And we ourselves know how to do that, and have sometimes seen it, but we could not manufacture more gold than the weight of that gold from which we extracted the spirit. For since that spirit is an extended and not an intense form <note>original: "forma extensa & non intensa." In scholastic philosophy, this suggests the spirit's power is spread out through its volume rather than being concentrated in strength. Agrippa argues that without a way to "intensify" it, the spirit can only transform an amount of matter equal to its own original source.</note>, it cannot transform an imperfect body into a perfect one beyond its own measure; however, I do not deny that this can be done.

## How we ought to investigate and test the virtues of things, by a path taken from similarity.
### Chapter 15.

<meta>catchword: It is evident</meta>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "spirit of the world," shifting from the Alchemical extraction of this spirit to the general principle of how occult properties influence their surroundings. It follows the previous page’s argument that this spirit acts as the vehicle for celestial power.</meta>

### BOOK I.

It is clear, therefore, that in things there are **hidden properties** <term>occulta proprietas: secret qualities—like magnetism or medicinal effects—that cannot be explained by the physical mixture of the four elements (hot, cold, moist, dry)</term>, implanted not from an elemental nature, but from heaven. These are hidden from our senses and, in the end, are barely known even to our reason. Indeed, they proceed from the life and **spirit of the world** <term>spiritus mundi: a subtle, invisible substance that permeates the universe, acting as a bridge between the physical and the spiritual</term> through the very rays of the stars. We cannot investigate these properties in any way other than through experience and conjecture.

For this reason, O seeker, you who desire to work in this study, you must consider that every single thing moves and turns toward its own likeness and inclines toward itself according to all its power. This happens both in its property—that is, in its **occult virtue** <note>original: "uirtute occulta"</note>—and in its quality—that is, its **elemental virtue** <note>original: "uirtute elementali"</note>. Sometimes this occurs even in its very substance, as we see with salt; for whatever remains with salt for a long time becomes salt. For every agent, once it begins to act, does not move toward something lower than itself, but in some way, as far as possible, moves toward its own equal and like. 

We see this clearly in sentient animals: within them, the **nutritive virtue** <note>original: "uirtus nutritiua," the natural biological power that manages growth and sustenance</note> does not transmute food into a herb or plant, but converts it into sentient flesh. Therefore, in those things where there is an excess of some quality or property—such as heat, cold, **boldness** <note>original: "audacia"</note>, fear, sadness, anger, love, hatred, or any other **passion** <term>passio: an internal state or emotion that acts upon the soul or body</term> or virtue—whether it is present in them by nature, or sometimes even by art or chance (such as boldness in a prostitute), these things most powerfully move and provoke one toward that same quality, passion, or virtue. Thus fire moves toward fire, and water moves toward water, and the bold man moves toward bold[ness]...

<meta>catchword: [bold]ness.</meta>

<summary>Agrippa explains that occult properties originate from the heavens via the "spirit of the world" and operate on the principle of "like attracts like." He argues that things possessing an excess of a certain quality—whether physical like heat or emotional like boldness—will naturally influence their surroundings to mirror that quality.</summary>

<keywords>occult properties, spirit of the world, law of likeness, celestial influence, elemental virtue, nutritive virtue, passions, boldness, sympathy</keywords>

...ledge. And it is well known among physicians that the brain aids the brain, and the lung aids the lung. Thus they say that a frog’s right eye [aids] the right [eye], and the left [aids] the left, when hung from the neck in a cloth of natural color, they cure **eye inflammations** <note>original: "lippitudines," referring to bleary, watery, or inflamed eyes</note>; they say the same of crab eyes. Thus the feet of a **turtle** <note>original: "testudinis," which can mean a turtle or a tortoise</note> benefit those suffering from **gout** <term>podagricis: individuals afflicted with gout, a painful inflammatory arthritis</term>, if they are bound on so that foot matches foot and hand matches hand—the right [turtle foot] hung to the right [human foot], and the left to the left. 

In this way they say that every sterile animal induces sterility, and especially parts from such an animal such as the testicles, the **womb** <note>original: "matrix"</note>, or the urine. Thus they say a woman will not conceive if she takes some of a mule's urine every month, or something steeped in it. Therefore, if we wish to work toward a certain property or virtue, we should seek out animals or other things in which such a property exists most excellently, and from these, we should take for ourselves the part in which such a property or virtue flourishes most strongly. 

For instance, if we ever wish to promote love, we should seek some animal that is most loving, such as the dove, the turtledove, the sparrow, the swallow, or the **wagtail** <note>original: "motacilla," a bird traditionally associated with restless movement and love charms</note>. From these we should take the members or parts in which the **lustful appetite** <term>uenereus appetitus: the desire for love and physical intimacy, named after the goddess Venus</term> flourishes most: which are the heart, the testicles, the womb, the **penis** <note>original: "priapus"</note>, the seed, or the menstrual fluid. And let this be done at that time when these animals are most possessed by and intent upon such an affection; for then they strongly provoke and instill love. In a similar way, to increase **boldness** <note>original: "audaciam"</note>, let us seek the lion or the rooster, and from these let us take the heart, the eyes, or the forehead. As Michael Psellus the Platonist <note>Michael Psellus (c. 1017–1078) was a Byzantine polymath and philosopher known for his works on demonology and Neoplatonism.</note> says, dogs, ravens, and roosters contribute to wakefulness; likewise do the nightingale, the bat, and the owl, and from these especially the head, heart, and eyes. The dried head of a bat does the same when tied to the right arm of one who is awake. For if it is placed upon one who is sleeping, it is reported that they will not wake up until it has been removed. In the same way, the frog and the horned owl <note>original: "bubo," typically the eagle-owl, often associated in folklore with death or secrets.</note> produce talkativeness, and from these especially the tongue and the heart: thus the tongue of a water frog placed under the head makes one speak in their dreams, and the heart of a horned owl placed upon the left breast of a sleeping woman is said to cause her to utter all her secrets. In the same way, all long-lived animals contribute to a long life, and whatever things possess a restorative power within themselves contribute to the renewal of our bodies and the restoration of youth; this is something physicians have often shown themselves to know, as is manifest regarding the viper and serpents. And it is well known that stags renew their old age by eating serpents. In the same way, the Phoenix is renewed by the funeral pyre which it builds for itself, and a similar power exists in the Pelican: if its right foot is placed under warm dung, after three months a Pelican is regenerated from it. ### How the workings of various virtues are transferred from one thing to another, and how they are mutually shared
### Chapter 16.

You should know that the power of natural things is so great that they not only affect all things close to them with their own virtue, but beyond this, they infuse into those things a similar power, through which they also may affect others with this same virtue: Just as we see in the magnet <note>original: "magnete," the lodestone or natural magnet.</note>: for this stone not only attracts iron rings, but also infuses its power into the rings themselves, so that they may do the same thing, just as Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280) was a Dominican friar and polymath who wrote extensively on the natural sciences and "secrets" of minerals and animals.</note> reports having seen. In this same way, it is said that a public prostitute in whom there is boldness and a total lack of shame <note>original: "impudentia exterminata," literally a shamelessness that has "driven out" all restraint.</note> affects all things close to her with this same quality, which then pass it on to others. Therefore they say that if anyone wears the garment or shirt of a prostitute, or keeps with them a mirror in which she has gazed daily, they will be rendered bold, fearless, shameless, and lustful. In a similar way, they say a cloth that has been at a funeral conceives from this a certain quality of sadness and a saturnine influence <note>original: "saturnalem," referring to the planet Saturn, which in traditional astrology governs melancholy, mourning, and death.</note>; and the rope of a hanged man <note>original: "laqueum suspendiosi." Such items were frequently sought after in historical folk magic as powerful talismans.</note> is likewise [said] to have some marvelous

### How the virtues of things are to be investigated and tested through strife and friendship — Chapter 17.

It now remains to see that all things have friendship and enmity <note>The concepts of "sympathy" and "antipathy" were central to Renaissance magic: the idea that certain things naturally attract or repel each other.</note> among themselves; and every thing has something to be feared, a horrible enemy and destroyer; on the contrary, [it has] something exulting, gladdening, and comforting. Thus in the elements, fire is opposed to water, and air to earth; otherwise, they agree among themselves. Again, in the heavens, Mercury, Jupiter, the Sun, and the Moon are friends to **Saturn**; his enemies are **Mars** and **Venus**. To **Jupiter**, all planets are friends except **Mars**. In the same way, all hold **Mars** in hatred except **Venus**. **Jupiter** and **Venus** love the **Sun**; **Mars**, **Mercury**, and the **Moon** are hostile to it. All love **Venus** except **Saturn**. The friends of **Mercury** are **Jupiter**,

...[To Mars, only Venus is a friend.] To **Venus**, the friends are Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn; her enemies are the Sun, the Moon, and Mars. To the **Moon**, however, the friends are Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn; her enemies are Mars and Mercury. There is another kind of enmity among the stars, namely when they have opposite "houses" <note>In astrology, a "house" or domicile is the zodiac sign over which a planet has authority. Enmity occurs when planets rule signs opposite one another, such as Saturn (ruling Capricorn) being opposite the Moon (ruling Cancer).</note>, such as Saturn to the luminaries <note>The "luminaries" are the Sun and the Moon.</note>, Jupiter to Mercury, and Mars to Venus. And a stronger enmity exists between those whose exaltations <note>An "exaltation" is a specific sign in the zodiac where a planet's influence is at its most powerful.</note> are opposite: such as Saturn and the Sun, Jupiter and Mars, and Venus and Mercury. 

However, the strongest friendship is between those who agree in nature, quality, substance, and power, such as Mars with the Sun, and Venus with the Moon, and similarly Jupiter with Venus. There is also a friendship between those where the exaltation of one is in the house of the other: such as Saturn with Venus, Jupiter with the Moon, Mars with Saturn, the Sun with Mars, Venus with Jupiter, and the Moon with Venus. 

Whatever kind these friendships and enmities of the higher beings <note>referring to the planets</note> may be, such are the inclinations of the things subject to them here in these lower realms. Therefore, these states of friendship and enmity are nothing other than certain inclinations of things toward one another: desiring a particular thing if it is absent, moving toward it unless hindered, and finding rest once it is obtained; while conversely fleeing from its contrary, dreading its approach, and finding no rest in its presence. Led by this opinion, Heraclitus <note>Heraclitus of Ephesus (c. 535–475 BC), a philosopher famous for the idea that "strife is the father of all things," meaning that the tension between opposites creates the world.</note> declared that all things come to be through strife and friendship. 

Indeed, these inclinations of friendship exist in plants and minerals: such as the attractive power the Magnet <note>original: "Magnes," the lodestone or magnetic iron ore.</note> has for iron, and the Emerald for riches and grace; the Jasper for childbirth, and the Agate for elo-

# BOOK I.

...ndship. <note>The text begins mid-word, completing "amicitia" from the previous page.</note> In a similar way, **Naphtha** <term>Naphtha: a highly flammable petroleum distillate known in antiquity for its "attraction" to fire.</term> draws fire to itself and leaps toward it from wherever it is seen. Likewise, the root of the **Aproxis** herb draws fire from a distance just like Naphtha. There is also a similar inclination between the **male and female palm trees**; when the branch of one touches the branch of the other, they wrap themselves in a mutual embrace. The female does not bear fruit without the male, and a solitary **almond tree** is less fertile. **Vines** love the **Elm** and the **Maple** <note>original: "Oppium," referring to the *Acer campestre*, which was traditionally used as a support for grapevines.</note>, and the **Olive** and **Myrtle** share a mutual affection; the same is true for the Olive and the **Fig**. 

Among animals, however, there is friendship between the **Blackbird** and the **Thrush**, the **Crow** and the **Heron**, and between **Peacocks** and **Doves**, **Turtle-doves** and **Parrots**. On this subject, the poet **Sappho** writes to Phaon:

> "White doves are often joined with various mates,
> And the black turtle-dove is loved by the green bird." <note>original: "Et uarijs albæ iunguntur sæpe columbæ, / Et niger à uiridi turtur amatur aue."</note>

Again, the **Pilot-fish** <note>original: "Musculus." In ancient natural history, this small fish was believed to lead whales away from danger.</note> and the **Whale** are friends. Nor is there friendship only among animals themselves, but also with other things, such as metals, stones, and plants. For instance, the **Cat** delights in the herb **Catnip** <note>original: "Nepita."</note>; it is said that by rubbing against it, cats may conceive and fulfill the lack of a male. It is also told that **mares in Cappadocia** expose themselves to the breeze and, from its breath and drawing-in, they conceive. Likewise **Frogs**, **Toads**, **Serpents**, and all kinds of venomous creeping things delight in the plant called **Laughing Parsley** <note>original: "Apium risus," likely the Celery-leaved Buttercup (*Ranunculus sceleratus*). It was said to cause a "sardonic grin"—a fatal contraction of the facial muscles—in those who ate it.</note>, of which doctors say that if anyone eats it, they die laughing. Conversely, when the **tortoise** is hunted by a serpent, it eats **oregano** <note>original: "origanum."</note> and is strengthened by it.

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural sympathies by exploring how animals possess an innate knowledge of medicine, often providing humans with the blueprints for our own remedies.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>

<header>ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY</header>

The **Stork** <note>original: "Ciconia"</note>, when it has fed upon serpents, seeks health for itself in **oregano**. And the **Weasel**, when it is about to fight against the **Basilisk** <note>original: "Regulum," literally "little king," a mythical serpent whose breath or gaze was thought to be lethal.</note>, eats **rue**. From this we recognize that oregano and rue possess a power against poisons. In this way, there is a natural skill and an innate medicinal art in certain animals. For the **Toad** <note>original: "Buſſo"</note>, when it is wounded by the bite or poison of another creature, is accustomed to go to rue or sage and rub the site of the wound, and thus free itself from the danger of the poison. 

In the same way, humans have been taught a great many remedies for diseases and the hidden powers of things by the beasts. Thus, **Swallows** showed **Celandine** <term>Celandine: its name in Greek, *chelidonion*, is derived from the word for swallow; it was believed swallows used its sap to heal their chicks' eyes.</term> to be a herb healthy for the sight, as they heal the eyes of their chicks with it. The **Magpie**, when it is sick, places a **laurel leaf** into its nest and so recovers. Similarly, **Wood-pigeons, Jackdaws, Partridges, and Blackbirds** purge their annual sluggishness with laurel leaves; with these same leaves, **Crows** extinguish the poison of the **Chameleon**. 

The **Lion**, if he suffers from a fever, recovers by devouring a **monkey**. The **Hoopoe**, having been harmed by eating grapes, heals itself with **Maidenhair fern** <note>original: "Adianto"</note>. Likewise, **Deer** taught that the herb **Dittany** <term>Dittany: a plant from Crete famous in antiquity for causing arrows to fall out of wounds.</term> is useful for extracting arrows; for when they are struck by a missile, they eject it by feeding on this herb. The **Goats of Crete** do the same. Furthermore, female deer, shortly before giving birth, purge themselves with a certain herb called **Hartwort** <note>original: "Seſelis"</note>. Similarly, those bitten by **venomous spiders** <note>original: "Phalangia"</note> seek health by eating **crabs**. **Pigs**, when harmed by serpents, heal themselves with the same diet. And **Crows**, when they feel themselves poisoned by **Gallic poison** <term>Gallic poison: likely Aconite or Monkshood, which was used by ancient Gauls to tip their arrows.</term>, seek out the **Oak**, or as others say, the **Crow-herb** <note>original: "Coruatiam"</note>, as a remedy. **Elephants**, after eating a **Chameleon**...

<header>BOOK I.</header>

having devoured a **Chameleon** <note>original: "maleonte," completing the word "chamaeleonte" from the previous page.</note>, they help themselves with **Wild Olive** <note>original: "Oleastro"</note>. **Bears**, when harmed by **Mandrake**, escape by eating **ants**. **Geese**, **Ducks**, and other aquatic birds heal themselves with the herb **Sideritis** <term>Sideritis: also known as Ironwort, it was believed to heal wounds caused by iron weapons.</term>. **Pigeons**, **Turtledoves**, and **Hens** use the herb **Helxine** <note>original: "Helxine," likely Pellitory-of-the-wall.</note>. **Cranes** heal themselves with a rush, and **Panthers** protect themselves against the poison of **monkshood** <note>original: "aconitum"</note> by using human dung. **Wild Boars** use **ivy**, and **Hinds** <note>female deer</note> use the herb **Cinnara** <note>original: "Cinnarae," likely the artichoke or wild thistle.</note>.

### On the Inclinations of Enmities Chapter 18.

Conversely, there are the inclinations of enmities. These inclinations are like a natural hatred, or like anger, indignation, and a certain commanding contrariety, so that a thing flees from its opposite, or drives it away as if fleeing from its presence. Such inclinations are found in **Rhubarb** against **yellow bile** <note>original: "choleram," one of the four humors associated with heat and anger.</note>; in **Theriac** <term>Theriac: a famous ancient "universal" medicinal compound used as an antidote to all poisons.</term> against poison; in **Sapphire** against **carbuncles** <note>original: "anthracem," a type of burning skin ulcer or sore.</note>, feverish heats, and ailments of the eyes; in **Amethyst** against **drunkenness**; in **Jasper** against the **flow of blood** and harmful apparitions. **Emerald** and **Agnus Castus** <term>Agnus castus: also known as the "Chaste Tree," it was historically used by monks and others to suppress sexual desire.</term> have an inclination against **lust**; **Agate** against poisons; **Peony** against the **falling sickness** <term>falling sickness: an archaic name for epilepsy.</term>; **Coral** against the illusions of **black bile** <note>original: "atrae bilis," referring to melancholy.</note> and pains of the stomach; and **Topaz** against spiritual heats, such as **greed**, **lechery**, and all excesses of love. 

There is a similar inclination in **ants** toward the herb **Oregano**, and toward the wing of a **Bat**, and the heart of a **Hoopoe**, from whose presence they flee. **Oregano** is also contrary to **sun-fleers** <term>sun-fleers: original "solifugis," likely referring to venomous spiders or nocturnal insects believed to be poisonous.</term> and the **Salaman[dra]**...

the **Vine** <note>original: "vitis," though OCR shows "dris," likely a fragment of the word from the previous page</note> resists and disagrees with **cabbage** <note>original: "brassica"</note> with such a stubborn hatred that they destroy one another. Likewise, **cucumbers** hate **oil** so much that they curl themselves into a hook to avoid touching it. It is also said that **Raven’s gall** <note>original: "Fel corui"</note> puts to flight and terrifies humans from any place where it has been hidden alongside certain other things. Similarly, the **Diamond** <note>original: "Adamas"</note> is at odds with the **Magnet**, so that when placed near it, it does not allow the iron to be attracted. Sheep flee from **wild parsley** <note>original: "Apium rauinum," likely a reference to *Ranunculus sceleratus* or Celery-leaved buttercup</note> as if it were deadly; and what is more wonderful, nature has painted the sign of this death in the livers of sheep, in which the shape of wild parsley appears naturally described. Similarly, **goats** so hate **basil** <note>original: "Ocimum"</note> that they consider nothing more pestilential to themselves. 

Again, among animals, **mice** and **weasels** <note>original: "mustellae"</note> disagree; for this reason, they say that if the brain of a weasel is added to the rennet <note>the curdled milk used in cheesemaking</note>, the resulting cheeses will never be touched by mice, nor will they rot with age. Thus, the **star-lizard** <term>Stellio: a spotted lizard or newt, traditionally believed to be a natural enemy of the scorpion.</term> is so contrary to **scorpions** that it brings fear to them even by sight, along with the numbness of a cold sweat; therefore, scorpions rot and die in oil containing this lizard, and that oil is used to heal those struck by scorpions. There is also an enmity between **scorpions** and **mice**, which is why a **mouse** applied to a scorpion’s sting is said to cure it. Enmity also exists between the **scorpion** and **venomous spiders** <note>original: "stalabores," likely referring to the *phalangia* or tarantula</note>, and between **asps** and **ichneumons** <term>Ichneumon: the Egyptian mongoose, famous in antiquity for its ability to slay snakes by coating itself in mud as armor.</term>. It is also reported that nothing is so opposed to **serpents** as **crabs**; and **pigs** that have been bitten heal themselves by eating them. Also, when the **Sun** is in the sign of **Cancer** <note>the Crab</note>, serpents are tormented. Furthermore, the **scorpion** and the **crocodile** kill each other, and if anyone touches a **crocodile** with a feather from an **ibis**, they will render it immobile,

The **bustard** <note>original: "otidis auis"</note> bird flies away at the sight of a horse, and the **stag** <note>original: "ceruus"</note> flees at the sight of a **ram**; it also flees at the sight of a **viper**. The **elephant** <note>original: "elephas"</note> is terrified by the grunting of a **pig**, and **lions** <note>original: "leones"</note> are afraid at the sight of a **rooster**. There is also an enmity <note>original: "inimicitia"</note> between **foxes**, **swans**, **bulls**, and **crows**. Among birds, a perpetual war is also waged between **crows** and **owls**, the **kite** and the **raven**, the **woodpecker** <note>original: "pitalles," likely the *pipo* or *picus*</note> and **turtle-doves**, the **bicuithus** and the **sea-bream** <note>original: "bicuithus & pagrus"; these are likely fish names from Pliny mistakenly grouped here or indicating a cross-species rivalry</note>, the **greenfinch** <note>original: "Clorius"</note> and the **turtle-dove**, **vultures** <note>original: "Aegepij"</note> and **eagles**, and **stags** and **dragons** <note>original: "dracones," often referring to large serpents in this context</note>. Among aquatic creatures there are also enemies: **dolphins** and **whales**, the **mullet** <note>original: "cestris"</note> and the **sea-bass** <note>original: "lupus"</note>, and the **moray eel** and the **conger eel**. Likewise, the **lobster** <note>original: "locusta"</note> is so terrified of the **octopus** <note>original: "polypum"</note> that if it sees one nearby, it dies instantly. **Conger eels** tear at the lobster, and in turn, they tear at the octopus. The **hyena** is said to be in such fear of the **panther** that it does not even attempt to resist, and the panther can touch any part of its hide. It is said that if the skins of both are hung opposite each other, the hair of the panther's skin will fall out. **Horapollo** <note>original: "Orus Apollo," a 5th-century author of a treatise on Egyptian hieroglyphs</note> says in his *Hieroglyphics* that if someone girded with a hyena’s skin is carried into the midst of enemies, he will be harmed by no one and will pass through fearlessly. Similarly, the **lamb** <note>original: "Agnus"</note> has a natural enmity and suffers harm from the **wolf**; it shudders, flees from it, and fears it. They also say that if the tail, skin, or head of a wolf is hung above a manger, the sheep become despondent and will not eat anything due to excessive fear. And **Pliny** reports that the **merlin** <note>original: "Esalon"</note>, a small bird that breaks the eggs of the crow (whose chicks are harassed by foxes), in turn plucks at the fox’s cubs and the fox itself; when the crows see this, they help the bird as if against a common enemy. The **linnet** <note>original: "Acanthis"</note>, a tiny bird living among thorns, hates **donkeys** because they devour the flowers of the thorns.

The **hedge-sparrow** <note>original: "Aegitus"; a small bird mentioned by Aristotle and Pliny as a rival to the donkey</note>, truly a very small bird, is said to disagree so much with the **donkey** <note>original: "Asinus"</note> that it is certain their blood will not mix; furthermore, when the donkey brays, the sparrow's eggs and chicks perish from the shock. The **olive tree** also is said to disagree with a **harlot** <note>original: "meretrice"</note> to such an extent that if it were planted by one, it is said to either remain forever fruitless or to wither away entirely. A **lion** fears nothing more than burning torches, and it is believed to be tamed by no other thing as much as these. The **wolf** fears neither iron nor spear, but a stone, for if a wound is made by a thrown stone, worms are bred in the wolf. The **horse** dreads the **camel** so much that it can neither bear to look upon its form nor endure its scent.

### Chapter 19.

FURTHERMORE, you must consider that the **virtues** <term>virtues: the inherent "powers" or spiritual qualities of a thing that allow it to act upon the world</term> of things reside in some according to their **species**, such as **boldness** and **courage** in the **Lion** and the **Rooster**, **timidity** in the **Hare** and the **Lamb**, **rapacity** or **voracity** in the **Wolf**, **guile** and **deceitfulness** in the **Fox**, **flattery** in the **Dog**, **avarice** in the **Raven** and the **Crow**, **pride** in the **Horse**, **anger** in the **Tiger** and the **Boar**, **sadness** and **melancholy** in the **Cat**, **lust** in the **Sparrow**, and so on for similar cases. For the greatest part of natural virtues accompanies the species. Truly, certain virtues reside in things according to the **individual**, just as there are certain men, who <note>The text cuts off here, continuing with the catchword "vehemently" on the next page</note>

...vehemently abhor the sight of a **Cat**, so much so that they cannot look upon one without the most profound horror; this horror is certainly not inherent to them as a trait of the human species, as is clearly manifest. And **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians and philosophers of the Islamic Golden Age</note> tells that in his own time there lived a man from whom all poisonous creatures would flee; if any creature happened to bite him, it would die, while he remained entirely unharmed. Furthermore, **Albertus** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a German Dominican friar and philosopher known for his work on the natural sciences</note> reports that in **Cologne** <note>original: "Agrippina Colonia"</note> he saw a girl who hunted **Spiders** for food, and taking great delight in that kind of nourishment, she was sustained by it. 

In the same way, there is boldness in a harlot and timidity in a thief. And for this reason, the Philosophers say that a specific individual who has never suffered from a disease can provide a cure for all illness. Thus they say that the bone of a dead man who never suffered from fevers, if suspended over a patient, frees them from **quartan fever** <term>quartan fever: a form of malaria where the fever paroxysm occurs every fourth day</term>. There are also many singular virtues in individuals infused by the celestial bodies, as we have demonstrated above.

And these virtues are not made clear by reason, but by experience.

### *On the virtues of things which are in them only during life, and those which remain in them even after death. Chapter 21.*

It will be necessary to know, moreover, that certain properties are in things only during life, and some also remain after death: thus the Remora <note>original: "Echeneis"; a legendary fish believed to be able to halt the progress of a ship by attaching to its hull</note> stops ships; and the Basilisk <note>original: "Regulus"; literally "little king," the Latin name for the Basilisk</note> and the Catoblepas <note>A mythical beast said to kill any who look upon it</note> kill with their sight while they are alive, but after death they do nothing <meta>catchword: such (tale)</meta>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 21, which discusses the duration of occult virtues. The previous page ended mid-sentence, noting that certain "proper virtues" function in specific ways while an animal is alive or shortly after death.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY</header>

...act in such a way: for instance, it is said that for colic <note>original: "torminibus"; refers to severe abdominal pain or griping of the bowels</note>, if a living duck is placed against the belly, the sickness passes into it and the duck dies. Archytas <note>Archytas of Tarentum (c. 435–360 BC) was a Pythagorean philosopher, mathematician, and friend of Plato</note> says something similar: if a heart recently taken from an animal, still warm and beating, is hung over someone suffering from a quartan fever <term>quartan fever: a form of malaria where the fever recurs every four days</term>, it cures them. Likewise, if one swallows the heart of a hoopoe, a swallow, a weasel, or a mole while it is still alive and beating, it grants them better memory, recollection, intellect, and the power of divination.

From this comes a general rule: whatever is gathered from animals—be it stones, limbs, or waste products like hair, dung, and hooves—should be taken while the animal is still alive and, if possible, in such a way that the creature survives the process. This is why they instruct that when you take the tongue of a frog, you should return the frog alive to the water. If you take the tooth or eye of a wolf, you must not kill the wolf; and the same applies to other such things. 

Thus Democritus <note>Democritus (c. 460–370 BC) was a Greek philosopher; in the Renaissance, many "secret" recipes were attributed to him</note> records that if one removes the tongue from a living sea frog <note>original: "Ranæ marinæ"; likely referring to the Anglerfish or "fishing-frog," known for its unique anatomy</note> so that no other part of the body is attached, and returns the frog to the sea, then places that tongue over the heart of a sleeping woman, she will answer truthfully to any question asked of her. Similarly, they claim that tying the eyes of frogs to a sick person before sunrise—provided the frogs are then released blind into the water—will drive away tertian fevers <term>tertian fever: a fever that recurs every three days, also a symptom of malaria</term>. It is also said that these same eyes, bound in deer skin along with the flesh of a nightingale, will cause wakefulness and banish sleep. In the same way, the spine of a stingray <note>original: "Pastinacæ radius"; the "radius" refers to the sharp, bone-like stinger found on the tail of a ray</note> tied to the navel is said to ensure an easy childbirth,

<page-num>57</page-num>
### BOOK I.

...to return it <note>original: "reddere"; completing the sentence from the previous page</note>, if it was taken while the creature was living, and then it must be released back into the sea. In this way, they say that the right eye of a serpent, when tied on as an amulet, is effective against eye-watering <term>epiphoras: an excessive flow of tears, often due to inflammation</term>, provided the serpent is released alive. There is also a certain fish, or perhaps a very large serpent, called the Myrus <note>original: "Myrus"; likely referring to the Moray eel (Muraena helena)</note>; if its eye is plucked out and bound to the forehead of a patient suffering from Ophthalmia <term>Ophthalmia: a general historical term for severe inflammation of the eye</term>, it is said to heal them. It is said that the eye grows back for the fish, but that the person who did not release the creature alive will instead lose the sight of one of their own eyes.

Likewise, the teeth of all serpents, when taken from them while living and hung over a person suffering from a quartan fever <term>quartan fever: a form of malaria where the fever paroxysms occur every fourth day</term>, are said to cure them. Similarly, if a tooth is taken from a living mole <term>Talpæ: the Latin term for a mole</term> and the animal is released, it heals toothaches. It is also said that dogs will not bark at those who carry the tail of a weasel <term>Mustellæ: a weasel</term> that has been set free. 

Democritus <note>Democritus (c. 460–370 BC) was a Greek philosopher; in the Renaissance, many "secret" recipes and magical properties were attributed to him in pseudo-epigraphical works</note> relates that the tongue of a chameleon, if snatched while it is still alive, is powerful for the outcome of legal trials. He also says the same tongue is beneficial to women in labor if kept near the house; however, one must take care that it is not brought *inside* the house, as that would be most dangerous.

There are also properties that remain even after death. Regarding these, the Platonists <note>Followers of Plato's philosophy, particularly the Neoplatonists who believed that material objects are governed by spiritual "Ideas" or archetypes</note> say that in those things where the <insert>idea of the matter</insert> <note>original: "idea materiæ"; refers to the celestial archetype or spiritual form that gives a thing its identity</note> is less submerged, that which is immortal does not cease to perform wonders even after the thing has died or passed away. Thus, in herbs and plants that have been uprooted and dried, that very virtue still thrives and works, having been once infused by the celestial Idea. This is why, just as the Eagle in its life surpasses and conquers all other birds, so even after its death, its feathers destroy and consume the feathers and plumage of other birds. For the same reason, the skin of a Lion [destroys] all...

And certain of these things do not act only through bodily contact, but also sometimes through vocal harmony: thus a drum <term>tympanum: a hand drum or tambourine</term> made from the skin of an Adib <note>original: "Adib"; an Arabic-derived term for a wolf</note>, that is, a wolf, causes a drum made from the skin of a lamb to fall silent. In a similar way, a drum made from the skin of a sea urchin <term>Ericij marini: literally "sea hedgehog," likely referring to a sea urchin or a rough-skinned fish</term> drives away all reptiles from a great distance, as far as the sound of it can be heard. It is also manifest that strings made from the intestines of a wolf, if they are stretched upon a lyre or a lute <term>cithara: a classical stringed instrument</term> alongside sheep-gut strings, can produce no harmony whatsoever.

### How lower things are subject to celestial bodies, and how the human body, and the very activities and manners of men, are distributed among the stars and signs. Chapter 22.

It is manifest that all lower things are subject to higher things, and in a certain way (as Proclus <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a prominent Neoplatonist philosopher who argued for a "chain of being" where every level of reality reflects the others</note> says) they exist within each other: namely, the highest things in the lowest, and the lowest in the highest. Thus, in heaven there are earthly things, but in the manner of a cause and in a celestial way; and on earth there are celestial things, but in a terrestrial way—that is, according to their effect. So we say there are certain beings here that are solar <note>solaria: things influenced by or belonging to the Sun</note>, and some that are lunar <note>lunaria: things influenced by or belonging to the Moon</note>, in which the Sun and Moon cause something of their own virtue. Wherefore things of this kind receive many operations and properties similar to the operations of the stars and figures under which they are placed: thus we recognize that solar things are related to the heart... <meta>The catchword "dis," at the bottom of the page completes the Latin word "cordis," meaning "of the heart."</meta>

...and of the head, because of Leo, the house of the Sun; and Aries, the exaltation of the Sun: likewise things of Mars <note>original: "Martialia"</note> are beneficial for the head and testicles because of Aries and Scorpio.
