## On rings and their compositions
### Chapter 47.

Rings also, which antiquity always held in high 
regard, when they are manufactured at the 
opportune time, in a similar way bring forth 
their virtue <note>original: "uirtutem"; in this context, it refers to an inherent spiritual power or efficacy</note> in us, to the extent that they affect 
the wearer and his spirit, making it joyful 
or sad, mild or terrible, bold or fearful, 
lovable or hateful; furthermore, they protect us against 
illnesses, poisons, enemies, evil demons <term>cacodæmones: from the Greek "kakos" (evil) and "daimon" (spirit); refers specifically to malevolent entities</term>, and against any other harmful things, or 
bind us to the same. The method of constructing 
rings of this kind is as follows: namely, 
when some fortunate star rises, and is

conforming to this star, and fix the small stone 
within it, with the herb or root placed underneath

images, names, and characters: besides these, 
do not omit the suffumigations <term>suffumigationes: the practice of burning specific substances as incense to attract planetary spirits</term>; but we shall

are to be treated. Thus we read in Philostratus <note>Flavius Philostratus, a 3rd-century author who wrote the "Life of Apollonius of Tyana"</note> that

Furthermore, we read in Plato about Gyges, the king of the Lydians, who possessed a ring of wonderful and unusual power <note>original: "uirtutis"; referring to an inherent magical efficacy</note>. When he turned the seal of this ring toward his palm, he was seen by no one, while he himself saw all things. Relying on the opportunity provided by this ring, he committed adultery with the queen, murdered the king his master, and slew whoever he thought stood in his way; no one could see him during these crimes, and finally, by the benefit of that ring, he became the king of Lydia <note>This famous parable appears in Book II of Plato’s *Republic* as a thought experiment regarding morality and invisibility</note>.

### On the powers of places, and which places correspond to each of the stars. Chapter 48.

There are also wonderful powers <note>original: "uirtutes"</note> belonging to places, which accompany them either because of the things situated there, or from the influences of the stars, or coming from elsewhere in some other way. For as Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist and author of *Natural History*</note> reports concerning the cuckoo: in whatever place a person first hears that bird, if the right foot is traced and that footprint is dug up, fleas will not be produced wherever that earth is scattered. They say the same of dust from a footprint...

...the cast-off skin <note>original: "exuvium"</note> of snakes, if collected and used to sprinkle bees, causes them to return to the hive. Similarly, dust in which a mule has rolled itself, if sprinkled on the body, is said to mitigate the heats of love. Furthermore, they say that dust in which a hawk has rolled itself—if it be bound in a reddish cloth—is a remedy for quartan fevers <note>A fever where paroxysms occur every fourth day, often associated with malaria</note>. In the same way, a pebble collected from a swallow's nest is said to immediately revive those suffering from the falling sickness <term>morbus comitialis: epilepsy, so named because an occurrence was considered a bad omen that would suspend the "comitia" or public assemblies in ancient Rome</term>; if worn as an amulet, it is said to protect them perpetually, especially if it was wrapped in the blood or heart of swallows. 

It is also told that if anyone with a cut vein and while fasting passes over the place where someone has recently fallen from the falling sickness, the disease will pass into him. Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the 'Natural History,' a primary source for ancient magical and medicinal lore</note> relates that to drive an iron nail into the spot where a person’s head first struck the ground when collapsing from the falling sickness will be a deliverance from that evil. Thus they also hand down that a herb grown on the head of a statue, if collected and bound to someone in a cloth of a garment with red thread <note>original: "lino ruffo"; red flax or linen thread</note>, immediately soothes a headache. Likewise, any herb collected from streams or rivers before sunrise—provided no one sees the person collecting it—wards off tertian fever <note>A fever recurring every third day</note> when bound to the left arm, so long as the sick person remains ignorant of what it is. 

Regarding the places which are appropriated to the stars: to Saturn correspond any places that are foul, dark, subterranean, religious, and funereal; cemeteries, tombs, and dwellings deserted by men, or those falling into decay from age; obscure, horrific, and solitary places; caves, caverns, and wells. Furthermore, fishponds, pools, marshes, and the like. To Jupiter are ascribed any privileged places,

By this logic, those about to perform works for the sake of love are accustomed to bury or hide the instruments of their art—whether rings, images, mirrors, or other things—for a certain time in a brothel, so that from that place they may be affected by a certain Venus-like <term>venereal: here meaning related to the goddess Venus and the power of attraction or lust</term> faculty. They believe this happens in no different way than things standing in a foul-smelling place become foul-smelling, and those in an aromatic place become aromatic and fragrant. Thus, in a solar work, one should look toward the east or south, or rather, always toward the solar body and light itself.

### Concerning light and colors, concerning wicks and
### lamps, and which colors are distributed
### to which stars, houses, and
### elements. Chapter 49.

Light is also a highly formal quality <note>In traditional philosophy, "formal" refers to the "form" or essential nature that gives a thing its identity</note>, a simple act of intelligence, and an image, first diffused from the divine mind into all things. But in God the Father himself, who is the Father of Lights, resides the first true light; then in His Son, an illuminating and overflowing splendor; and in the Holy Spirit, a burning radiance surpassing all understanding, even (as Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a late 5th-century theologian influential in medieval mysticism</note> says) that of the seraphim <note>The highest order of angels, traditionally associated with fire and light; the name is derived from the Hebrew word for "burning"</note>. Thus, when diffused among the angels, it becomes a shining intelligence and an overflowing joy beyond the limits of all reason, though in various degrees according to the [capacity of] the recipient...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hierarchy of light from Chapter 49. It connects the divine light mentioned on the previous page (attributed to the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit) to its manifestation in the physical and intellectual worlds.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>
<header>ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY</header>

it descends, having taken on the nature of wise intelligence <note>The OCR "pientis" completes the word "sapientis" from the previous page</note>; then it passes into the celestial spheres where there is an abundance of life, effective propagation, and even visible splendor. In fire, however, there is a certain natural vigor implanted from the heavens. In humans, finally, it becomes the bright discourse of reason and the knowledge of divine things, entirely rational: yet this is manifold, either by the disposition of the body, as the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle, who emphasized physical and observable causes</note> maintain: or, as is truer, according to the pleasure of the Giver of causes, who distributes to each as He wills. From there it passes to the imagination <note>original: "phantasiam," the faculty of forming mental images</note>, which is above the senses yet only capable of what can be imagined; then finally to the senses, especially to the eyes. In the eye, it becomes visible clarity, and it extends through other transparent bodies, in which it becomes color and a shining beauty. In opaque bodies, however, it acts as a certain beneficial and generative power that penetrates even to the center: where, its rays being gathered into a narrow space, it becomes a dark heat, agonizing and boiling. Thus all things feel the vigor of light according to their capacity; light connects all things to itself with life-giving warmth, and then, permeating all beings, it carries their qualities and powers through everything. For this reason, the Magi <note>Ancient practitioners of natural magic and philosophy</note> forbid anyone to be sprinkled by the shadow of a sick person, or for the urine of a sick person to be exposed to the sun or moon. This is because the penetrating rays of light, having carried harmful qualities from the diseased body, cast them upon whatever is opposite and afflict it with that same poor quality. This is the reason why those who cast spells <note>original: "fascinantes," those who practice fascination or the "evil eye"</note> take care to cover their victim with their own shadow; in the same way, a hyena makes dogs fall silent simply by the touch of its shadow.

Certain lights are also made artificially by means of lamps, wicks, candles, and the like, fashioned from specific things and liquids chosen opportunely according to the pattern of the stars, and composed among themselves to match the nature of those same stars. When these are lit and shine alone, they are accustomed to produce some wonderful and celestial effects, which men often marvel at. For instance, Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the famous Roman "Natural History"</note> relates from Anaxilaus <note>A Pythagorean philosopher and physician from the time of Augustus</note> that if the fluid from mares after mating <note>original: "equarum uirum à coitu." This likely refers to hippomanes, a substance used in ancient love philters and magic</note> is burned in lamps, a monstrous vision of horse-heads is represented; a similar thing happens with donkeys. Likewise, flies <note>original: "musciliones," likely small gnats or flies</note> mixed with wax and lit make one see flies; and the skin of a snake burned in a lamp makes snakes appear to be present. They also say that when grapes are in bloom, if one ties a phial full of oil around them and leaves it there until they are ripe, if that oil is afterwards lit in a lamp, it makes grapes appear to be seen. The same holds true for other fruits. 

Furthermore, if centaury <note>A medicinal herb named after the centaur Chiron</note> is mixed with honey and the blood of a hoopoe <note>The hoopoe was a bird frequently used in medieval and Renaissance magic for its supposed occult properties</note> and placed in a lamp, those standing around will appear larger than usual; and if it is lit at night under a clear sky, it seems as though the stars are running about toward one another. The ink of the cuttlefish also has such power that, when added to a lamp, it makes people appear as Ethiopians <note>original: "Aethiopes," here used in the historical sense to mean dark-skinned people</note>. It is also said that a candle made from certain Saturnian things <note>Items associated with the planet Saturn, which governs death, lead, and melancholy</note>, if extinguished in the mouth of a recently dead man and afterwards lit, brings the greatest sadness and fear to those standing around whenever it shines alone. Plato, Chyramides <note>An ancient author, possibly legendary, cited in Hermetic and alchemical texts regarding the virtues of stones and herbs</note>, and among more recent authors, Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval scholar and bishop who wrote extensively on the natural sciences</note>, in a certain treatise on this unique subject [concur]. Colors are also a kind of light which, when mixed into physical things, are accustomed to expose them to the stars and celestial bodies with which they are in harmony. We shall say in later parts <note>Referring to Book II of this work</note> which colors belong to the lights of the planets, by which the natures of the fixed stars may also be discovered; these principles may also be applied here to the flames of lamps and torches. But in this section, we shall relate how the colors of lower and mixed things are distributed among the various planets.

For indeed, all colors that are black, shining, earthy, lead-colored, or dusky <note>original: "fusci"</note> relate to Saturn. Colors that are sapphire-blue, airy, perpetually green, clear, purple, darker [purple], or gold mixed with silver belong to Jupiter. Colors that are red, burning, fiery, flame-like, violet, purple, blood-colored, and iron-colored relate to Mars. Colors that are golden, saffron-yellow, and a brighter purple relate to the Sun. However, all colors that are white, beautiful, variegated, green, reddish, and somewhat saffron or purple relate to Venus, Mercury, and the Moon.

Furthermore, out of the houses of heaven <note>The twelve divisions or "houses" used in an astrological chart</note>, the first and seventh have the color white; the second and twelfth have green; the third and eleventh have saffron; the fourth and tenth have red; the fifth and ninth have honey-colored; the sixth and eighth have black. The elements also have their own colors, by which natural philosophers <note>original: "Physici," referring to those who study nature and medicine</note> judge the temperament <note>original: "complexione," the balance of the four qualities—hot, cold, moist, and dry—within a body</note> and properties of nature: for the earthy color, produced from the cold and dry, is dusky and black,

However, all colors are more powerful when they are silky, or metallic, or in transparent substances, or precious stones, and in those things which more closely imitate celestial likeness, but most especially in living things.

**Fascination** <note>Historically, fascination refers to the power of the "Evil Eye" or a hypnotic binding through sight</note> is a binding, which, having entered from the **spirit** <note>In Renaissance philosophy, "spirit" is a subtle vapor or medium that connects the soul to the body</note> of the fascinator through the eyes of the fascinated, reaches into his very heart.

reaches. Now, the instrument of **fascination** <note>original: "fascinationis"; in this context, it refers to the "evil eye" or the ability to influence another person through a gaze</note> is the **spirit** <note>original: "spiritus"; in Renaissance medicine, this was a thin, subtle substance that traveled through the blood to link the mind and body</note>, namely a certain pure, bright, and subtle vapor, generated from the purest blood by the heat of the heart. This spirit always sends out rays like itself through the eyes. These emitted rays carry the spiritual vapor with them, and that vapor carries blood, just as is seen in bleary and red eyes; the ray from such an eye, sent out as far as the eyes of someone looking at it, draws with it the vapor of the corrupt blood, and by its contagion, it forces the onlooker's eyes to suffer from a similar disease. 

Thus, an eye opened and intent upon someone with a strong **imagination** <note>The power of the mind to shape the "spirit" and direct it outward</note>, using its rays as needles—which are the vehicles for the spirit—darts them into the opposing eyes. This clinging spirit, striking the eyes of the person being fascinated, and being spurred on by the heart of the one striking, enters the **chest** <note>original: "praecordia"; the internal organs, particularly the area around the heart and diaphragm, thought to be the seat of emotions</note> of the person hit, as if it had found its own territory; this foreign spirit wounds the heart and infects the local spirit. For this reason **Apuleius** <note>Lucius Apuleius (c. 124–170 AD), a Roman philosopher and author of "The Golden Ass"</note> says:

> Those eyes of yours, he said, having slipped through my own eyes into my innermost chest, stir up a most sharp fire in my very marrow.

Know then that humans are most fascinated when, by frequent gazing, they long to receive the sharp edge of the looker's vision into their own eyes; rays are coupled with rays, and light joined with light; then spirit is joined to spirit, and fixes its sparks. In this way, the strongest **bindings** <note>original: "ligationes"; magical or psychological bonds that constrain a person's will</note> and the sharpest loves are kindled solely by the rays of the eyes, even by a sudden glance, like a dart or a blow penetrating the whole body; from which then the spi-

the spirit <note>original: "spiritus"</note> and the blood of one wounded by love are carried toward the lover and the fascinator <note>The person casting the influence through their gaze</note> in no different a way than the blood and spirit of revenge from someone who has been killed flow out toward their slayer. Hence **Lucretius** <note>Titus Lucretius Carus (c. 99–55 BC), Roman poet and philosopher known for "On the Nature of Things"</note> sang of these amorous fascinations:

Such is the power of fascination, especially when the vapors of the eyes serve the emotions. Therefore, those who practice fascination use **eye-salves** <term>collyria: medicinal or magical preparations applied to the eyes</term>, ointments, bindings, and similar things to affect and strengthen the spirit in a particular way. For example, to induce love, they use "Venereal" eye-salves <note>Associated with the planet Venus</note> made from **hippomanes** <term>hippomanes: a rare substance found on the forehead of a newborn foal, or sometimes a discharge from mares, highly prized in antiquity for love charms</term>, the blood of doves or sparrows, and other similar materials. To induce fear, they use "Martial" eye-salves <note>Associated with the planet Mars</note> made from the eyes of wolves, hyenas, and the like. For calamity or sickness, they use "Saturnine" preparations <note>Associated with the planet Saturn</note>. A similar logic must be applied to each individual case.

Thus they say **quartan fevers** <note>A recurring fever associated with malaria, where the paroxysm occurs every fourth day</note> are driven away if the parings of the patient’s nails are tied to the neck of a live eel,

<meta>This page continues a series of examples regarding "ligatures" and physical objects that carry occult virtues or "fascination." *...alive in a small linen cloth, and thus returned into the water. And **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), the Roman author of "Natural History," a primary source for Agrippa’s examples</note> relates that nail clippings <term>resegmina unguium: parings of the nails</term> taken from the feet and hands of a sick person and mixed with wax are said to be a remedy for quartan, tertian, or daily fevers <note>These terms describe the cycles of malarial fevers common in antiquity: quartan (occurring every fourth day), tertian (every third day), or daily.</note> if it is said that they are a cure, and then fixed upon the door of another person before sunrise, the disease will be cured in the patients. In a similar way, if all the nail clippings are thrown at an ant hill, they command that the first ant which begins to drag a clipping away be caught and tied around the patient's neck, and thus the disease is dispelled. It is also said that a disease may be driven away by a piece of wood struck by lightning, if it is thrown behind one's back with the hands. And for quartan fevers, they tie a fragment of a nail from a cross <note>original: "claui è cruce"</note> wrapped in wool around the neck, or a rope <note>original: "spartum," likely a cord used in an execution</note> from a cross; once the person is freed from the illness, they hide the object in a cave or hole where the sun cannot reach it. Likewise, they affirm that scrofulous tumors <term>strumas: swellings of the lymph nodes, typically of the neck</term> and swellings behind the ears <term>parotidas: inflammation of the parotid salivary glands</term> are healed by the touch of the hand of one snatched away by an untimely death, if the throat is touched. They also say that difficult labors are immediately resolved when someone throws over the bed in which the pregnant woman lies a stone or a missile weapon that has killed three animals with single strikes—namely, a man, a boar, and a bear. They say a military spear pulled from a human body accomplishes the same, provided it has not touched the ground. They also report that arrows drawn out of a body, if they have not touched the ground, act as a love charm <note>original: "amatorium"</note> when placed under those who are sleeping. It is said that epilepsy <term>comitiales morbos: literally "assembly diseases," so named because a seizure was considered an ill omen that required the suspension of a Roman public meeting or "comitia"</term> can be cured by eating the meat of a wild beast killed with the same iron weapon that killed a man. They claim that eye defects do not occur, nor do people suffer from bleary eyes, if they touch their eyes three times with the water used to wash their feet. Some treat pains in the groin by taking a thread from a woven web, and tying it in nine* <note>The text cuts off here, continuing with the catchword "knots" or "veins" on the next page.</note>

<meta>The catchword "uenis" suggests the completion of the word "nouenis" (ninefold) or perhaps "nouenis nodis" (nine knots), a common ritual number in such charms.</meta>

This is proven, he says, by a hamstrung quadruped <note>original: "delumbata quadrupede," referring to an animal that has been struck or weakened in the loins</note>, for the animal’s gait is corrected immediately by such a remedy. Likewise, it is said that if someone has measured a dead man with a rope—first from the elbow to the longest finger, then from the shoulder to the same finger, and finally from the head to the feet, performing all these measurements three times—if any other person is later measured with that same rope in a similar way, they say that person will meet with ill-fortune and bad luck, and fall into calamity and sorrow. ## Concerning the face, gesture, the habit of the body, and its form, and which of these correspond to which stars: from which <term>physiognomy</term> <note>the study of character from facial features</note>, <term>metoposcopy</term> <note>divination through the lines of the forehead</note>, and <term>chiromancy</term> <note>palmistry; reading the lines of the hand</note>, the crafts of divination, have their foundations.

### Chapter 52.

The face, gestures, and the movements and positions of the body, as well as the forms applied to us from above, are conducive to receiving celestial gifts; they expose us to the powers above and produce certain effects in us. This is no different than with <term>hellebore</term> <note>a plant used in ancient medicine as a powerful purgative</note>: when someone gathers this herb, whether they pull the leaf upward or downward, that very motion causes it to draw the humors <note>the bodily fluids that determined health and temperament</note> upward or downward. Furthermore, no one is ignorant of how much the face and gestures affect the sight, the imagination, and the animal spirit <note>the "spiritus animalis," a refined substance believed to travel through the nerves to facilitate movement and sensation</note>. Thus, those coming together for procreation often [reproduce] the expressions they themselves then make...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how human gestures and physical forms act as mirrors or magnets for celestial influences, specifically detailing the behaviors and appearances associated with the planets Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars.</meta>

...or imagine, they are accustomed to imprint upon the children they are about to bear. In the same way, the kind and joyful face of a Prince in his city makes the people happy, while a fierce or sad face suddenly terrifies them. Similarly, the gesture and appearance of a mourner easily moves one to mercy, and the figure of a lovely person easily excites love. You ought to know, however, that gestures and forms of this kind—as harmonies of the body—expose the body to celestial things, in no different a way than odors and medicines expose the spirit, or internal passions expose the soul. For just as medicines and the passions of the mind unite with certain celestial dispositions, so also the gestures and movements of the body attain efficacy through certain influences of the heavenly bodies. 

There are, moreover, gestures relating to **Saturn** <note>The planet traditionally associated with melancholy, discipline, and gravity</note>, which are sad and mournful, such as lamentations or striking the head. Likewise, religious gestures, such as kneeling with the gaze fixed downward, as well as striking the breast as if in prayer, and similar faces—the austere look of a **Saturnine** <note>aggressively gloomy, slow, or serious</note> man, such as the satirist <note>Persius, *Satires*, 3.80-82</note> describes:

> With head bent low, and fixing his eyes on the ground,
> When he mutters to himself, and gnaws on a rabid silence,
> And weighs his words with a jutting lip.
<note>original: "Obstypo capite, & figentem lumine terram, / Murmura cum secum, & rabiosa silentia rodit, / Atq; exporrecto trutinatur uerba labello."</note>

Those things relating to **Jupiter** <note>The planet associated with justice, joy, and social order</note> are a cheerful face and honest gestures, an honorable joining of the hands, as if clapping or praising. Likewise, kneeling with the head lifted upward, as if in adoration.

Those relating to **Mars** <note>The planet associated with war, heat, and aggression</note> are sharp, fierce, cruel, angry, and grim gestures, and faces of a similar kind.

*Solar things are bold, honor-seeking, and have similar faces. Likewise, their movements include certain walks and kneeling on a single knee, as if honoring a king. ### Concerning the divination of auspices and auguries, Chapter 53. ###

There are certain other types of divinations depending on natural causes, which consist of their own skill and experience in various matters; through these, physicians <note>original: "media," likely a typographical error for "medici" (physicians)</note>, farmers, shepherds, and sailors each make prognostications from probable signs. Aristotle recounted many of these in his book *On Weather Signs*. <note>original: "libro de temporibus," likely referring to the pseudo-Aristotelian *De Signis* or portions of the *Meteorologica*.</note> Among these types of divination, **auspices** and **auguries** hold the primary place. These were once held in such high regard among the Romans that they completed nothing pertaining to public or private business without taking auspices; Cicero, in his book *On Divination*, declares at length that the Etruscan people especially excelled in this craft.

Indeed, there are several kinds of auspices: for some are called **pedestrian**, which are clearly taken from four-footed animals; some are **auguries**, taken from birds; some are **celestial**, taken from thunder and lightning; some are **accidental** <note>original: "caduca," meaning "falling" or "unforeseen events"</note>, such as when something has fallen in a temple or elsewhere; and some are **sacred**, which were taken from sacrifices. Some of these were called **expiatory** <note>original: "piacula," referring to signs requiring a ritual of atonement</note> and grim auspices, such as when a sacrificial victim had fled from the altar, or when, having been struck, it let out a bellow, or...

To these is added **Exauguration** <note>original: "Exauguratio"; here used to mean a spoiled omen rather than its usual meaning of deconsecrating a temple</note>, which occurs when the wand <note>original: "uirga," likely the *lituus* or curved staff used by Roman augurs</note> falls from the hand of the one performing the augury, with which it was the custom to observe and take the auspice. Michael Scot <note>A 13th-century mathematician and scholar known as a "wizard" in folklore, who served at the court of Emperor Frederick II.</note> recounts twelve types of auguries: six on the right side, the names of which he says are *fernova, fervetus, confert, emponethem, scimafarnova, scimafervetus*; and another six on the left side, the names of which are *confernova, confervetus, viaram, hartenam, scaffarnova, scaffarvetus*. Then, explaining these names, he says:

<term>Fernova</term> <note>These terms are Michael Scot's unique nomenclature for bird and human encounters, likely derived from a mix of corrupted Latin and other influences.</note> is an augury when you have left your house for the purpose of doing some business, and as you go you see a man or a bird walking or flying so that it places itself before you on your **left side**; this is for you a sign of good meaning concerning your business.

<term>Fervetus</term> is an augury when you have left home for the purpose of doing something, and as you go you first find or see a bird or a man resting before you on your **left side**; this is for you a bad sign concerning your business.

<term>Viaram</term> is an augury when a man or a bird, in its own journey or flight, passes before you, coming from your **right side** and moving toward the **left**, and then vanishes; this is for you a good sign concerning your business.

<term>Confernova</term> is an augury when you first find a man or bird going or flying, and it pauses before you on the **right** while you are watching; this is for you a good sign concerning your business.

<term>Confervetus</term> is an augury when you first find or see a man or bird reclining on your **right side** while you are watching; it is

### Concerning certain diverse animals and other things, and what significance they have in auguries. Chapter 54.

Any first auspices <note>original: "auspicia"; specifically the observation of signs before a major undertaking</note> are to be taken at the very beginning of any work; for instance, if at the start of a task you notice your garment has been gnawed by mice <note>original: "soricibus"; this refers to shrews or mice, which were considered particularly ominous in Roman superstition if they damaged property.</note>, you should cease from what you have begun.

...undertakings: if, while heading out, you stumble at the threshold, or trip your foot on the path, stop your journey. If any evil omen occurs to you at the beginning of your business, postpone what you have started; lest your whole intention be frustrated, or completed to no effect. But wait until, with a better omen, you seize a favorable hour for your tasks. 

We see many animals that, by a certain power implanted in them by nature, are wondrous <note>original: "praestigiosa"; in this context, it refers to a supernatural or deceptive power to show signs</note> and prophetic. Does not the rooster most skillfully announce the hours with its song, and put the lion to flight with outspread wings? Many birds by their song and chattering, flies by their sharp sting, foretell rain; and dolphins, by frequent leaps above the water, run ahead of storms. 

It would be a long task to recount all the presages which the Phrygians, Cilicians, Arabs, Umbrians, Tuscans, and other peoples—who followed <term>auguries</term> <note>the practice of interpreting omens from the behavior of birds</note>—learned from birds and animals, which have been proven by them through many experiences and examples. For in all things there are hidden oracles of the future, but the most important ones will be announced to you by birds of omen. These are the ones which the poets report were transformed from humans into birds. 

Therefore, listen diligently to what the crow speaks, and its position whether sitting or flying, to the right or to the left; whether it is talkative or noisy, or silent; whether it precedes or follows, or waits for the arrival of one passing by, or flees, or where it departs—pay careful attention to all these things. Horapollo says in his <term>Hieroglyphica</term> <note>original: "Orus Appollo in hieroglyphicis"; Horapollo was the legendary author of a work explaining Egyptian symbols, which was highly influential in the Renaissance</note> that twin crows signify marriage, because this animal lays two eggs, from which it is necessary that a male and a female be born: if

<meta>This page continues the specific discussion on bird augury from page 138, moving from general observations of crows to ravens, swans, and finally the supreme omen of the eagle.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>BOOK I.</header>

Moreover, if—which happens very rarely—two males are born, or two females joined together, the males do not mix with other crows <note>original: "cornici"; the hooded crow was often associated with marital fidelity in folklore</note>, nor do the females with another male; instead, separated, they live alone. Therefore, encountering a single crow leads to the augury that one must live a widowed life. The black dove portends the same; for after the death of its mate, it always lives alone. You shall observe ravens <note>original: "coruos"</note> with no less diligence, for they say these are preferred for greater matters and take precedence over the crow. *Epictetus, the Stoic philosopher* and a weighty authority, also felt that if a raven should croak against someone, it portends adversity for the body, fortune, honor, wife, or children. Next, you shall consider swans, which foreknow the secrets of the waters; for their joyfulness presages happy outcomes not only for sailors, but for all others who travel, unless they are overcome by the arrival of a stronger bird—such as the eagle. The eagle, by the most powerful majesty of its empire, voids the reliability <note>original: "fidem"; here meaning the prophetic authority or trustworthiness</note> of all birds if it speaks to the contrary. Indeed, it flies higher than all birds, sees more deeply, and is never excluded from the secret council of Jupiter himself. It portends high status and victory, but through blood, because it drinks not water, but blood. When the Locrians <note>People of a Greek colony in Southern Italy</note> were fighting against the Crotonians, an eagle flying overhead brought victory. For Hiero <note>Hiero II, the King of Syracuse</note>, as he was heading out to the first stages of a war, an eagle unexpectedly settling on his shield announced that he would be king. At the birth of *Alexander the Macedonian*, two eagles sitting above the house for the whole day gave the omen of a double empire

# ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY
### BOOK I.

...portended dominion over Asia and Europe to him. Now indeed, **vultures** also signify difficulty, hardness, and capacity, which was verified by experience at the original founding of the city <note>original: "origine urbis condendæ"; a reference to the famous contest between Romulus and Remus, who used a flight of vultures to determine who should rule Rome</note>. They also reveal the locations of future slaughter, appearing seven days in advance; and because they look toward that region where the lower party is destined to fall—as if gaping at the greater number of those to be killed—ancient kings were accustomed to send out scouts to report which way the vultures were turned and looking.

The **Phoenix**, however, promises the success of a singular happiness; when it was seen, "New Rome" <note>Constantinople</note> was founded under better auspices.

The **Pelican**, because it endures danger for the sake of its young, signifies a man who will undergo a crisis out of the devotion of his friendship <note>Medieval bestiaries claimed the pelican would peck its own breast to feed its young with its blood, making it a symbol of sacrifice and piety</note>.

The **Painted bird** <note>original: "Picta auis"</note> gave its name to the city of the *Pictavi* <note>The city of Poitiers in France</note>, and prefigured the gentleness of that people through its color and voice.

The **Heron** <note>original: "Ardea"</note> is an omen of "arduous" <note>original: "arduarum"; Agrippa is using a common Renaissance etymological pun linking the bird's name to the word for difficult or high things</note> matters.

The **Stork** <note>original: "Cyconia"</note>, being a bird of concord, brings about harmony.

**Cranes** <note>original: "Grues"</note>—named from the ancient word *gruere*, as if to say *congruere* <note>To agree or to come together</note>—always bring that which is expedient, and they cause one to beware the snares of enemies.

The **Hoopoe** <note>original: "Cucupha"</note> indicates gratitude; for this bird alone returns the favor of its parents when they are worn out by old age <note>A common ancient belief that young hoopoes would groom and care for their elderly parents</note>. On the contrary, the **Hippopotamus**, a murderer of its own father, announces ingratitude for favors and injustice.

The **Oryx-bird** <note>original: "Orygis auis"; likely referring to a legendary desert bird or a misinterpretation of the antelope Oryx in a bestiary context</note>, a most envious creature, signifies envy.

And now, even among the smaller birds:
The talkative **Magpie** <note>original: "Pica"</note> announces guests.
The **Hen-harrier** <note>original: "Albanellus"</note> flying past—if it is carried from the left to the right—indicates the cheerfulness of hospitality; if the opposite, it indicates the opposite.
The **Screech-owl** <note>original: "Strix"</note>, a bird always unfa-

### BOOK I.

...this is the case, and even the **owl** <note>original: "noctua"; specifically the Little Owl, often associated with wisdom but here linked to the suddenness of death</note>: because it attacks chicks unexpectedly at night, just as death comes upon us unawares, it is said to foretell death. Yet sometimes, because it is not blinded by the shadows of the night, it signifies a man's diligence and vigilance, which was proven when one perched upon the spear of Hiero <note>Hiero II of Syracuse; the owl's presence was seen as a sign of his watchful leadership</note>. Dido, moreover, felt the **horned owl** <note>original: "Bubonem"; the Great Horned Owl, traditionally a more sinister omen than the *noctua*</note> was an ill-fated sign when she was involved with Aeneas; regarding this, the *Poet* <note>Virgil, in the *Aeneid* IV.462-463</note> sang,

> *And often the solitary horned owl on the rooftops with its funereal song*
> *Seemed to complain, drawing out its long cries into a wail.*

->*And elsewhere,*<-

> *The sluggish horned owl is a dire omen for mortals.*

*Almadel* <note>A reference to the *Ars Almadel*, a popular medieval grimoire of ritual magic</note> says that when **horned owls** and **night-ravens** <note>original: "Nycticoraces"; likely referring to the black-crowned night heron or a similar nocturnal bird</note> turn aside to unusual regions and dwellings, it is a sign that the people of those regions and houses are about to die. This is because those birds delight in corpses and sense them in advance; and those who are about to die are already, in potential, corpses. Furthermore, *Naso* <note>The Roman poet Ovid</note> sings of the **hawk** <note>original: "Accipitre"</note> as a harbinger of strife:

> *We hate the hawk, because he lives always in arms.*

And *Almadel* says that birds of the same species fighting among themselves signify a change of the kingdom. If, however, they are of different species and wage war against one another, and birds arrive that have not been seen before, they portend a new fortune for that region. Even **tiny birds** <note>original: "Minutulæ etiam aues"</note> portend by their arrival or departure that a family will increase or diminish; and the more serene their flight, the more praiseworthy the omen. Wherefore *Melampus* the augur <note>A legendary Greek seer said to have been taught the language of birds by snakes who licked his ears</note>, from...

### BOOK I.

...predicted the slaughter of the Greeks by the flight of small birds <note>original: "auicularum"; the observation of bird flight for divination was a core practice of Roman augury</note>, when he says: "You see how no bird <note>original: "Ales"; a poetic term for a winged creature</note> moves in serene courses." **Swallows** <note>original: "Hirundines"</note>, because when they are dying they prepare hiding places for their chicks, portend a large inheritance or a legacy left by the dead. A **bat** <note>original: "Vespertilio"</note> meeting someone who is fleeing signifies an escape; for although it has no feathers <note>The author notes the bat's leather-like wings as a unique trait that allows it to "fly away" from its classification as a bird</note>, it nevertheless flies away. 

The **sparrow** <note>original: "Passer"</note>, however, is a bad omen for someone fleeing, for while fleeing a **hawk** <note>original: "Accipitrem"</note>, it runs to an **owl** <note>original: "noctuam"</note>, where it is in equal danger; nevertheless, it is a good omen in matters of love, since, stirred by lust, it mates seven times in an hour. **Bees** <note>original: "Apes"</note> are good omens for kings: for they show an obedient people; but **flies** <note>original: "muscæ"</note> signify annoyance and impudence, because although they are driven away many times, they nevertheless return immediately. Domestic birds are also not without auguries; for **roosters** <note>original: "galli"</note>, with their song, encourage hope and the journey of one setting out.

Indeed, when **Tiberius** <note>Tiberius Caesar, the second Roman Emperor (reigned 14–37 AD)</note> was about to be born, his pregnant mother **Livia** <note>Livia Drusilla, mother of Tiberius and wife of Emperor Augustus; this anecdote is famously recorded by Pliny the Elder</note> took a hen's egg and warmed it in her bosom until a rooster with a remarkable crest was hatched; from this, the augurs interpreted that the boy to be born would become a ruler. And **Cicero** <note>The Roman statesman Marcus Tullius Cicero, likely referencing his work *On Divination*</note> writes that in the region of **Thebes** <note>original: "Thebaidã"; a powerful city-state in ancient Greece</note>, roosters, by crowing through entire nights, foretold victory for the **Boeotians** <note>The people of the region surrounding Thebes</note> against the **Lacedaemonians** <note>The Spartans, famous for their military prowess</note>; which the augurs interpreted thus: because that bird remains silent when defeated, but crows if it wins. 

By a similar reasoning, omens of events are also taken from animals and beasts: for the encounter with a **hare** <note>original: "leporis"</note> is to be feared by a traveler, unless it happens to be captured. A **mule** <note>original: "Mulus"</note> is also unfavorable, because

The **donkey** <note>original: "Asinus"</note> is useless, yet it benefited **Marius** <note>Gaius Marius (157–86 BC), a Roman general and statesman</note>, who, when he was declared an enemy of his country, saw a donkey spurning the offered fodder <gloss>food</gloss> and hurrying toward water. Judging by this omen that a path of safety was shown to him, he begged the helpful crowd of friends to offer this one kind of assistance and lead him to the sea; which being granted, placed upon a small boat, he escaped the threats of the victor **Sulla** <note>Lucius Cornelius Sulla, Marius's great rival in the Roman civil wars</note>. Nevertheless, whenever a little donkey occurs in an omen, it always signifies labor, patience, and obstacles.

# BOOK I.

...that if **ants** had devoured a **dragon** <note>original: *draconem*</note>, it was interpreted as a warning that he should beware the tumult of the common crowd. If you should encounter a **snake**, beware of a slanderous actor, for this animal is powerful in no other part of its body than its mouth. Likewise, the **viper** signifies wicked women and criminal sons; and the **eel** signifies a man who is hostile to everyone, for this creature lives apart from other fish and is never found joined with others. 

However, among all auspices and omens, none is more effective, none more powerful, and none more clearly reveals the truth than **Man**. Therefore, you must diligently note and examine every aspect of a person you encounter: their status, age, sex, profession, posture, gesture, movement, occupation, complexion, clothing, name, words, and speech. For since there are so many lights of prophecy within other animals, there is no doubt that these are infused much more effectively and clearly into the human soul. This is something that **Tullius** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and philosopher.</note> himself testifies to, saying that there is naturally within human minds a certain auspice of their own eternity, allowing them to recognize the series and causes of all things.

# ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY

...subsequently held in high regard among all nations. However, these [auspices] are verified through the **luminosity of the sense of nature** <note>term: *sensus naturae* — a concept in Renaissance philosophy referring to an innate, instinctive ability to perceive truths or future events without rational deduction.</note>, as if from this source certain lights of divination descend upon four-legged beasts, birds, and other living creatures. Through these lights, they are able to presage human events for us, a phenomenon that **Virgil** <note>The Roman poet Publius Virgilius Maro; the following quote is from his *Georgics*, Book I.</note> also seems to perceive where he sings:

> Indeed I do not believe that it is because they possess a divine spirit, 
> or a greater foresight of things by fate.
> <note>original: "Haud equidem credo, quia sit diuinius illis Ingenium, aut rerum fato prudentia maior."</note>

Moreover, this kind of **sense of nature**, as **William of Paris** <note>Guilielmus Parisiensis, also known as William of Auvergne (c. 1180–1249), a Bishop of Paris and philosopher who wrote extensively on the intersection of theology and natural magic.</note> says, is itself loftier than all human apprehension, and is very close—indeed, strongly similar—to prophecy. From this sense, a certain wonderful splendor of divination is naturally instilled in some animals. This clearly appears in certain **dogs**, who recognize, find, track, and seize thieves and hidden men—even those entirely unknown to the dogs or to other humans—by this kind of sense, rushing at them with their jaws and teeth. 

By a similar sense, **vultures** foresee future slaughters from battles and gather in places where they are to occur, as if they foresee for themselves a future feast of corpses. By the same sense, **partridges** recognize their true mother, whom they have never seen, and they will abandon a partridge that has stolen the mother’s eggs to brood over them. By this same sense, while the human soul remains entirely unaware, certain harmful and terrifying things are sensed; from this, terror and horror invade many people who are otherwise sensing or thinking nothing of such matters...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter 55, providing specific examples of the "sense of nature"—an instinctive, almost prophetic perception—found in both humans and animals.</meta>

->### BOOK I.<-

...[inhabiting]. Thus, someone hiding in a house, even if their presence is entirely unknown or unthought of, strikes horror, fear, and restlessness of heart into the residents of that same house—perhaps not into all of them, because this kind of **splendor** <note>Referring to the "splendor of divination" or natural light of the soul mentioned previously.</note> is not present in all people, but only in a few. Likewise, a harlot hidden in a very large house is sometimes sensed by someone who is otherwise entirely unaware of her presence.

The same **William of Paris** <note>term: *Guilielmus Parisiensis* — William of Auvergne (c. 1180–1249), a Bishop of Paris and philosopher who sought to explain natural phenomena through a blend of theology and science.</note> also relates that in his own time, a certain woman, by this same sense, felt the approach of the man she loved from two miles away as he neared the place where she was staying. He also relates that in his time, a certain **stork** <note>original: *ciconia*</note> committed adultery; the male stork sensed this by scent and gathered a great multitude of other storks. After the crime of the female was exposed by him, she was judged guilty by that entire assembly—as if by a public trial—and was plucked of her feathers and torn apart. 

He further recounts the story of a **horse** who, having unknowingly mated with his own mother and later discovering the fact, bit off his own genitals with his teeth as if to avenge the **incest** <note>original: *incestum*</note> against himself. Similar stories about horses are recorded by **Aristotle**, **Varro**, and **Pliny** <note>Ancient authorities: Aristotle (philosopher), Marcus Terentius Varro (Roman scholar), and Pliny the Elder (naturalist), all of whom were standard sources for natural history in the Renaissance.</note>. **Pliny** also tells a similar story about a certain **asp** <note>original: *aspis* — a venomous snake.</note> in Egypt. This snake came to a certain man's table and was fed there daily. She gave birth to young, and one of them killed the host's son. When the mother returned and realized the crime, she put her own offspring to death and never returned to that house again. Now, from these examples, we see how the lights of **presages** <note>term: *praesagium* — a natural sign or foreboding of a future event, perceived through instinct rather than reason.</note> [shine] upon certain living creatures...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 55 regarding the "sense of nature," specifically how natural signs and animal behaviors reflect celestial influences and serve as omens.</meta>

### ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY

...can descend, as signs of things, established in their gesture, motion, voice, flight, gait, food, color, and the like. For according to the doctrine of the Platonists <note>term: *platonicorum* — followers of Plato's philosophy, who believed earthly things are reflections of higher, divine realities.</note>, there is a certain power implanted in lower things, through which they largely agree with superior things; from which also the silent agreements of animals seem to harmonize with divine bodies <note>Refers to the planets and celestial spheres.</note>, and their bodies and emotions are affected by these powers, the names of which are ascribed to their own deities. One must therefore consider which animals are Saturnine <note>original: *Saturnalia* — associated with the planet Saturn.</note>, which represent Jupiter <note>original: *Iouem*</note>, which Mars <note>original: *Martem*</note>, and so on for each, and to derive omens <note>original: *praesagia*</note> according to their properties. Thus, those which represent Saturn and Mars are all called ill-omened and funereal birds, such as little owls <note>original: *noctuae*</note>, screech owls <note>original: *ululae*</note>, and the others which we have recounted above. The eagle-owl <note>original: *bubo*</note> also, because it is a Saturnine bird, solitary and nocturnal, is said to be of the most unpropitious omen, of which the Poet <note>Likely referring to a classical source like Lucan or Ovid.</note> says:

> And so the foul bird, messenger of coming grief,
> <note>original: "Foedaque sic uolucris uenturi nuncia luctus"</note>
> The sluggish eagle-owl, a dire omen for mortals.
> <note>original: "Ignauus bubo dirum mortalibus omen"</note>

The swan <note>original: *Cygnus*</note> indeed, a delightful bird, Venus-like, and even sacred to Phoebus <note>Apollo, the god of light, music, and prophecy.</note>, is said to be of the happiest omen, especially in the auspices of sailors, because it is not submerged by water; whence Ovid <note>The Roman poet Publius Ovidius Naso.</note> sings:

> The swan is always the most joyful bird in auspices.
> <note>original: "Cygnus in auspiciis semper laetissimus ales"</note>

There are, moreover, "oscine" birds <note>term: *oscines* — in Roman augury, birds that provided omens through their cries or songs rather than their flight.</note>, which make an omen by their mouth and song, such as the raven, the woodpecker, and the crow; whence Virgil <note>The Roman poet Publius Vergilius Maro.</note> says:

> Often the ill-omened crow foretold it from the hollow oak.
> <note>original: "Saepe sinistra caua praedixit ab ilice cornix"</note>

# BOOK I.

...like buzzards, ospreys <note>original: *sanquales* — birds dedicated to the god Sancus, likely ospreys or a type of eagle.</note>, eagles, vultures, cranes, swans, and similar birds: for they are observed in flight, whether they fly slowly or swiftly; whether they fly to the right or to the left, and how many fly together at once. In this way, cranes, when they fly swiftly, signify a storm; when they fly silently, they signify calm weather. Likewise, when two birds fly together and are birds of ill-omen <note>original: *ferales* — associated with death or the underworld.</note>, they are said to portend evil, because the number two is the number of confusion <note>In Pythagorean and occult thought, the number two (the dyad) represents the first departure from the unity of the number one, and thus symbolizes division or discord.</note>. You shall investigate other matters in a similar way, applying the logic of numbers to this practice. Furthermore, it is a mark of mastery to observe the similarity in these conjectures, just as Venus herself, in disguise, teaches her son Aeneas in these verses from Virgil:

> Unless my parents taught me omens in vain,
> Behold twice six swans rejoicing in a flock,
> Whom the bird of Jupiter <note>original: *Iouis ales* — referring to the eagle, the messenger of the king of the gods.</note>, gliding from the heavenly region,
> Was scattering in the open sky; now in a long line
> They seem either to take their place on the ground, or to look down on those already taken.
> Just as they, returning, play with rustling wings,
> And have circled the pole in a group and given their songs,
> Not otherwise do your ships and the youth of your people
> Either hold the harbor, or enter the mouth with full sail.

The most wonderful kind of augury <note>term: *augurandi genus* — the practice of interpreting omens from the behavior of birds.</note>, however, is that in which it is read that Apollonius of Tyana <note>A famous Greek Neopythagorean philosopher and reputed wonder-worker of the 1st century AD.</note> excelled, who they say understood the language of birds. Concerning him, Philostratus and Porphyry <note>Classical biographers and philosophers who wrote about the life and mystical powers of Apollonius.</note> relate that once, when Apollonius sat in a gathering of friends, looking at the sparrows

...sitting in a tree, one little sparrow <note>original: *passerculum*</note> came from elsewhere, chirping and making a loud noise with its voice; and when they had all followed it and flown away, [Apollonius] said to his companions that this sparrow had announced to the others that a donkey near the city, in a narrow lane, had fallen while loaded with wheat, and the wheat was scattered on the ground. Moved by these words, many went to see for themselves, and they found it had happened just as Apollonius had said, and they were filled with wonder. However, Porphyry the Platonist, in the third book of *On Sacrifices*, says that it was a swallow. For it is certain that every voice of any animal is expressive of some passion of its soul, whether a joyful, sad, or angry disposition; and it is not so very surprising that these voices can be understood by men who are practiced in these matters. Indeed, Democritus <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher (c. 460 – c. 370 BCE), often associated with hidden or magical natural properties in later occult traditions.</note> himself handed down this art (as Pliny says), naming certain birds from whose mingled blood a serpent is born; whoever eats this serpent will understand the conversations of birds. And Hermes says that if anyone goes out to hunt on a certain day of the Kalends of November <note>The first day of November.</note> and cooks the first bird he catches along with the heart of a fox, all who eat from this will understand the voices of birds and other animals.

The Arabs also handed down that those who have eaten the heart or liver of dragons <note>original: *draconum* — in this context, usually referring to large serpents or mythical snakes believed to possess secret wisdom.</note> are skilled in understanding the meanings of brute animals. And Proclus the Platonist himself believed and taught that the heart of a mole contributes to foretelling the future. There were also divinations and auspices taken from the entrails <note>original: *extis*</note> and fibers of sacrifices, of which the inventor was Tages <note>In Etruscan mythology, Tages was a prophetic child with the wisdom of an old man who emerged from a plowed field to teach the art of reading animal entrails (haruspicy).</note>, about whom Lucan sang:

> And let there be no faith in the fibers, but the founder of the art...
<note>Lucan, *Pharsalia*, Book 1. The poet is describing the uncertainty and terror of omens before the Roman Civil War.</note>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the observation of animals for divination. ### BOOK I.

Tages <note>original: *Tages* — A legendary Etruscan prophet who was said to have been plowed up from a field as a child; he revealed the secrets of divination to the Roman and Etruscan peoples.</note> invented those things. 

Roman religion considered the liver to be the "head" or chief of the <term>exta (the vital internal organs used in sacrifice)</term>. From this, the <term>Haruspices (diviners who specialized in reading entrails)</term>, when seeking future events in the organs, would inspect the liver first. Two "heads" <note>original: *capitibus* — Referring to the lobes of the liver, specifically the *processus pyramidalis*.</note> were established upon it: one they named for the citizen and the other for the enemy. Having compared these lobes, they would announce the victory of one side or the other, just as we read in the poet Lucan <note>Marcus Annaeus Lucanus (39–65 CE), author of the epic poem *Pharsalia*.</note> that the entrails signaled the slaughter of Pompey’s followers and the victory of Caesar’s men in these verses:

> And what wickedness appeared with impunity in no entrails,
> Behold, he saw a mass of fibers grow upon the head,
> A part of the other head hung sick and withered,
> While another part gleamed and moved the veins with a rapid, wicked pulse:

Then, after examining the other vital organs, they would look for the heart. If the sacrificial animal was found without a heart, or if the "head" of the liver was missing, these were considered death-bearing omens and were called <term>expiatory (signs requiring ritual atonement)</term>. It was the same if the victim fled from the altar, or let out a lowing sound when struck, or fell upon a part of the body other than what was proper. We read of these signs regarding Julius Caesar; on the day he went out in purple robes and sat upon a golden chair, the heart was twice found missing from the entrails while he was sacrificing. And for Gaius Marius <note>A famous Roman general and statesman (157–86 BCE).</note> as he sacrificed at Utica, the liver was missing from the entrails. Similarly, this happened to Cato the leader <note>Likely referring to Cato the Younger, a Stoic philosopher and politician.</note> and to Marcus Marcellus, which is certainly thought to occur either by the power of the gods or the work of demons. Hence, it was always considered of the greatest importance among the ancients whenever something unusual appeared in the entrails: just as when Sulla <note>Lucius Cornelius Sulla (c. 138–78 BCE), the Roman dictator known for his belief in his own divine luck.</note> was sacrificing at Laurentum, the likeness

So great was the veneration for these arts in former times that all the most powerful and wisest men pursued them; indeed, the Senate and kings would do nothing without taking the <term>auspices (signs from the gods)</term>. But today all these things have been abolished, as much by the negligence of men as by the authority of the Church fathers.

### Chapter 56. On the divination of flashes and bolts of lightning, and how portents and prodigies are to be interpreted.

Furthermore, regarding the <term>augury (the interpretation of signs)</term> of flashes and bolts of lightning, and of <term>ostents (miraculous appearances)</term>, and how <term>portents (signs of coming evil)</term> and <term>prodigies (unnatural events)</term> are to be interpreted, the Etruscan <note>The Etruscans were an ancient Italian civilization known for their complex religious rituals and divination, which the Romans later adopted.</note> prophets and priests handed down the knowledge. For they established sixteen regions of the sky, with their own <term>numina (divine powers)</term> assigned to each; furthermore, they identified eleven types of lightning and nine gods who would hurl them, providing explanations for what they signified from each direction. Indeed, whenever portents, prodigies, and ostents occur, it is certain that they always foretell something of great importance.

BOOK I.

it is. Moreover, the interpreter of these signs must be at the same time an excellent conjecturer of similarities, as well as a diligent investigator of those affairs of princes and provinces that are current at that time. For it is the concern of princes, peoples, provinces, and heavenly matters alone that they, above all others, are prefigured and warned by the stars, the constellations, portents, and prodigies. But if in past ages the same thing or something very similar appeared, it will be necessary to consider what followed from that then, and based on these, to predict either the same or similar outcomes; for the same signs apply to the same things, and like signs to like things.

In this way, prodigies preceded many excellent men and kings at their birth or death. For instance, Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman and philosopher, particularly in his work *De Divinatione*.</note> records concerning the boy Midas <note>The legendary King of Phrygia, later famous for his "golden touch."</note> that while he was sleeping, ants dropped grains of wheat into his mouth, which was an omen of great wealth. Likewise, when bees settled on the mouth of Plato <note>The Greek philosopher.</note> while he slept in his cradle, the sweetness of his speech was foretold. To Hecuba, when she was about to give birth to Paris, it appeared in a vision that she gave birth to a burning torch which would set fire to Troy and all of Asia. To the mother of Phalaris <note>The tyrant of Akragas in Sicily, notorious for his cruelty.</note>, it appeared that a statue of Mercury poured blood upon the ground, with which the entire house was flooded. To the mother of Dionysius <note>Dionysius I, the tyrant of Syracuse.</note>, it appeared that she gave birth to a little satyr <note>original: *satyriscum* — a small satyr, a mythological creature associated with wild nature and unbridled impulses.</note>; the events themselves followed and confirmed these prodigious dreams. The wife of Tarquinius Priscus <note>The fifth king of Rome (r. 616–579 BCE).</note>, seeing a flame lick the head of Servius Tullius <note>The sixth king of Rome; the flame was a sign of his future divine favor and royalty.</note>, predicted his future kingship. In a similar way, after the capture of Troy, while Aeneas was debating with his father Anchises

[...Dionysius it appeared that she gave birth to a satyr.] While his father <note>Aeneas, the legendary hero of Troy.</note> was preparing for flight, a flame appeared licking the crown of Ascanius’s head <note>Ascanius was the son of Aeneas; this omen is famously described in Virgil’s Aeneid.</note> without causing him any harm. Because this portended kingship for Ascanius, it persuaded them all to depart, and thus notable disasters were anticipated and avoided through portents and prodigies.

So we read in Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 CE), the Roman author of Natural History.</note> that during the consulship of Marcus Atilius and Gaius Porcius, it rained milk and blood, which foretold that a powerful plague would invade Rome the following year. Likewise, during the consulship of Lucius Paulus and Gaius Marcellus, it rained wool around the fortress of Corisanum, near where Titus Annius Milo was killed a year later. Also, during the Cimbrian Wars <note>A series of battles fought between Rome and Germanic tribes around 113–101 BCE.</note>, the clatter of weapons and the blast of a trumpet were heard from the sky. Livy <note>Titus Livius, the preeminent Roman historian.</note>, writing of the Macedonian War, says that in the year Hannibal departed, it rained blood for two days. The same author reports concerning the Second Punic War <note>The conflict (218–201 BCE) in which Carthage, led by Hannibal, nearly defeated Rome.</note> that water mixed with blood descended from the sky like rain at the time when Hannibal was ravaging Italy.

To the Spartans, shortly before the disaster at Leuctra <note>The Battle of Leuctra (371 BCE), where Sparta’s military supremacy was broken by Thebes.</note>, armor rang out in the shrine of Hercules; and at the same time in Thebes, in the temple of Hercules, the doors, though closed with bolts, opened by themselves, and armor fixed to the walls was found lying on the ground. It is fitting to predict similar outcomes from similar events, just as something was once foretold from them at various times in the past. But it is also necessary concerning these matters to judge the influence of the celestial influxes...

## Chapter 57: On Geomancy, Hydromancy, Aeromancy, and Pyromancy: Divinations of the Four Elements

Now, even the elements themselves teach us of fateful events, from which those four famous types of divination—Geomancy, Hydromancy, Aeromancy, and Pyromancy—take their names. Regarding these, in the works of Lucan <note>Lucan (39–65 CE) was a Roman poet; this refers to his epic poem Pharsalia.</note>, that sorceress <note>Erictho, a powerful Thessalian witch in Lucan's poem known for her necromancy.</note> is seen to boast when she says:

> "To the truth, the earth, the air, the ether, and the chaos, the seas, the fields, and the rocks of Rhodope <note>A mountain range in Southeast Europe, associated in myth with Orpheus and Dionysus.</note> shall speak."
<note>original: "Ad uerum tellus, aëráq;, ætheráq;, chaosq;, Aequoráq;, & campi, rhodopeiaq; saxa loquentur."</note>

First, therefore, is **Geomancy** <note>From the Greek *ge* (earth) and *manteia* (prophecy).</note>, which forewarns of the future through earthquakes, rumblings, swellings, tremors, fissures, chasms, exhalations, and other such impressions of the earth. The art of this was handed down by Almadel the Arab <note>A legendary figure to whom several medieval magical texts, such as the *Ars Almadel*, were attributed.</note>. But there is also another species of Geomancy which divines through points inscribed upon the earth by some force or by chance; this does not belong to the present investigation, but we shall speak of it among the "lots" <term>Sortes: a system of divination using randomized symbols or casting lots</term> in the following sections.

**Hydromancy** <note>Prophecy by means of water.</note>, however, provides prophecies through watery impressions: their ebbs and flows, rises and falls, storms, colors, and similar phenomena. To this are also joined visions that occur in the water. This was a type of divination discovered by the Persians, such as Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BCE), an influential Roman scholar.</note> relates, where a boy saw an image of Mercury in the water...

We also read that Numa Pompilius <note>The legendary second king of Rome, credited with establishing many of the city's religious rites.</note> practiced **Hydromancy**; for he used to summon the images of gods in the water and learn of future things from them. Pythagoras also practiced this art a long time after Numa. There was also once among the Assyrians a type of hydromancy held in great esteem, called **Lecanomancy** <term>Lecanomancy: from the Greek *lekanē* (basin), a method of divination involving the observation of water in a bowl.</term>, named after a basin filled with water, into which plates of gold and silver and precious stones were placed, inscribed with certain images, names, and characters. To this may also be referred that art by which things we desire to know are expressed through manifest signs and images when molten lead or wax is poured into water.

In a similar way, **Aeromancy** <note>Divination by means of the air or atmospheric phenomena.</note> provides prognostications through aerial impressions: through the blowing of winds, through rainbows, through halos, through mists and clouds, and through shapes imagined in the clouds and visions seen in the air. 

Thus also **Pyromancy** <note>Divination by means of fire.</note> divines through fiery impressions: through comets <term>Stella caudata: literally "tailed star," the historical term for a comet.</term>, through fiery colors, and through visions and shapes imagined in the fire. In this way, Cicero’s wife <note>Terentia; this omen occurred during the festival of the *Bona Dea*.</note> predicted that he would be consul the following year because, when she wished to observe something in the ashes after a sacrifice had been performed, a flame suddenly leaped up. Of this kind are those things which Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist and author of *Natural History*.</note> mentions: that earthy fires which are pale and murmuring are perceived as messengers of storms; also rain, if "fungi" <note>original: "fungi." In this context, it refers to the carbon crust or "snuff" that forms on a lamp wick, which was thought to predict rain.</note> appear on lamps; if the flame flickers in a winding way, it portends wind; and when lamps...

### On the revival of the dead, on long-lived sleep, and fasting. Chapter 58.

Arab philosophers <note>A reference to thinkers like Avicenna (Ibn Sina), whose work on the "Prophetic Soul" suggested that the human mind could influence physical matter and transcend biological limits.</note> agree that some men can raise themselves above the powers of the body and above the sensitive powers <note>In the psychology of the time, the "sensitive powers" referred to the senses and the animal instincts, as opposed to the higher rational soul.</note>, and having overcome these, they receive into themselves the perfections of heaven and a divine vigor from the Intelligences <term>Intelligences: In Renaissance philosophy, these are spiritual, non-material beings (often equated with angels) that govern the celestial spheres and mediate between God and the physical world.</term>. Since, therefore, the souls of all men are eternal, all spirits also obey these perfected souls. The Magi believe that perfected men

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's remarkable powers, specifically moving from the general concept of the soul's transcendence to its ability to perform the "vivification" or restoration of life to the dead or dying, citing both natural analogies and historical examples.</meta>

<header>ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY</header>

through the powers of one's own soul, it is possible to restore other inferior souls—already in a sense separated from their dying bodies—and to breathe life into them once again. This is not unlike how a slain weasel <note>Ancient and medieval folklore often attributed miraculous restorative powers to weasels.</note> is recalled to life by the breath and voice of its parent, or how lions give life to their lifeless offspring by breathing upon them <note>A common medieval belief was that lion cubs were born dead and "awakened" by the father's breath after three days.</note>. And since, as they say, all like things applied to like things are made of similar natures, every passive subject receiving the action of some agent into itself is also clothed in and naturalized to the nature of that agent. For this reason, they believe that certain herbs and magical preparations contribute significantly to this reviving <term>Vivification: The act of restoring life or animation to a body that appears dead.</term>, such as those said to be prepared from the ash of the **Phoenix** and the cast-off skins of serpents. To most, this might seem like a mere fable, and to many even impossible, were it not proven by historical record. For we read that many who were drowned in water, others cast into fires and placed upon the funeral pyre, others killed in war, and others otherwise deprived of breath, have returned to life even after several days. **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 CE), a Roman author who recorded many such "wonders" in his *Natural History*.</note> testifies to this regarding **Aviola**, a man of consular rank, as well as **L. Lamia**, **Caelius Tubero**, **Corfidius**, **Gabienus**, and many others. We likewise read that **Aesop the storyteller**, **Tyndareus** also, and **Hercules**, as well as the **Palici** <note>Ancient Sicilian twin deities born of Zeus (Jupiter) and the nymph Thalia; they were said to have been born twice.</note> (the sons of **Jupiter** and **Thalia**), lived again after death. Many others were also recalled to life by **Magi** and physicians, as has been handed down in history concerning **Asclepius** <note>The Greek god of medicine, who was said to have the power to raise the dead.</note>, and as we have narrated above from the accounts of **Juba**, **Xanthus**, and **Philostratus**, concerning **Tillo**, and a certain **Arab**, and **Apollonius of Tyana** <note>A famous Neopythagorean philosopher and miracle-worker of the 1st century CE.</note>.

[Luci]neo. Others say he came back to life after tasting a honeyed medicine, from which the proverb arose: *Glaucus rose again after drinking honey* <note>original: "Glaucus poto melle resurrexit". This refers to the myth of Glaucus, son of Minos, who drowned in a vat of honey and was revived.</note>. **Apuleius** <note>Lucius Apuleius (c. 124 – c. 170 CE), a Roman philosopher and prose writer.</note> also describes a ritual of this kind of summoning when speaking of **Iachla** <note>Commonly identified as Zatchlas in modern editions of Apuleius's *The Golden Ass*.</note>, an Egyptian prophet. He says: "The Prophet, having been thus appeased, placed a certain small herb upon the mouth of the body, and another upon its chest; then, turning toward the east, having silently prayed for the rising of the august sun, this venerable scene drew the attention of those present in anticipation of so great a miracle; soon the chest was swollen by a deep breath, the healthy pulse began to beat, the body was filled with spirit, and the corpse rose up, and the young man spoke." <note>This is a translation of the Latin passage from Apuleius's *Metamorphoses*, Book II.</note>

If these things are to be believed as true, it must be that dying souls sometimes hide within their bodies, overwhelmed by more violent <term>Ecstasies: States of being "outside" the body or in a deep trance where physical functions appear to stop.</term>, and are released from all bodily action: so much so that life, sense, and motion desert the entire body. However, the man is not yet truly dead, but lies breathless and as if dead, even for a long period of time. 

And this has very often been discovered during times of pestilence: namely, that many who were being carried out to be buried as dead revived in their tombs. We read that this has also happened many times to women suffering from **suffocation of the womb** <note>original: "suffocatione matricis"; an ancient medical term for a condition where the uterus was believed to move or compress, causing a state of apparent death or fainting.</note>. Furthermore, **Rabbi Moses** <note>Maimonides (1138–1204), the preeminent Jewish philosopher and physician.</note> relates from a book by **Galen** <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire.</note> (which the Patriarch translated), that a suffocation happened to a man which lasted for [several]

Indeed, I have often seen a dissected dormouse remain immobile as if dead until it was cooked; and only then, in the boiling water, did the dissected limbs show signs of life. Ecclesiastical histories confirm this opinion regarding the **Seven Sleepers** <note>A famous legend of a group of Christian youths who hid in a cave near Ephesus to escape persecution around 250 AD and awoke nearly two centuries later.</note>, whom they say slept for one hundred and ninety-six years.

There are also serious authors who describe a certain **Spartan herb**, with which they say the **Scythians** <note>Ancient nomadic people of Central Eurasia.</note> could endure twelve days without food or drink, having once tasted it or held it in the mouth.
