<header>BOOK I.</header>

[...as is handed down by] authorities, and proven by examples from history and daily experience. Now, I speak here of a **Dream**, not of a phantasm or an "idle vision" <term>insomnium: a dream caused by physical distress or remnants of waking thoughts, which lacks prophetic value</term>, for these are empty and possess nothing of divination. Instead, they are born from the remnants of our waking hours or from a disturbance of the body. For as often as we are weighed down by the body's comforts or troubles, or by our worldly fortune, the same matters that fatigued the mind while awake thrust themselves upon the sleeper; or sometimes, the dreamer is deceived by their opposite. 

I say here that a true dream occurs in the **imaginative spirit** <term>spiritu phantastico: the "spirit" or subtle substance within the brain where images are processed; the seat of the imagination</term> when the mind and body are in good condition, and is caused by the influx of the celestial bodies. A rule for interpreting these dreams is indeed found among **Astrologers** in that branch of the art titled "On Questions" <note>Refers to Horary Astrology, used to answer specific questions based on the time they are asked.</note>, yet it is not entirely sufficient. This is because dreams of this kind come to different men in different ways, according to the varied quality and disposition of their imaginative spirits. 

For this reason, the same common rule for interpreting dreams cannot be applied to every individual. Instead, according to the opinion of **Synesius** <note>original: "Sinesij"; Synesius of Cyrene (c. 373–414 AD), a Neoplatonist philosopher and bishop who argued that every person must develop their own "dream-diary" to understand their unique symbolic language.</note>, since the same circumstances follow the same things, and like follows like, he who has frequently fallen into the same or a similar vision should designate for himself the same or a similar meaning, passion, fortune, action, or event. 

Just as **Aristotle** <note>Refers to Aristotle's *Posterior Analytics*, describing the cognitive journey from perception to science.</note> says: memory is confirmed by the senses; from the memory of the same thing frequently obtained, experience is born; and from many experiences, art and science are gradually accumulated. By a similar reasoning, regarding dreams, it will be necessary to be...

From this accumulation, a specific art of divination and a method for interpreting one’s own dreams gradually arises, provided nothing slips from the memory. However, the truest and most certain divination comes neither from nature nor human arts, but proceeds from divine inspiration into purified minds. ### Chapter 60.

It happens that not only those who are sleeping, but also those who are awake, can sometimes divine when the soul is set free <term>soluto animo: a state where the soul is released from its usual preoccupation with the senses or the body's physical needs</term> and driven by its own impulses. **Aristotle** calls this type of divination "frenzy" <term>furorem: a state of inspired madness or divine agitation</term>, and he teaches in his treatise on divination that it proceeds from the **melancholic humor** <note>One of the four "bodily fluids" in ancient medicine; black bile was believed to cause deep contemplation, sadness, or, in this context, heightened spiritual sensitivity.</note>. He says that melancholic individuals, because of their intensity, are very good at guessing and quickly form mental images of a condition.

<header>BOOK I.</header>

[ima]gined, and they most easily receive the impression of celestial things. And in the *Problems* <note>Aristotle's *Problemata*, specifically Book XXX, which deals with melancholy and genius.</note> he says that the **Sibyls**, the **Bacchides** <note>Inspired prophets or followers of Bacchus/Dionysus.</note>, **Niceratus of Syracuse**, and **Ammon** were diviners and poets by their natural melancholic complexion. Therefore, if there is a cause of frenzy within the human body, it is the melancholic humor. This is not, however, that substance called **black bile** <note>original: "atra bilis"</note>, which is such a corrupt and horrible thing that its force is said by natural philosophers and physicians to not only cause mania, but even to entice evil demons to possess human bodies.

Therefore, the humor we mean here is the melancholic one which is called the natural and **white bile** <note>original: "candida bilis"; a term used by Renaissance philosophers like Marsilio Ficino to describe a "cleansed" or "illustrious" form of melancholy that aids intellectual work.</note>. For when this is ignited and burns, it excites a frenzy that leads us toward knowledge and divination, especially if it is aided by some celestial influence—above all that of **Saturn**. Since Saturn himself is cold and dry, just like the melancholic humor, he daily flows into it, increases it, and preserves it.

Furthermore, since he [Saturn] is the author of secret contemplation, a stranger to all public business, and the highest of the planets, he both continually recalls the soul from external duties to its innermost depths and makes it ascend from lower things, drawing it toward the highest heights. He bestows sciences and foreknowledge of the future. This is what **Aristotle** intends in his book of *Problems*, saying: *From melancholy, some have become like divine beings, predicting the future, and some have become poets.* He says, moreover, that all men who have been outstanding in any science have for the most part been melan-

166
<header>ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY</header>

...melancholics, to whom even **Democritus** and **Plato** bear witness, asserting that some melancholics so excel in talent that they seem divine rather than human. Thus many melancholics, who were at first unrefined, inept, or even appeared insane—such as they say **Hesiod**, **Tynnichus** <note>original: "Iouem Thynnicum"; referring to Tynnichus of Chalcis, a poet whom Plato mentions in the *Ion* as having written a famous paean by divine inspiration despite having no other talent.</note>, **Kalanos** <note>original: "Calanensem"; Kalanos was an Indian gymnosophist who accompanied Alexander the Great and was famous for his prophetic powers.</note>, **Homer**, and **Lucretius** were—are often seized by a sudden frenzy and emerge as poets. They sing certain wondrous and divine things, which they themselves barely understand.

Wherefore the divine **Plato** says in the *Ion* that most prophets, after the impulse of the frenzy has subsided, do not sufficiently understand what they have written, even though while in that frenzy they treated individual arts correctly—as the experts in those specific arts can judge by reading their works. Furthermore, they teach that the power of the melancholic humor is so great that by its force even <term>celestial spirits</term> <note>original: "caelestes dæmones"; in this context, "daemons" refers to mediating spirits or guiding intelligences that bridge the gap between the divine and the human, rather than "demons" in the modern malevolent sense.</note> are sometimes drawn into human bodies. All antiquity testifies that by the presence and instinct of these spirits, men rave <note>original: "debacchari"; to be filled with the enthusiasm of Bacchus, a state of ecstatic frenzy.</note> and speak many wonders. They believe this happens in three different ways, according to the three types of the soul’s apprehension: namely, the **imaginative**, the **rational**, and the **mental**.

They say, therefore, that when the soul is driven by the melancholic humor and—with nothing restraining it—transgresses the reins of the body and the chains of the limbs, and is entirely transferred into the **imagination**, it suddenly becomes the dwelling place of lower spirits. From these it often receives wonderful methods of <term>manual arts</term> <note>Crafts and technical skills involving physical creation.</note>. Thus we see some most unrefined man suddenly become an excellent painter, or an architect, or a master of some other art...

...to emerge as a master. When, however, spirits <note>original: "dæmones"; here referring to mediating entities or "daemons" that bridge the divine and human realms, rather than malevolent beings.</note> of this kind portend future events to us, they show those things which pertain to the disturbances of the elements and the changes of the seasons, such as future rain, storms, floods, earthquakes, plagues, famine, slaughter, and the like. This is just as we read in **Aulus Gellius** <note>A 2nd-century Roman author of the *Attic Nights*, a collection of notes on grammar, philosophy, and history.</note> regarding the priest **Cornelius**. At the time when **Caesar** and **Pompey** were clashing in **Thessaly**, Cornelius was seized by a frenzy in Padua and foretold the time, the order, and the outcome of the battle. 

But when the soul is entirely turned toward reason, it becomes the dwelling place of <term>middle spirits</term>. From this, it gains knowledge and wisdom concerning natural and human affairs. Thus we see some man suddenly emerge as an excellent philosopher, physician, or orator. Regarding the future, such a person predicts things pertaining to the changes of kingdoms and the restoration of ages, just as the **Sibyl** <note>The Sibyls were ancient prophetesses of the Greco-Roman world; their "Sibylline Books" were consulted by the Roman state during crises.</note> did for the Romans. 

When, however, the soul has risen entirely into the mind, it is then made a receptacle for <term>sublime spirits</term>. From them, it learns the secrets of divine things—namely, the law of God, the orders of angels, and those things which pertain to the knowledge of eternal matters and the salvation of souls. Regarding the future, it foresees those things that lie within divine providence, such as future portents or miracles, a future Prophet, or a change in the Law. In this way, the Sibyls prophesied concerning **Christ** a long time before his coming. Thus **Virgil**, understanding that Christ was soon to be born and was already near, and remembering the words of the **Cumaean Sibyl**, sang to **Pollio**: <note>This refers to Virgil’s Fourth Eclogue, which many Renaissance scholars interpreted as a pagan prophecy of the birth of Jesus.</note>

"With you as leader, if any traces of our crime remain,

The lands, the reaches of the sea, and the deep heaven;

O may the last part of my long life remain to me, 
And enough breath to tell of your deeds!"

<meta>This page continues the discussion of divination and the soul's prophetic abilities, specifically focusing on the "middle state" between natural and supernatural insight. It transitions from the poetic prophecies of Virgil into a more philosophical and theological examination of how the human body and soul were formed. The catchword "boundaries" from the previous page leads directly into the discussion of the "boundaries of natural and supernatural divination."</meta>

BOOK I.

boundaries between natural and supernatural divination are the middle states, such as in those who are close to death or weakened by old age; they sometimes foresee future things because, as Plato says in the *Republic*, those who are less hindered by the senses understand more sharply. This is because they are closer to the place where they are about to migrate; with their bonds now somewhat loosened, they are no longer entirely subject to the body, and they easily receive the lights of divine revelation.

### On the formation of man, on the external and internal senses, and the mind: on the threefold appetite of the soul, and the passions of the will. Chapter 61.

It is the opinion of certain theologians that God himself did not immediately create the body of the first man, but composed and formed it from the elements with the assistance of the heavenly beings <note>original: "cœlitum adiutorio"; referring to angels or celestial spirits.</note>. This opinion is also supported by Alcinous <note>A Middle Platonist philosopher, author of "The Handbook of Platonism."</note> based on the teachings of Plato, believing that the supreme God is the creator of the entire world, the gods, and the demons, and that those beings are therefore immortal. However, he believed that the junior gods <note>original: "iuniores deos"; in Platonic thought (specifically the Timaeus), these are lesser deities created by the Demiurge who then fashion the mortal parts of the human soul and body.</note> procreated the other types of mortal living things at the command of the supreme God. For if God himself had produced these as well, they would have been born immortal. Thus, the gods, borrowing certain portions from earth and fire, air and water, and binding them together, fashioned one body out of them all, which they subjected to the service of the soul, assigning specific provinces <note>original: "prouincias"; here used to mean specific regions or functional areas of the body.</note> within it to each of the soul's powers <note>original: "potentijs"; refers to the various faculties or capacities of the soul, such as reason, memory, or the senses.</note>.

<meta>catchword: marking (signantes)</meta>

<summary>This page discusses how the loosening of physical bonds through age or impending death allows for prophetic foresight and examines the Platonic theory that lesser deities, rather than the supreme God, fashioned the mortal human body from the four elements.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "junior gods" (lesser deities in Platonic thought) who were tasked with creating the mortal parts of the human being. ->ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY<-

...assigning <note>The previous page ends with "procreate man," so this begins: "...assigning [the parts of man]..."</note> more lowly seats to the lower faculties. For instance, they assigned the diaphragm <note>original: "præcordia"; literally the area around the heart, considered the seat of anger or spirit.</note> to anger, and the belly <note>original: "uterum"; here used broadly for the abdomen or gut, the seat of physical desire.</note> to desire; but the nobler senses they placed in the head, as if in the citadel of the whole body, followed by the various organs of speech.

They divide the senses into the external and the internal. The external senses are further partitioned into the five most familiar to everyone, to which five organs or subjects are assigned as their foundations. These are so arranged that those placed in the higher parts of the body possess a higher degree of purity. 

For the eyes, located in the highest position, are the purest and are kindred to the nature of fire and light. Next, the ears, holding the second rank in both location and purity, are compared to air. The nostrils occupy the third level, existing as a medium between air and water. Following these is the organ of taste, which is denser and most like the nature of water. Finally, the sense of touch, diffused throughout the whole body, is assigned to the density of earth. 

The purer senses are those that perceive from a distance without coming into direct contact with their objects, such as sight and hearing. Smell also perceives distant things through the medium of air. Taste, however, only senses things that are close at hand. Touch functions in both ways: it feels bodies that approach it, and just as sight sees through the medium of air, touch can also feel hard, soft, and wet objects through an intervening rod or staff <note>Agrippa is referencing a common philosophical idea that sensing through a tool is an extension of the sense of touch.</note>. Indeed, touch is the only sense common to all animals; and this sense is considered the most certain...

<summary>This page explains the hierarchical arrangement of the human senses, mapping them to the four elements (fire, air, water, and earth) and their physical locations in the body. Agrippa argues that higher senses like sight are "purer" because they perceive objects from a distance, while touch is the most fundamental and certain sense.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the human senses. The previous page concluded by describing how the sense of touch is the most physical and dense, distributed throughout the whole body.</meta>

# BOOK I.

...[close at hand, which] man possesses: for in this <note>Referring to the sense of touch discussed at the end of the previous page.</note> and in taste, man excels the other animals. However, in the other three senses, he is surpassed by certain creatures—just as by the dog, who sees, hears, and smells more acutely than man. Similarly, lynxes and eagles see more sharply than man and many other living beings.

The internal senses, however, as Averroes <note>Ibn Rushd (1126–1198), an influential Almohad philosopher known for his commentaries on Aristotle.</note> maintains, are divided into four parts. The first is called the **common sense** <note>original: "communis"; the faculty that unites the disparate data from the five external senses into a single perception.</note>, because it is the first to collect and perfect all the images that are drawn in through the external senses. 

The second is the **imaginative power** <note>original: "uis imaginatiua"</note>; its duty is to retain the images received from the preceding senses—even though it perceives nothing directly—and to offer them to the third nature of sensing. This third faculty is **phantasy** <note>original: "phantasia"; in this context, it refers to the "estimative" power or the ability to process and think about perceptions.</note>, which is the power of evaluation and active thought. 

Its work is to take the received images and perceive and judge what the object is, or what qualities it possesses. Once it has distinguished, combined, perceived, and judged these things, it entrusts them to be preserved by the fourth power, which we call **memory**. 

Indeed, within its general scope are the faculties of reasoning <note>original: "discursus"; the logical movement of the mind from one idea to another.</note>, arrangement, pursuit and avoidance, and the impulses to act. But specifically regarding intellectual matters, it governs understandings, virtues, the logic of the sciences, and the choice of causes. Furthermore, it is this faculty which shows us future things through dreams; for this reason, phantasy is sometimes called the **phantastic intellect**. It is the final trace of intelligence which—as Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who integrated Greek philosophy with mystical and theurgic practices.</note> says—is innate to all the powers of the soul, shaping all figures, the likenesses of species, and their operations...

### ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY.

...and perceptions, or transmits the impressions of some powers into others. Indeed, those things which flash from the senses, it stirs up into opinion <note>original: "opinionem"; in Renaissance psychology, this refers to the level of belief or judgment formed from sensory data.</note>; those things which come from the intellect, it offers in the second place to opinion. But from itself, it receives images from all things and, through its own nature, designates them specifically according to their likeness. It shapes all the actions of the soul and adapts external things to internal ones, even imprinting these impressions upon the body.

Furthermore, these senses have their own organs in the head. For the **common sense**, along with the **imagination**, occupies the front cells of the brain <note>original: "cerebri cellulas"; referring to the medieval "cell doctrine" which located mental faculties in specific ventricles of the brain.</note>—though Aristotle placed the organ of the common sense in the heart. The **cogitative power**, however, occupies the highest and middle part of the head, and then **memory** occupies the hindmost part. 

Furthermore, the organs of the voice and of speech are many: namely, the muscles between the ribs of the inner chest, the thorax, the lungs, the windpipe <note>original: "trachea arteria"</note>, the throat, and especially the cartilaginous part of these <note>The larynx.</note>, following the recurrent nerves, and the "plectrum" of the tongue <note>original: "plectrum linguae"; the author uses the metaphor of a plectrum used to pluck a lyre.</note>, and all these small parts and muscles which are the organs of exhalation. 

But the proper organ of speech is the mouth, in which words and talk are molded, formed, and figured. In this, the tongue and the throat <note>original: "gurgulione"; likely referring to the uvula or the gullet.</note> take the place of the plectrum, while the palate produces the sound. The opening of the teeth and the mouth bear a resemblance to the strings of a lyre, and the nose moreover contributes to a resonance that is either good or bad. 

Above the sensible soul, which unfolds its powers through the organs of the body, the incorporeal mind itself occupies the highest place, and this...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the nature of the mind and the soul’s faculties. The opening word "minam" is the conclusion of "geminam" (twin/double) from the preceding sentence.</meta>

### BOOK I.

...having a double nature <note>original: "geminam naturam"; the author is describing the two-fold function of the human intellect.</note>: One which investigates the causes, properties, and progressions of those things contained within the order of nature, and is satisfied in the contemplation of truth; for this reason, they call it the **contemplative intellect**. 

The other nature or power of the mind, which discerns through deliberation what things should be done and what should be avoided, is entirely engaged in deliberation and action; for this reason, they call it the **active intellect**. 

Therefore, nature established this order of powers in man, so that through the **exterior senses** we might know bodily things; through the **interior senses**, beyond this, we perceive the likenesses of bodies; and further, through the **mind** or **intellect**, we grasp abstract things which are neither bodies nor similar to them. 

And according to this threefold order of the soul's powers, three **appetites** <note>In this context, "appetite" refers to a natural drive or inclination toward a goal, rather than just a hunger for food.</note> arise in the soul: 

1.  The first is **natural**, which is a certain inclination of nature toward its own end—such as a stone falling downward—which is present in all things. 
2.  The second is **animal**, which follows the senses and is divided into the **irascible** <note>The "spirited" faculty used to overcome obstacles or resist threats.</note> and the **concupiscible** <note>The faculty of desire, seeking what is perceived as pleasant or good for the body.</note>. 
3.  The third is **intellectual**, which is called the **will** <note>original: "uoluntas"</note>. 

This intellectual appetite differs from the sensitive appetite because the sensitive appetite is, by its nature, directed toward those things which can be offered to the senses, desiring nothing unless it is perceived in some way. The **will**, however, although it is of itself directed toward all possible things, because it is free by its very essence, can even be directed toward impossible things—such as was the case with the demon <note>A reference to Lucifer’s pride and fall.</note> desiring himself to be equal to God. 

Thus, the will is continually altered and corrupted by pleasure and pain while it gives its assent to the inferior powers. From this corrupted appetite, four [passions] arise in it...

<summary>This page describes the hierarchy of the human mind, distinguishing between the contemplative and active intellect, and categorizes the three types of "appetite" (natural, animal, and intellectual/will) that drive human action.</summary>
<keywords>contemplative intellect, active intellect, interior senses, natural appetite, animal appetite, will, irascible faculty, concupiscible faculty, psychology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 173 regarding the intellectual appetite or the "will." The text begins mid-sentence, concluding the thought that the intellectual appetite is the source of various passions that affect the body.</meta>

174
->ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY<-
<meta>catchword: Concerning the passions</meta>

passions <note>original: "passiones"; in this context, "passions" refers to intense emotions or "movements of the soul" that can overpower reason.</note>, by which the body is likewise sometimes affected. The first of these is called *delight* <note>original: "oblectatio"</note>, which is a certain softness and assent of the mind or will; by this, the mind most willingly consents, obeys, and yields to those things the senses offer for its pleasure. For this reason, they define it as an inclination of the soul toward a softening pleasure. 

The second, however, is called *effusion* <note>original: "effusio"; literally an "outpouring," describing a state where the mind's focus and strength dissolve into the object of its desire.</note>, which is a relaxation or dissolution of virtue. This occurs when, clearly moving beyond mere delight, the entire power and intention of the mind melts and flows away through the sweetness of a present good, thoroughly spreading itself out and carrying itself toward the enjoyment of that thing. 

The third is called *vaunting* <note>original: "iactantia"; also translated as boasting or exultation.</note>, which is a triumphant elation. It arises when the mind believes it has achieved some great good; in the possession of this, it carries itself quite insolently, exults, and glories in vain. 

The fourth and last is *malevolence* <note>original: "maleuolentia"</note>, which is a certain pleasure taken from the misfortune of another without any gain to oneself. It is specifically said to be "without gain" because if someone rejoices in another’s misfortune for their own utility, this would stem from benevolence toward oneself <note>The author argues that self-interest is a form of self-love, whereas true malevolence is pure spite without personal profit.</note> rather than malevolence toward others. 

And just as these four passions are born from a depraved appetite for pleasure, pain itself generates just as many contrary passions: namely *horror* <note>original: "horror"; a visceral dread or shivering fear.</note>, *sadness*, *fear*, and that pain felt at the good fortune of another which brings no loss to oneself—which we call *envy* <note>original: "inuidia"</note>. This is to say, envy is sadness at the goods of others, just as *mercy* <note>original: "misericordia"; also "compassion" or "pity."</note> is a certain sadness felt at the misfortunes of others.

<summary>This page categorizes the "passions of the soul," defining four types arising from the pursuit of pleasure (delight, effusion, vaunting, malevolence) and four from the experience of pain (horror, sadness, fear, envy).</summary>
<keywords>passions, psychology, delight, effusion, vaunting, malevolence, envy, mercy, Renaissance philosophy</keywords>

### *Concerning the passions of the soul, and their origin, difference, and species*
### Chapter 62.

The passions of the soul <note>original: "passiones animi"; in this era, "passions" refers to any emotion or mental state where the soul is "acted upon" by an external stimulus.</note> are nothing other than certain movements or inclinations arising from the apprehension <note>original: "apprehensione"; the act of the mind grasping or perceiving an object.</note> of a thing as either good or evil, suitable or unsuitable. Perceptions of this kind are threefold: namely, sensual, rational, and mental. According to these, there are three types of passions in the soul. 

Sometimes they follow sensitive perception; in this case, they regard temporal good or evil in terms of convenience or inconvenience, pleasure or offense. These are called natural or animal passions. 

Sometimes they follow rational perception; thus, they regard good or evil in the light of virtue and vice, praise and blame, the useful and the useless, or the honorable and the base. These are called rational or voluntary passions. 

Sometimes they follow mental perception and regard good and evil in the light of the just and the unjust, or the true and the false. These are called intellectual passions, or <term>syndereses</term> <note>original: "syndereses"; a technical term in medieval philosophy for the innate spark of conscience that allows a person to instinctively know right from wrong.</note>. 

The subject <note>In the sense of the "seat" or "location" within the soul.</note> of the passions of the soul is the desiring power of the soul itself. This is divided into the <term>concupiscible</term> <note>the faculty that seeks simple pleasures and avoids pain.</note> and the <term>irascible</term> <note>the faculty that pursues difficult goods or resists threats through struggle or "spirit."</note>. Both of these regard good and evil, but in different ways. For the concupiscible power sometimes regards good and evil in an absolute sense, and in this way love <note>original: "amor"</note> or lust <note>original: "libido"</note> is caused, and conversely, hatred <note>original: "odium"</note>; or [regards...]

<summary>This page establishes a psychological framework for human emotions, categorizing them into sensual, rational, and intellectual "passions" based on how we perceive the world. It further divides the soul's desires into the faculties of simple attraction (concupiscible) and spirited struggle (irascible).</summary>

<keywords>passions of the soul, perception, synderesis, concupiscible, irascible, moral philosophy, psychology, virtue and vice</keywords>

CONCERNING OCCULT PHILOSOPHY

[...it] regards <note>The text begins with "spicit," the completion of the Latin word "respicit" from the previous page.</note> good as absent, and thus is caused cupidity or desire; or it regards evil as absent or imminent, and thus is caused horror, flight, and abomination. Or it regards good and evil as present and attained, and thus from the former arises delight, joy, and pleasure, and from the latter, sadness, distress, and pain. The <term>irascible power</term> <note>original: "vis irascibilis"; in medieval psychology, this is the faculty of the soul that deals with "difficult" goods or evils—those that require a struggle to obtain or avoid.</note>, however, regards good and evil under the aspect of difficulty in attaining or obtaining them, or in fleeing or driving them away. And this occurs sometimes with confidence, and thus is caused hope on one hand, and boldness on the other; sometimes it occurs with a lack of confidence, and thus arises despair and dread or fear. Sometimes the irascible power itself rises toward vengeance, and this happens only regarding a past evil, such as an inflicted injury or hurt, and thus is caused anger. And so we find eleven passions in the soul itself, which are: love, hatred, desire, horror, joy, pain, hope, despair, boldness, fear, and anger.

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the internal passions of the soul (concluded on the previous page) to the external power of speech and names. It explores how words, when aligned with celestial harmony and the true nature of things, possess the power to effect change in the physical world.</meta>

BOOK I.

Recognizing, therefore, these celestial influences and the properties of individual things, the **first-formed man** <note>original: "protoplastes"; referring to Adam as the first created human.</note> assigned names to things according to their essences, just as it is written in **Genesis**: because God brought all things He had created before Adam so that he might name them, and as he himself called a thing, that was its name. These names certainly contain within themselves the marvelous powers of the things signified. Therefore, every meaningful voice [or word] signifies first through the influence of **celestial harmony**, and secondarily through the imposition of the name by man, although more frequently it happens differently through one or the other. 

However, when both significations concur in any voice or name—those which come from harmony and those imposed by men—then with a doubled virtue (namely, the natural and the voluntary), that name is rendered most effective for action. This occurs whenever it is spoken in the proper places and times, and with the required solemnity and exact intention, upon matter that is prepared and by its nature capable of being affected by it. 

Thus we read in the works of **Philostratus** <note>Flavius Philostratus (c. 170–250 AD), a Greek scholar who wrote the biography of the miracle-worker Apollonius.</note> that when a certain girl at Rome, having died on her wedding day, was brought to **Apollonius** <note>Apollonius of Tyana, a first-century philosopher and reputed magician often compared to Jesus in antiquity.</note>, he touched the maiden and accurately inquired after her name. Once he knew it, he pronounced something secret, by which the girl revived. There was also a practice among the Romans in their sacred rites that when they besieged any city, they would diligently seek out its proper name, and the name of that divinity under whose protection that city was placed. Once these were known, they would then, by a certain incantation, [evoke] the **guardian gods** of that city...

->*On complex speeches and chants,*<-
->*and the virtues and bindings*<-

Beyond the virtues of words and names, another greater virtue is found in **complex speeches** <note>original: "orationibus complexis"; sentences, phrases, or verses as opposed to single nouns.</note>, arising from the truth contained within them. This truth has the greatest power for impressing, changing, binding, and establishing, to such an extent that when it is agitated, it shines more brightly; when attacked, it becomes more firm and consolidated. This virtue of truth is not found in simple words, but in **propositions** <note>original: "enunciationibus"; logical statements or declarations.</note> in which something is affirmed or denied. Of this kind are chants, incantations, imprecations, deprecations, prayers, invocations, entreaties, adjurations, exorcisms, and the like. Therefore, in composing chants and prayers for attracting the virtue of a star or some deity, it is necessary to diligently consider what [qualities] <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword "bet" indicates the next page begins with "qualibet" (any/every).</note>

BOOK I.

...[the properties] which the star contains—its virtues, effects, and operations—and to insert these into **chants** <note>original: "carminibus"; these are magical poems or songs from which the word "charm" is derived.</note> by praising, extolling, amplifying, and adorning those things which a star of this kind is accustomed to bring and to influence; by demeaning and condemning those things which it is accustomed to destroy and hinder; by supplicating and entreating for that which we desire to obtain, and by accusing and detesting that which we wish to be destroyed and hindered; and for this reason to compose the prayer of the chant in an ornate and elegant style, properly divided into sections with specific and suitable rhythms and proportions. 

Furthermore, the **Magi** <note>Ancient Persian priests or wise men; here, the practitioners of high magic.</note> command that we must invoke and pray through the names of the same star or deity to which such a chant pertains, and through its wonders or miracles, through its courses and its paths in its sphere, through its light, through the nobility of its kingdom, through the grace and clarity that is within it, through its strong and wonderful virtues, and through things similar to these. Just as in **Apuleius** <note>Lucius Apuleius (c. 124–170 AD), a Roman writer and initiate into the mysteries; this quote comes from his famous novel *The Golden Ass*.</note>, Psyche prays to **Ceres** <note>The Roman goddess of agriculture and the harvest.</note>:

> *I implore you,* she says, *by your fruit-bearing right hand, I earnestly pray by the joy-bringing* <note>original: "letiferas" (usually meaning "death-bearing"), but in context of Ceres it is likely a variant of "laetiferas" (joyous/fruitful).</note> *ceremonies of the harvests, by the silent secrets of the chests* <note>original: "cistarum"; these were sacred containers used to carry secret objects in mystery cults.</note>, *by the winged chariots of your servant dragons, by the furrows of the Sicilian soil, by the grasping chariot* <note>Refers to the chariot of Pluto, who abducted Ceres' daughter, Proserpina.</note>, *and the clinging earth, and the descents to the sunless nuptials of **Proserpina** <note>The daughter of Ceres and Queen of the Underworld.</note>, and the return journeys of the daughter’s luminous discoveries, and the other things that **Eleusis** <note>The Greek site of the Eleusinian Mysteries, the most famous secret religious rites of the ancient world.</note>, the sanctuary of Attica, covers in silence...*

furthermore, with the many-jointed names of the stars. # ON OCCULT PHILOSOPHY

->BOOK I.<-

...presiding over the stars, about which we will speak more extensively in its proper place. But he who desires more ample examples of these things, let him search the hymns of **Orpheus** <note>The legendary Greek poet and musician whose hymns were considered foundational texts for Western mystery traditions and magic.</note>. But let us return to our purpose. Therefore, **chants** <note>original: "carmina"; these are magical poems, songs, or incantations from which the word "charm" is derived.</note> of this kind, aptly and properly composed according to the pattern of the stars, full of intellect and sense, and pronounced with vehement emotion at the right time—both according to their meanings and according to the number and proportion of their segments, as well as the form resulting from those segments together—and through the force of the imagination, they unite with a great power within the enchanter. Immediately after, they transfer that power into the thing being enchanted, in order to bind it or direct it toward the goal intended by the enchanter's emotions and words. 

The instrument of these incantations is a certain most pure **spirit** <note>In Renaissance philosophy, the "spiritus" was a subtle, invisible substance—a vapor of the blood—that acted as a bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</note> that is harmonic, warm, breathing, and living, carrying with it movement, emotion, and meaning. It is composed of its own parts, endowed with sense, and finally conceived by reason. Therefore, through the quality of this spirit and through its celestial likeness—besides those things already mentioned and from the opportunity of the right timing—these chants conceive most excellent virtues from heaven. These are indeed much more sublime and effective than the spirits and vapors exhaling from vegetable life, such as from herbs, roots, gums, spices, **incense** <note>original: "suffitibus"; the smoke produced by burning aromatic substances in rituals to attract specific spirits or planetary influences.</note>, and other similar things. 

And for this reason, the **Magi** <note>Ancient Persian priests or wise men; here, the learned practitioners of high magic.</note>, when enchanting, are accustomed to breathe upon the objects and to "inhale" the words of the chant, as if the virtue were being breathed in by the spirit itself. In this way, the entire power of the **soul** <note>original: "animae"</note> is directed into the thing enchanted, so that it may receive the said virtue...

<meta>catchword: disposed.</meta>

<summary>This page describes the mechanics of magical incantations, explaining how the "spirit" acts as a bridge to transmit a practitioner's intent and celestial power into an object. Agrippa argues that these spoken charms, when properly timed and structured, are more potent than physical ingredients like herbs or incense.</summary>

disposed. And here it must be noted that every speech, writing, and word—just as they induce their accustomed movements through their usual numbers, proportions, and form—so also when they are uttered or written outside the usual order or in a **retrograde** <note>backward or reversed; in magic, doing things in reverse was often thought to undo or subvert the natural order.</note> fashion, they move toward unusual effects.

## On the Marvelous Power of Incantations, Chapter 72.

They say that the power of **incantations** <note>original: "incantamentorum"; refers to ritual chants intended to produce a magical effect.</note> or songs is so great that they are believed to be able to subvert almost all of nature. As **Apuleius** <note>A 2nd-century Roman philosopher and author of "The Golden Ass," who was famously accused of practicing magic.</note> says: by a magical whispering, swift rivers are turned back, the sluggish sea is bound, the winds expire in unison, the sun is inhibited, the moon is made to foam <note>A reference to "lunar foam," a substance ancient witches supposedly drew from the moon during eclipses.</note>, the stars are plucked out, the day is taken away, and the night is held back. Of these things, **Lucan** <note>A Roman poet whose epic "Pharsalia" contains a famous scene involving the witch Erichtho.</note> sings:

> The successions of things have ceased, and the day,
> Delayed by a long night, has lingered; the heavens
> Did not obey their law, and the headlong world
> Grew numb upon hearing the song.
> <note>original: "Cessauere uices rerum, dilataq; longa, / Hæsit nocte dies, legi non paruit æther, / Torpuit & præceps audito carmine mundus"</note>

->And a little before,<-

> The song of the Thessalian women <note>Thessaly was a region in Greece famous in antiquity for its powerful witches.</note> has flowed
> Into hard hearts; love, not sufficiently induced,
> <note>original: "Carmen Thessalidum dura in præcordia fluxit, / Non satis adductus amor"</note>

->And elsewhere,<-

> A mind polluted by no gore of swallowed poison
> Perishes, enchanted.
> <note>original: "Mens hausti nulla sanie polluta ueneni, / Excantata perit"</note>

->Likewise **Virgil** in the song of Damon,<-

> Songs can even draw down the moon from heaven.
> <note>original: "Carmina uel cælo possunt deducere lunam"</note>

> Acorns fall from the holm-oaks and grapes from the enchanted vines,
> And the fruits fall away with no one moving them.

If these things were not true, such a strict penalty would not have been established and ordained by **laws** <note>Agrippa refers to the ancient Roman "Laws of the Twelve Tables," which included capital punishment for those who used magic to "enchant" a neighbor's crops away.</note> against those who had enchanted the harvests. And **Tibullus** says of a certain enchantress:

> I saw her drawing the stars down from the sky;
> She turns the course of the swift river with her song;
> She splits the ground with her chant, and draws spirits from the tombs,
> And calls down bones from the warm pyre;
> When she pleases, she dispels the clouds from the gloomy sky;
> When she pleases, she calls forth snow in the summer heat.
> <note>original: "Hanc ego de cælo ducentem ſydera uidi, / Fluminis hæc rapidi carmine uertit iter, / Hæc cantu finditq; ſolum, maneſq; ſepulchris / Elicit, & tepido deuocat oſſa rogo, / Cum libet hæc triſti depellit nubila cælo, / Cum libet æſtiuo conuocat orbe niues"</note>

About all these things, that **sorceress** <note>original: "uenefica"; specifically refers to Medea in Ovid's *Metamorphoses*.</note> seems to boast much in Ovid when she says:

> When I have willed it, with the banks themselves wondering, the rivers
> Have returned to their sources; I stay the shaken waters,
> And shake the still; with a chant I drive away the cloudy seas,
> And bring on the clouds; I drive away and call the winds;
> I burst the viper's jaws with words and song;
> I move the living rocks and the oaks torn from their own earth,
> And the woods; I command the mountains to tremble,
> And the ground to bellow, and the spirits to go out from their tombs.
> You also, **Moon**, I draw down.
> <note>original: "Cum uolui, ripis ipſis mirantibus, amnes / In fontes rediere ſuos, concuſſaq; ſiſto, / Stantia concutio, cantu freta nubila pello, / Nubilaq; induco, uentos abigoq; uocoq;, / Vipereas rumpo uerbis & carmine fauces, / Viuaq; ſaxa ſua conuulſaq; robora terra, / Et ſyluas moueo, iubeoq; tremiſcere montes, / Et mugire ſolum, maneſq; exire ſepulchris: / Te quoq; luna traho."</note>

Furthermore, all the poets sing, and the philosophers do not deny, that many marvelous things can be accomplished through songs, such as driving away crops <note>This refers to the ancient belief, mentioned in the Roman Laws of the Twelve Tables, that magic could be used to "enchant" a neighbor's harvest so that it moved to one's own field.</note>, forcing or commanding lightning bolts, curing diseases, and things of such kind. For even **Cato** <note>Marcus Porcius Cato (Cato the Elder), a Roman statesman; his work *De Agri Cultura* contains various folk remedies and charms for livestock.</note> in his work on agriculture used certain songs against the diseases of beasts, which still exist in his writings to this day. **Josephus** <note>Flavius Josephus, the 1st-century Jewish historian, who wrote about Solomon’s legendary power over demons.</note> also testifies that **Solomon** was skilled in such incantations. **Celsus Africanus** also relates that, according to the doctrine of the **Egyptians**, the human body is cured—corresponding to the signs of the **zodiac** and the number of their **faces** <note>The "faces" or "decans" are 10-degree subdivisions of the zodiac signs. There are 36 in total.</note>—by just as many demons, namely thirty-six. Each of these demons takes up and protects a specific part of the body; the Egyptians call upon their names in their native tongue, and by invoking them in their incantations, they restore the damaged parts of the body to health.

### *On the power of writing, and on making imprecations and inscriptions*
### *Chapter 73.*

The duty of words and of speech is to announce the inner workings of the mind, to draw secrets out from the depths of thoughts, and to reveal the will of the speaker. **Writing** itself, however, is the ultimate expression of the mind. It is the measure, the collection, the state, the end, the containment, and the repetition of speech and voice, creating a lasting character <note>original: "habitũ" (habitum); here meaning a settled disposition or a permanent record.</note> that is not achieved by the fleeting act of a single spoken word. And whatever is in the mind, in the voice, in the word, in the prayer, or in the speech—all of this, and

And just as nothing conceived in the mind is not expressed by the voice, so nothing that is expressed is not also written; and for this reason, the Magi command that in every magical work, certain imprecations <note>original: "imprecationes"; in this context, these are solemn invocations or ritual prayers intended to direct spiritual power.</note> and inscriptions should be made, through which the operator may express his specific intent. For example, if he should gather a herb or a small stone, he should declare for what purpose he does so; if he fashions a magical image, he should say and write down to what effect it is made. Indeed, even **Albertus** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a Dominican friar and philosopher. We find also that this same kind of precept was in use among the ancients, as even

<meta>This page transitions from the general necessity of written charms to the specific divine origin of alphabets. It introduces the concept that language was not humanly invented but divinely ordained, specifically positioning the Hebrew alphabet as a celestial map reflected in the stars.</meta>

# BOOK I.

<page-num>201</page-num>

God gave to man mind and speech, which, as Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "Mercurius Trismegistus"; a legendary Hellenistic figure to whom a vast body of Egyptian-Greek wisdom texts, the Hermetica, were attributed.</note> says, are judged as rewards of the same virtue, power, and immortality. Indeed, almighty God by his providence divided the speech of men into different languages. These languages, according to their diversity, also received different and proper characters for writing, consisting of a certain order, number, and figure. These were not arranged by chance, nor by accident, nor by the fragile judgment of men, but were disposed and formed by divine power so that they might agree with the celestial and divine bodies and virtues. 

Before all the signs of languages, the writing of the Hebrews is the most sacred of all. In the figures of its characters and the tips of its accents <note>original: "apicibus accentuum"; this refers to the "tittles" or small strokes and crowns (tagin) found on Hebrew letters in Torah scrolls, which were believed to hold deep mystical secrets.</note>, it consists of matter, form, and spirit, as it were. It was first engraved in the seat of God—which is heaven—by the position of the stars. According to the figure of these stars, as the masters of the Hebrews <note>The author is referring here to the rabbis and mystical scholars of the Jewish tradition.</note> testify, the letters themselves were formed. Thus, they are most full of celestial mysteries, both through their figure and form and through what they signify, as well as through the numbers signified within them and the various harmony of their connections. 

For this reason, the more secret Hebrew <term>Mechubales</term> <note>From the Hebrew "Mekubbalim," meaning "those who have received [the tradition]"; the Kabbalists.</note> derive hidden meanings from the figure of those letters and the forms of the characters, and from their signatures: their simplicity, composition, separation, twisting, direction, lack, abundance, largeness, smallness, crowning, opening, closing, order, transmutation, binding, revolution of letters and points, and

<summary>This page argues that human language and writing are divine gifts, specifically identifying the Hebrew alphabet as the most sacred because its letterforms were modeled after the constellations in the heavens.</summary>

->## OF OCCULT PHILOSOPHY.<-

<page-num>202</page-num>

of the strokes and the calculation of numbers signified by the letters, they allow all things to be explained: how they proceeded from the first perfect cause and, in turn, how they may be led back to that same source. Furthermore, the Hebrews divide the letters of their alphabet into three parts: namely, twelve simple letters, seven double letters, and three "mothers" <note>This refers to the classification found in the *Sefer Yetzirah* (Book of Formation), a foundational text of Jewish mysticism, which categorizes the 22 Hebrew letters based on their phonetic and symbolic properties.</note>. These they say signify, as the characters of things, the twelve signs of the Zodiac, the seven planets, and the three elements: namely fire, water, and earth. For they do not consider air to be an element in itself, but rather the "glue" and spirit of the elements. To these letters they also coordinate points and strokes. Therefore, just as all things were and are produced from the aspects <note>In astrology, "aspects" are the angular relationships between celestial bodies that determine their influence.</note> of the planets and signs together with the elements—through the working of the spirit and truth—so from these letter-characters and points (which signify those produced things) are the names of all things formed. These names are like sacred signs <note>original: "sacramenta"; in this context, it refers to mysterious or holy symbols that bridge the physical and spiritual worlds.</note> and vehicles for the things they describe, carrying the essence and powers of those things wherever they go. The profound meanings and mysteries of these things reside in those characters—in their shapes, numbers, positions, order, and combinations <note>original: "revolutione"; referring to the mystical permutation of letters, such as the practice of Gematria or Temurah, where rearranging letters reveals hidden truths.</note>. For this reason, Origen <note>An early Christian theologian (c. 185–253 AD) who argued that names have natural power and that divine names lose their efficacy if translated from their original tongue.</note> believed that when these names are translated into another language, they retain almost none of their original power. Thus, the twenty-two letters are the foundation of the world and of all creatures that exist and are named within it. Every word and every created thing proceeds from them; from the permutations of these letters, things receive their name, their existence, and their power. An investigator must examine every combination of these letters until the voice of God is revealed and the clear meaning of the most sacred scriptures presents itself. But these matters belong to a higher level of contemplation,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mystical linguistics from the previous text. While the previous section explained how names act as "sacred signs" or vehicles for the essence of things, this page moves into the specific categorization of alphabets—Hebrew, Greek, and Latin—and how their individual letters correspond to the celestial bodies and the elements of the universe.</meta>

# BOOK I.

...which are to be treated in this book. But let us return to the division of the letters. Among the Hebrews, there are three "mothers" <note>In the *Sefer Yetzirah* (The Book of Formation), these are the primary letters from which the others originate.</note>, namely **Aleph, Vav, and Yod** <note>original: "א ו י". Note that standard Kabbalistic tradition usually cites Aleph, Mem, and Shin as the mothers; Agrippa here provides a variation or focuses on the letters often associated with the Divine Name.</note>. There are seven **Double Letters** <note>These are letters that have two different sounds (hard and soft) in Hebrew: Bet, Gimel, Dalet, Kaph, Pe, Resh, and Tav.</note>, namely **בגד כפרת**. The remaining twelve, namely **ה ו ח ט ל מ נ ס ע צ ק ש**, are **Simple Letters**. The same reasoning also applies among the Chaldeans <note>referring here to the Aramaic or Babylonian linguistic traditions often studied alongside Hebrew in occult philosophy</note>. In imitation of these, the letters of other languages are also distributed among the signs, planets, and elements in their own specific order.

For among the Greeks, the seven letters **Alpha, Epsilon, Eta, Iota, Omicron, Upsilon, and Omega** correspond to the seven planets. Twelve other letters—**Beta, Gamma, Delta, Zeta, Kappa, Lambda, Mu, Nu, Pi, Rho, Sigma, and Tau**—are attributed to the twelve signs of the Zodiac. The remaining five—**Theta, Xi, Phi, Chi, and Psi**—represent the four elements and the spirit of the world.

Truly among the Latins, these same things are signified, but in a different order: for the five vowels **A, E, I, O, and U**, along with the consonants **I** and **V**, are assigned to the seven planets. The consonants **B, C, D, F, G, L, M, N, P, R, S, and T** preside over the twelve signs. The others, namely **K, Q, X, and Z**, make up the four elements. **H**, the aspiration, represents the spirit of the world. **Y**, because it is a Greek and not a Latin character and is used only in Greek words, follows the nature of its own language.

Truly, it must not be ignored that Hebrew letters, as discovered by the Sages, are the most effective of all, because they have the greatest similarity with the heavens and the world. The letters of other languages, however, do not have such great efficacy, because they are more distantly removed from those celestial origins. Indeed, the following table will explain their arrangement.

<meta>This page transitions from the technical tables of the "alphabet of nature" to a concluding address to the reader. It explains the publication status of the work and provides context for why the intended trilogy remains incomplete at this stage.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

<meta>Significant ink bleed-through from the reverse side reveals a grid with characters (possibly a linguistic or cryptographic table). A small ink stain is present at the bottom left.</meta>

Kind Reader, the author of this most divine work was intending to publish both a second and a third book, as he also promised readers at the beginning of the work, but the almost sudden and unexpected death of the divine Margaret <note>original: "divæ Margaritæ obitus"; this likely refers to Margaret of Austria (1480–1530), the Governor of the Habsburg Netherlands and a significant patron of the author, Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa.</note>, as well as other concerns, diverted him elsewhere and forced him to cease his undertaking. But there is no doubt that when he realizes this little book is not being scorned and is not entirely unwelcome to the learned, he will publish the other two. Now take this, and with a good heart embrace the most hidden mysteries and the secrets <term>arcana; refers to specialized or "hidden" knowledge accessible only to the initiated</term> of most divine matters contained within it:
->Farewell.<-

->THE END.<-

<insert>
[Octagonal library stamp:]
BAVARIAN
STATE
LIBRARY
MUNICH

[Rectangular library stamp:]
Bavarian
State Library
Munich
</insert>

<summary>A concluding note to the reader explaining that the author's planned second and third volumes were delayed by the death of his patron, Margaret of Austria, but may still be published if this first volume is well-received.</summary>
<keywords>Margaret of Austria, publication history, occult mysteries, divine secrets, Bavarian State Library</keywords>

# PRINTER’S MARK OF SÉBASTIEN NIVELLE

<meta>This page features the professional emblem of Sébastien Nivelle, a prominent Parisian printer active in the 16th century. Small ink stains are visible at the bottom of the page, likely from the printing process or later handling.</meta>

[Image Description: A woodcut printer’s device featuring a central elder tree (<term>sabucum; the Latin name for the elder tree, Sambucus</term>) with two birds, identified as robins (<term>erythacos; from the Greek word for robin or redstart</term>), perched upon its branches. A long, winding banner entwines the trunk and branches, inscribed with a Latin motto. The tree grows from a small mound of earth, surrounded by four smaller shrubs at its base.]

-> THE ELDER TREE DOES NOT NOURISH ONLY TWO ROBINS <-
<note>original Latin: "Sabucum non unicu[m] alit duos erythacos." This motto is a variation of an ancient Greek proverb which suggested that "one bush does not feed two robins," implying that two of the same kind cannot live together due to competition. By changing it to say the tree does *not* nourish *only* two, the printer Sébastien Nivelle suggests that his establishment is bountiful and can support many scholars and works.</note>

<summary>This page displays the symbolic printer's mark of the Parisian bookseller Sébastien Nivelle, featuring an elder tree and robins with a motto signifying abundance and professional generosity.</summary>
<keywords>Sébastien Nivelle, printer's mark, elder tree, robins, Latin motto, 16th-century printing, Paris</keywords>
